2.1C4 · wk 2Implicature and inference: sentence meaning vs speaker meaning; the cooperative principle and the four maxims (quantity, quality, relation, manner), kept practical; flouting and damning with faint praise; deniability; presupposition triggers (stop, again, still, even, only, the, regret, realize, manage to) and loaded questions; indirectness (hinting, understatement, strategic vagueness); slant in text (diction, omission, juxtaposition, distancing markers)decode what a workplace email, a reference, a review and a set of meeting minutes really say, name the mechanism behind each implied meaning, refuse the premise of a loaded question, and write a short message that conveys a difficult verdict entirely by implication

Lesson Plans

2.1 · C4

Implicature, Inference & Reading Between the Lines

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • distinguish sentence meaning (what the words say) from speaker meaning (what this speaker, here, intends), and infer the implicature of a reply that is too thin, too long, off-topic or obviously untrue
  • name the maxim that a speaker visibly flouts (quantity, quality, relation, manner) and explain why the implied meaning is deniable
  • spot presupposition triggers (still, again, stop, even, only, the, realize, regret, manage to), state what a sentence takes for granted, use the negation test, and refuse the premise of a loaded question before answering it
  • read a short text for its slant (diction, omission, juxtaposition, distancing markers such as reportedly, insisted, so-called) and state the verdict it never states
  • encode a face-threatening message by implication (hinting, understatement, strategic vagueness), so that a listener infers it at the intended strength
Materials
  • Workbook 2.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Implicature Lab frame, self-check)
  • Slides 3–8 · Genre picture cards (email, minutes, review, reference) · Maxim and trigger word cards · Implicature Lab source packs (4 texts) · lab worksheets · encode cards (6) · C4 A/B cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.1-A (the reference call) and 2.1-B (dictation)

Left: said vs meant. "It's cold in here." = Please close the window. Sentence meaning ≠ speaker meaning. Maxims: quantity (enough) · quality (true) · relation (relevant) · manner (clear). Flout on purpose → implicature. "Is she good?" "She's very punctual." → faint praise · deniable: "I only said she was punctual!"

Center: taken for granted. Triggers: still · again · stop · even · only · the · realize / regret · manage to. Negation test: "Why is your team still behind?" / "Your team isn't still behind." → both assume you were behind. Refuse first: "That assumes we're behind. In fact…"

Right: slant in text. diction (insisted vs said) · omission · juxtaposition · distancing (reportedly, allegedly, so-called). Two questions: What does it state? What does it want me to conclude? Toolkit: hint · understate · stay vague · frame as a question.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: finish the chunk7S–SReview C3 (1.3, fixed expressions and collocation). Board six half-chunks: by and ___ · part and ___ of the job · high and ___ · a heartfelt ___ · a snap ___ · as fit as a ___. Pairs complete them in 90 seconds, then choose two and build one natural sentence about their week, real or from a new identity card. Two pairs read theirs. Note one fossilized error (large and by, a fast decision) and one example of effortless range.
Lead-in: it's cold in here5T–SSlide 3. Act it: stand by the window, rub your arms, say "It's cold in here." Ask: "What did I say? (The temperature.) What did I mean? (Close the window.) Did I say 'close'? (No.) How did you know?" Show the four genre cards: "Today, the same trick in an email, a reference, a review and meeting minutes. What they say, what they mean, and how."
Meaning: implicature and the maxims12T–S → S–SDon't lecture Grice: say the four expectations in one line each, then read the four replies aloud before you show slide 4; pairs have 3 minutes to write the real message behind each and guess which expectation it breaks. Check, then focus on faint praise and deniability: "Could the diner say 'I only meant the plates were warm'? (Yes.) Do we still know what they meant? (Yes.)" CCQs (below).
Meaning: presupposition and loaded questions8T–SSlide 5. Trigger table; learners call out what each example takes for granted. Demonstrate the negation test. Then the loaded question: "Why is your department still so disorganized?" Ask for a yes/no answer; show how any answer signs for the premise. Model the refusal formula on the board: That assumes X, which isn't the case. What I can tell you is…
Drill6T–S → SThree rounds (see Drill script): "What does it take for granted?" (fast, individual), "Refuse the premise" (choral formula, then individual) and the intonation of implying phrases (It's certainly… bold.), choral then individual. Anyone may mouth rather than speak in the choral round.
Listening: the reference call7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.1-A twice (two voices, or with a confident learner). First: "Will Dana hire Leo as a team lead?" (Probably not.) Second: learners complete the six phrases, then pairs write what each one implies and name the maxim. Point out Dana's own loaded question (Did he manage to meet his deadlines in the end?).
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–3 alone (state the implicature; identify the presupposition and refuse two loaded questions), then compare in pairs. Board-check only disagreements. Do Ex 4 (the slanted news item) together in 2 minutes as the bridge into the lab; Ex 5 is homework.
Freer: Implicature Lab25S–S → T–SSlides 6–8. Round 1, Decode (10 min): groups of 3 get one source text (a workplace email, a reference letter, a hotel review, meeting minutes). On the lab worksheet they fill three columns for every loaded phrase: Said · Meant · Mechanism (maxim flouted, trigger, lever), then write the one-line real message. Round 2, Encode (8 min): each group draws an encode card (a difficult message and a reader) and writes a 4–6 line email or exchange that conveys it entirely by implication: no bald statement anywhere. Round 3, Peer test (7 min): groups swap encoded texts and run the "What's the real message?" test (below). Two groups share. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in four columns. Accuracy: "Only she got a B" (wrong position for the meaning intended) · "It assumes that we are behind of schedule". Precision: said where insisted was the lever. Appropriacy: an implication so buried that the peer group inferred the opposite. Effect: the best deniable line of the day, quoted. Learners fix and discuss.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 2.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button and play one model sentence ("Well, she certainly has a lot of energy — I'll give her that much."). Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 8 + 6 + 7 + 10 + 25 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: implicature. We assume a speaker is cooperative. When a reply seems to fall short (too little, too much, off-topic, obviously untrue, oddly worded) we don't decide the speaker has malfunctioned: we infer an extra meaning. That inferred meaning is the implicature. It is deniable: the speaker can retreat to the literal words.

"Is she good at her job?" "She's very punctual." Did the speaker say anything negative? (No.) What do you understand? (She isn't good at the job.) Why? (That's the only good thing mentioned: if there were more, they'd say it.) Could the speaker deny it? (Yes: "I only said she was punctual.") Which maxim was flouted? (Quantity: too little information.)
"What did you think of my presentation?" "Shall we get coffee?" Did the speaker answer? (No.) Is that an accident? (No: it's deliberate.) What does it mean? (I'd rather not say, so probably not good.) Which maxim? (Relation: the reply is off-topic on purpose.)

Meaning: presupposition. Not implied by the speaker but assumed by the sentence, carried by a trigger word. It survives negation, which is why it is hard to challenge.

"Have you stopped cutting corners?" If I say yes, was I cutting corners before? (Yes.) If I say no? (Yes, and I still am.) So what's the problem with answering? (Any answer accepts the premise.) What should I do first? (Reject the premise: "That assumes I was cutting corners.") · "Even Tom passed." What do we learn about Tom? (He was the least likely to pass.) Is that stated? (No, even assumes it.)

Meaning: slant in text. Writers imply a verdict without a false sentence: insisted (against the evidence), reportedly (I'm not vouching for this, but I'm telling you), a fact placed next to another fact (juxtaposition), a question not asked (omission).

"The CEO insisted the launch was a success." Is insisted neutral? (No.) What does it suggest? (Others disagree; the evidence says otherwise.) What's the neutral word? (said.) · "The app reportedly crashed twice." Does the writer say it crashed? (Not directly.) Why say it at all? (To tell us, while keeping a distance.)

Form.

Flouting a maximquantity · quality · relation · manner"The plates were warm." (quantity) · "Lovely day!" in a storm (quality) · "Shall we get coffee?" (relation) · "They produced sounds corresponding to the score." (manner)
Presupposition triggerschange-of-state verbs · again, still · even, only · definite the / possessive · factive verbs · manage tostop / start / keep · Are you late again? · Even Tom… · She only got a B. · the mistake in your figures · realize / regret / admit · Did you manage to finish?
Refusing the premisename the assumption → reject it → reframeThat question assumes X, which isn't the case. · I don't accept the premise. What I can say is… · Before I answer, "still" isn't quite right.
Indirect moveshint · understate · stay vague · frame as a question or observationI couldn't help noticing… · It's certainly… bold. · That's one way of doing it. · Let's circle back on that. · Have you had a chance to…?
Distancing markersadverbs, reporting verbs, scare quotesreportedly · allegedly · apparently · so-called · insisted / claimed / maintained · the "success"

Pronunciation. Implied meaning is carried by stress and pause as much as by words. Faint praise has a tell-tale fall-rise and a pause before the praise: "Well… she's very PUNCtual." The implying adjective comes after a hesitation: "It's certainly… BOLD." Presupposition triggers can be stressed to make the attack obvious ("Are you late aGAIN?") or left unstressed to slip them past the listener: point out that the unstressed version is the more dangerous one. In the refusal formula, stress the word you are rejecting: "That assumes we're STILL behind."

Drill script
  1. What does it take for granted? (fast, individual): T: "Did you manage to find the file?" → S: "Finding it was difficult." T: "Are you late again?" → "You've been late before." T: "Even the intern understood." → "The intern was the least likely to understand." T: "She only got a B." → "A B was less than expected." T: "When did you realize the budget was wrong?" → "The budget was wrong."
  2. Refuse the premise (choral formula, then individual): T: "Why is your team still behind schedule?" → S: "That assumes we're still behind. In fact, we're on track for the new date." T: "When will you stop missing deadlines?" → "I don't accept that I miss deadlines. What I can tell you is…" T: "Why did your project fail?" → "I wouldn't say it failed. What happened was…"
  3. Say it with the pause (choral, then individual): "Well… she's very punctual." · "It's certainly… bold." · "That's one way of doing it." · "I'm sure you had your reasons." Then ask one learner to say "It's certainly bold" sincerely, and another with the pause: the class says which one is a compliment.
Implicature Lab — how to run it
  • Groups of 3 with three roles: reader (reads the text aloud with the implied tone), scribe (fills the worksheet) and decoder (in Round 1 may speak only after the group has written its reading, then challenges it: "Is that really implied, or are we over-reading?"). Give the decoder role to a dominant speaker. Rotate roles between rounds.
  • Round 1, Decode (10 min): each group gets one source text: C1 a director's email rejecting a proposal, C2 a reference letter that says nothing about the work, C3 a polite hotel review that is a demolition, C4 meeting minutes that record a row without saying so. For every loaded phrase: Said · Meant · Mechanism. Then one line: "The real message is…". The teacher's model reading is printed small at the foot of each card: fold it under before handing out.
  • Round 2, Encode (8 min): each group draws an encode card (for example "Tell a colleague that their slides have far too much text" or "Tell a neighbor that their music is too loud after 11 p.m."). They write a 4–6 line email or exchange that conveys the message entirely by implication: at least one flouted maxim, one presupposition trigger and one indirect phrase from the bank, and no sentence that states the message. They keep the card hidden.
  • Round 3, Peer test (7 min): swap texts with the next group. Run the "What's the real message?" test: the reviewers write, in one line, the message they inferred and how strong it is (mild / clear / severe). Then the writers reveal their card. Pass = the message matches and every line could be defended literally. Too buried = the reviewers inferred nothing or the opposite. Too bald = one line says it outright. Reviewers give one strength and one suggestion, quoting the text.
  • Share: two groups read their encoded texts aloud (the reader role, or you read them). The class guesses the message before the card is revealed.
  • Keep the ethics visible: tact communicates a true message gently; manipulation uses inference to mislead. If a group's text implies something false (for example a reference that invents a problem), point it out as the line not to cross.
ICQs before the Implicature Lab
In Round 1, how many columns? (Three: said, meant, mechanism.) When can the decoder speak? (After the group has written its reading.) · In Round 2, can you say the message directly? (No, never.) What must you include? (A flouted maxim, a trigger, an indirect phrase.) Do you show your card? (No, not until the test.) · In Round 3, what do the reviewers write? (The real message, in one line, and how strong it is.) What does "too buried" mean? (Nobody could infer it.) Whose opinion is on the card, yours or the card's? (The card's.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C2 speakers are much stronger orally than in writing, and they are often superb at hearing implied meaning. Give them the reference letter (C2), the shortest text, with the loaded phrases already underlined, and a strip with the four maxim names and a one-word gloss (enough · true · relevant · clear). Let them be the reader in Round 1 and the voice in the share. In Round 2 they may dictate the encoded message while the scribe writes. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 3 items 1–4. Target: decode three phrases orally and produce one deniable line.

Stretch

Calibration. Ask strong learners to encode the same message at three strengths (a mild doubt, a clear objection, a severe verdict) using only implication, and let the group rank them blind. Then give them the online lesson's ironic manager dialogue and ask: which line would be hardest to deny in a formal complaint, and why? For reading: rewrite the slanted news item (Ex 4) twice, once neutral and once slanted the opposite way, without adding any new facts.

L1 watch-outs
  • Literal reading as a sign of respect: learners from low-context communication cultures (for example, German, Dutch, Scandinavian, Israeli and many Eastern European workplaces) may read faint praise as praise ("The reference says he is punctual, so it is positive"). Present implicature as information that native readers will act on, whether or not the learner likes the convention.
  • Over-reading: learners from high-context cultures (for example, Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai and Indonesian workplaces) are often expert decoders but may find implied criticism in every neutral sentence. The decoder's question "Is that really implied?" is for them.
  • only and even position: in English their position changes the presupposition (She only got a B · Only she got a B · Even Tom passed · Tom even passed). Speakers of Russian, Polish, Chinese, Spanish and Arabic often place only by L1 habit. Drill two or three contrasts.
  • Definite article as a trigger: learners whose languages have no articles (Russian, Polish, Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese, Hindi-Urdu) may not hear that the mistake in your figures presupposes a mistake while a mistake doesn't. Make the contrast explicit; it is also a common source of accidental accusations in their emails.
  • False friends in slant words: Spanish pretender (= to try to), French prétendre (= to claim), Italian pretendere (= to demand), and Portuguese pretender (= to intend) lead to "He pretended the scheme was a success." In English, pretend means to fake. Also eventually (≠ Spanish eventualmente, French éventuellement = possibly), which changes a sentence's implication.
  • "I have a doubt" (Spanish tengo una duda, Portuguese tenho uma dúvida, Italian ho un dubbio, Hindi-Urdu English): in English "I have a doubt about your figures" implies suspicion, not a question. Offer I have a question about…
Homework

Online lesson 2.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_04.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and copy the pauses and stress, not just the words; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Say It Without Saying It" project (analysis part required; the performed exchange may be written as a script instead of recorded). Workbook 2.1: Exercise 5 (rewrite to imply), "My new words" and the journal (a conversation in which someone meant more, or less, than they said: real, invented, or from a new identity card).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C The concert was disappointing, implied by praising only a trivial detail · Q2 B "When will you admit the data was falsified?" · Q3 (new) B "That question assumes we're behind schedule…". Listening (Ex 1): 1 punctual · 2 enthusiasm · 3 colorful · 4 social events · 5 restructured · 6 eventually.

2.2 · C5

Irony, Humor & Wordplay

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • tell verbal irony (saying the opposite, often gently) from sarcasm (irony with a target and an edge), and detect both from the three signals: stress, tone and context clash
  • use understatement and litotes (a bit of a problem, not bad at all, not exactly ideal) for dry humor and tact, and hyperbole sparingly for comic effect
  • recognize situational and dramatic irony in stories, news and film
  • explain exactly how a pun (two meanings, or two words that sound alike), a malapropism and a spoonerism work, and decode and write pun headlines
  • judge the register of humor (who is the target, who is listening, is this the moment?) and write a short comic piece that targets a situation or themselves
Materials
  • Workbook 2.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Wit Workshop frame, self-check)
  • Slides 9–13 · Irony-situation picture cards (umbrella, flight, coffee, mobile phone) · Device cards · Headline Desk story cards (8) · Mock review / wry anecdote prompt cards · Gap and Target checklists · C5 A/B cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.2-A (sincere, ironic or understated?) and 2.2-B (dictation)

Left: the gap. Irony = the opposite ("Lovely weather for it." in a storm) · sarcasm = irony + target + edge ("Oh, nice parking.") · signals: STRESS · tone (flat / drawled) · context clash. Understatement: a bit of a problem · not bad at all · I've had better days. Hyperbole: a mountain of paperwork. Keep it rare.

Center: play with the words. Pun: I used to be a banker, but I lost interest. (2 meanings) · a tie (homophone/polysemy) · malapropism: an escape goat → a scapegoat · spoonerism: a well-boiled icicle ← a well-oiled bicycle. Headlines: BAKERY RISES TO THE OCCASION.

Right: the rules of the room. Setup → incongruity → punchline (last!). Who pays? a situation · an object · a system · me. Never: nationality, religion, accent, disability, body. In writing: over-signal or don't risk it. Nobody has to perform.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: what's really meant?7S–SReview C4. Board three lines: "Thanks for the proposal. It's certainly comprehensive." · "Why are the figures wrong again?" · "The venue was, reportedly, full." Pairs write the real message and the mechanism for each, then refuse the premise of the second. Or use the C4 A/B cards ("Say it without saying it") if you printed them. Check two answers; collect or glance at the journals.
Lead-in and ground rules5T–SSlide 9 (umbrella, flight, coffee, phone). Point at the flight card and say, deadpan: "Flight canceled. Well, this is going brilliantly." Ask: "Do I mean it? (No.) How do you know? (The situation, my voice.)" Then say the ground rules plainly (slide 13 has them too): "Today we analyze humor and write a little. Nobody has to tell a joke or perform anything. Our targets are situations, objects, systems and ourselves; never people's countries, religions, accents or bodies."
Meaning: irony and sarcasm8T–SSlide 10. The three signals, the ironic set phrases (Oh, fantastic. · Just what I needed. · Well, that went well.) and the difference between irony and sarcasm: target and edge. Why irony misfires: in writing and across cultures. CCQs (below).
Listening: sincere, ironic or understated?6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.2-A: eight lines, each with its context. Learners circle S / I / U and note the signal. Pairs compare; check the two pairs of identical words (items 1–2 and 3–4) that change meaning with the context. Optional A/B follow-up: the C5 cards "What really happened?"
Meaning: understatement and hyperbole7T–S → S–SSlide 10 (lower half). Build an understatement ladder on the board for a disaster (the projector caught fire): a bit of a problem → not our finest hour → I've had better days. Contrast with hyperbole (a mountain of paperwork · I could sleep for a week) and its inflation. Pairs make a ladder for "the client was furious."
Meaning: wordplay and headlines8T–S → S–SSlide 11. Dissect one pun (lost interest): which word, which two meanings? Then malapropism and spoonerism, quickly. Show three pun headlines; pairs name the pivot word and both meanings in 60 seconds each. Setup → incongruity → punchline with the "broken arm in two places" joke from the online lesson (slide 12).
Drill5T–S → SIntonation, not jokes (see Drill script): the same words sincere vs ironic; the understatement ladder with a straight face; pivot words said with both stresses. Choral first; individuals only if they volunteer.
Controlled practice9S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (what's really meant?) and Ex 4 (name the device and fix it) alone, then check in pairs. Ex 3 (the headlines) is done in the workshop; Ex 5 (blunt → dry) is homework.
Freer: Wit Workshop25S–S → T–SSlide 13. Round 1, Headline Desk (7 min): pairs get two story cards (a bakery wins a prize, a new pool opens…) and write a pun headline for each, plus a one-line explanation of the pivot word. Round 2, Comic piece (10 min): pairs choose a prompt card and write 80–120 words: a mock review of a mundane object (the office stapler, a self-checkout machine) or a wry anecdote about a small invented or real disaster, with irony, understatement or hyperbole, and one piece of wordplay. Round 3, Gap and Target review (8 min): pairs swap and run the checklist (below); then up to three pieces are shared, read by the writers, a volunteer or you, or displayed. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in four columns. Accuracy: "It was a bit of problem" · "I nearly died of laugh". Precision: ironic used for coincidental. Appropriacy: sarcasm aimed at a named colleague in a mock review. Effect: the best pun headline of the day, and why the pivot word works.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 2.2 on screen; play one 🔊 model sentence ("The weather's a touch damp, said the man wringing out his coat.") and ask where the understatement is. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total907 + 5 + 8 + 6 + 7 + 8 + 5 + 9 + 25 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: irony vs sarcasm. Both say the opposite of what is meant. Irony can be gentle, affectionate or aimed at yourself; sarcasm has a target and an edge and is meant to sting.

It's pouring on the company picnic. "Perfect weather for a picnic." Is the weather perfect? (No.) Is the speaker lying? (No: everyone knows the truth.) Is anyone being attacked? (No.) So: irony or sarcasm? (Irony.) · A colleague parks across two spaces. "Oh, nice parking." Is there a target? (Yes, the driver.) An edge? (Yes.) So? (Sarcasm.) Would you say it to your manager? (Probably not.)
How do we know it's ironic? (The situation clashes with the words; heavy stress; a flat or drawled voice.) Which signal disappears in an email? (Tone and stress.) So what's the risk in writing? (The reader takes it literally.)

Meaning: understatement and hyperbole. Understatement says far less than the situation deserves; the listener supplies the missing emotion. Hyperbole says far more. Both are signals, not lies.

"The client had a few reservations." Were they mildly unsure? (No, they were furious.) Why say it that way? (It's dry and calm, and it's funny because of the gap.) Is it also polite? (Yes.) · "I've told you a million times." A million? (No.) So what does it mean? (Many times, and I'm annoyed.) What happens if everything is "the worst ever"? (It stops meaning anything.)

Meaning: puns and headlines. A pun makes one word or phrase carry two meanings at once (interest: money / enthusiasm), or uses two words that sound alike. Headlines use puns because they are short and memorable.

"BAKERY RISES TO THE OCCASION." What does bread do in an oven? (It rises.) What does "rise to the occasion" mean? (Perform well when it matters.) How many meanings are active? (Two.) Would this headline work in your language? (Almost certainly not: puns rarely translate.)

Form.

Ironic set phrasespraise that means the oppositeOh, fantastic. · Just what I needed. · Well, that went well. · Couldn't be better. · Nice one.
Understatement / litotesa bit of / slightly / not exactly / not the most… · not bad / no small…a bit of a problem · slightly overdue · not exactly ideal · not our finest hour · no small feat · not bad at all
Hyperboleimpossible numbers, sizes, times; comic imagesa million times · a mountain of paperwork · waited an eternity · a coffee cup you could bathe in
Punone word, two meanings (polysemy) · two words, one sound (homophones)lost interest · impossible to put down · atoms make up everything · ended in a tie
Malapropism · spoonerismwrong word that sounds like the right one · swapped first soundsan escape goat (scapegoat) · for all intensive purposes (intents and) · a well-boiled icicle (a well-oiled bicycle)
Joke structuresetup → incongruity → punchline"I broke my arm in two places." → "Stop going to those places." (the punchline comes last)

Pronunciation. Irony lives in stress, pitch range and pace. Sincere praise uses a lively, rising-falling pitch; ironic praise is either exaggerated ("Oh, FANtastic" with a slow, wide fall) or completely flat. Deadpan understatement is spoken with a narrow pitch range, level volume and no smile: that is the joke. Stress can create the irony on its own: "Well, THIS is going brilliantly." In puns, the pivot word is usually said neutrally: stressing it too much "explains" the joke. Learners from tone languages or syllable-timed languages often use a narrower pitch range overall, so their sincere praise may sound ironic, and their irony may not register at all. Practice the contrast in the drill.

Drill script
  1. Same words, two meanings (choral, then volunteers): T gives the context, class says the line. "Your colleague fixed the printer." → "You're a genius." (warm, lively) · "The printer jams again." → "Oh, great. Genius strikes again." (flat or exaggerated). "Sunny, 75 degrees." → "Perfect weather for a picnic." · "Pouring rain." → "Perfect weather for a picnic."
  2. The understatement ladder (choral, straight face): T: "Your car broke down on the highway." → S: "It's been a bit of a day." → "I've had better days." → "Not exactly the trip we planned." T: "The cake collapsed in the oven." → "It's… a little flatter than intended."
  3. Find the pivot (individual, fast): T: "I'm reading a book about anti-gravity. It's impossible to put down." → S: "put down: stop reading / let it fall." T: "Don't trust atoms. They make up everything." → "make up: invent / form." T: "SWIMMING POOL PLAN MAKES A SPLASH." → "splash: water / a big impression."
Wit Workshop — how to run it
  • Pairs (or groups of 3 with an editor, who cuts words from the draft but writes none: give this role to a dominant speaker). The workshop is about writing and analyzing humor. Nobody is required to read aloud, act or "be funny" in front of the class.
  • Round 1, Headline Desk (7 min): each pair gets two story cards (all invented, harmless local news: a bakery wins a national prize, a town pool reopens, a library forgives all late fees, a dentist opens a free children's clinic…). Write a pun headline for each (max. 7 words, headline style: present tense, no articles) and one line naming the pivot word and its two meanings. Model headlines are on the back of each card for when a pair is stuck.
  • Round 2, Comic piece (10 min): choose one prompt card: a mock review (a mundane object reviewed as if it were a luxury product, or damned as if it were a disaster: the office stapler, the break-room microwave, a self-checkout machine, an umbrella that turns inside out) or a wry anecdote (a small disaster told dry: locked out of your own apartment, a flat-pack bookshelf with one extra screw, a video call with the camera on). 80–120 words. Include irony or sarcasm aimed at the object or situation, understatement or hyperbole, and one piece of wordplay. The punchline comes last.
  • Round 3, Gap and Target review (8 min): swap with another pair and fill the checklist: 1. The gap: underline the words where what is said and what is meant come apart. 2. The signals: in writing, is the irony clear without a tone of voice? Mark any line a reader could take literally. 3. The target: who pays for the joke? It must be the writer, an object, a situation or a system. 4. The cut: one word or phrase that delays the punchline. Then one strength and one suggestion, quoting.
  • Share (optional): up to three pieces. The writers decide how: read it themselves, ask a volunteer or you to read it, or show it on the document camera. "Pass" is fine. Ask the class to point at the pivot word, not to rate how funny it is.
ICQs before the Wit Workshop
In Round 1, how many words in a headline, at most? (Seven.) What else do you write? (The pivot word and its two meanings.) · In Round 2, how long is the piece? (80–120 words.) Three things to include? (Irony or sarcasm, understatement or hyperbole, and wordplay.) Where does the punchline go? (At the end.) Can the joke be about a person's country or accent? (No: an object, a situation, a system or yourself.) · In Round 3, what do you underline? (The gap.) Do you have to read your piece aloud? (No. You can ask someone else or just show it.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Headlines are short, which makes Round 1 accessible. Give a stronger oral learner the bakery and pool cards and a strip of pivot words to choose from (rises · on a roll · makes a splash · dives in · in the deep end). In Round 2 they may choose the wry anecdote and tell it to their partner, who writes it down; the teller then edits. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 4 items 1–3. Target: one explained headline and three lines of a dry anecdote.

Stretch

Irony that survives in print. Ask strong learners to write a mock review in which the irony is clear with no exaggeration words (so, very, amazing, literally) and no exclamation marks: only the gap between calm words and absurd facts. Then give them a sincere product review and ask them to change three words to make it ironic, and to explain which change did the most work. For wordplay: write a headline with a pun that works on two levels of the story (the literal event and its consequence).

L1 watch-outs
  • Irony across cultures: in some communication cultures heavy irony toward other people sounds cold or dishonest, and sarcasm toward a superior is unthinkable; learners from these backgrounds may detect irony perfectly but reasonably prefer not to produce it. That's a legitimate choice: the production target is understatement and situational irony, which travel well.
  • Pitch range: speakers of Mandarin, Cantonese, Vietnamese and Thai use pitch for word meaning and often narrow their pitch range in English, so ironic "Oh, FANtastic" may sound sincere and sincere praise may sound flat. Speakers of syllable-timed languages (Spanish, French, Italian, Turkish) may not produce the stressed THIS that flags irony. Practice the contrast.
  • Homophone puns: learners who don't separate English /ɪ/ and /iː/ (Spanish, Portuguese, Arabic, Russian, many others) or /l/ and /r/ (Japanese, Korean) may miss or mishear some homophone puns. That's not a comprehension failure: explain the sound, and choose polysemy puns (interest, put down) for the drill.
  • ironic for coincidental: many speakers of all backgrounds (native speakers too) use ironic for any coincidence. Situational irony needs an outcome contrary to expectation (the fire station burns down), not just a surprise.
  • Literal translation of L1 humor: "It's not my circus, not my monkeys" (Polish) travels; most L1 puns don't. Learners may enjoy sharing an L1 saying, but never require it, and never ask anyone to explain "how your culture jokes."
  • False friend: French humour ≈ English humor, but Spanish humor also means mood (estar de buen humor): "I'm in bad humor" → I'm in a bad mood.
Homework

Online lesson 2.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_05.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and copy the timing; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz, and the "Craft & Deliver a Comic Piece" project in writing (delivering it to a listener is optional). Workbook 2.2: Exercise 5 (blunt → dry), "My new words" and the journal (a moment when humor in English landed or missed; yours, someone else's, or invented).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Irony: she means it's going terribly · Q2 C interest carries two meanings at once · Q3 (new) B a gently self-deprecating line about your own first-day sense of direction. Listening (Ex 1): 1 S · 2 I · 3 I · 4 S · 5 I (sarcasm) · 6 U · 7 I · 8 S.

2.3 · C6

Figurative Language — Metaphor & Imagery

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • distinguish metaphor, simile, metonymy, synecdoche and personification, and say in a phrase what each achieves in context
  • group everyday expressions under their conceptual metaphor (ARGUMENT IS WAR, TIME IS MONEY, IDEAS ARE FOOD, LIFE IS A JOURNEY, UP IS GOOD, UNDERSTANDING IS SEEING) and coin a new expression that obeys the same map
  • tell dead, clichéd and live metaphors apart, and refresh or re-coin a cliché
  • analyze the imagery of a short descriptive passage: its controlling metaphor and source domain, its supporting images, and any image that breaks the frame (a mixed metaphor)
  • write and revise a 120–150-word descriptive passage built on one sustained metaphor, and critique a peer's passage precisely
Materials
  • Workbook 2.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Studio frame, self-check)
  • Slides 14–18 · Source-domain picture cards (bank, bread, station, lamp) · Figure cards · Metaphor family sort cards (24) · Imagery passages (3) · Subject and source-domain cards · Name the frame slips · C6 A/B cards · Between the Lines board game · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.3-A (the library at closing time) and 2.3-B (dictation)

Left: the figures. metaphor (X is Y: her argument was a fortress) · simile (like / as: fear rose like cold water) · metonymy (association: the front desk said…, Hollywood) · synecdoche (part / whole: all hands on deck, wheels) · personification (the house groaned).

Center: the hidden maps. ARGUMENT IS WAR: defend, attack, shoot down, win · TIME IS MONEY: spend, save, waste, budget · IDEAS ARE FOOD: half-baked, digest, food for thought · LIFE IS A JOURNEY: crossroads, dead end · UP IS GOOD: spirits rose · UNDERSTANDING IS SEEING: I see, murky. New on the map: her rebuttal outflanked him.

Right: keep it alive, keep it in one frame. dead (the leg of the table) · cliché (think outside the box) → refresh / re-coin · live (the fog comes on little cat feet). Mixed: We'll burn that bridge when we come to it. Test: can you see it? One source domain → sustained metaphor.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: headline desk review7S–SReview C5. Board three headlines from last class (or: DENTIST GETS TO THE ROOT OF THE PROBLEM · PRINTER LAUNCH FAILS TO MAKE AN IMPRESSION). Pairs name the pivot word and its two meanings, then turn "The meeting was terrible" into dry understatement. Check quickly; praise one pun from last class's work (with the writer's permission).
Lead-in: the hidden maps5T–SSlide 14 (bank, bread, station, lamp). Ask: "What do these have to do with time, ideas, life and understanding?" Elicit one expression each (spend time · food for thought · at a crossroads · I see). "We don't just talk this way. We think this way. Today: the maps under English, and how to build your own images."
Meaning: the core figures8T–S → S–SSlide 15. Quick definitions with one example each; spend the time on the two confusing pairs: metaphor vs simile (live inside it vs notice the likeness) and metonymy vs synecdoche (association vs part-whole). Pairs name the figure in four slide examples. CCQs (below).
Meaning: conceptual metaphor families10S–S → T–SPairs get the 24 family sort cards and 3 minutes to sort them into six families. Check against the family table on slide 15 (UNDERSTANDING IS SEEING is on slide 14). Then "extend the map": each pair coins one new expression for ARGUMENT IS WAR and one for TIME IS MONEY that a native reader would accept (her rebuttal outflanked him · that meeting was a total write-off). Two pairs share.
Meaning: live, dead, clichéd, mixed7T–SSlide 16. Three piles on the board: dead · cliché · live. Learners call out where each example goes. Then the gallery of mixed metaphors: learners describe the impossible picture ("You can't burn a bridge and cross it"). The master moves: refresh (the ball has been in your court so long it's gathering dust) or re-coin.
Drill5T–S → SSee Drill script: "Name the family" ping-pong, "Keep the frame" (continue a metaphor in the same domain) and "See the picture" (fix the mix). Fast, individual after a choral start.
Listening: the library at closing time7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.3-A slowly, twice. First: "What is the library compared to?" (A large animal falling asleep.) Second: learners complete six images. Pairs name the controlling metaphor, one simile and one personification that isn't part of the main frame but fits it.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (name the figure and its effect) and Ex 4 (fix the mix / refresh the cliché) alone, then check in pairs. Ex 3 was done with the cards; Ex 5 (imagery analysis) is Round 1 of the Studio or homework.
Freer: Figurative Language Studio23S–S → S → S–SSlide 17. Round 1, Analyze (6 min): groups of 3 get an imagery passage (the commute as a river, a deadline as a siege, a restaurant kitchen as an orchestra). They name the controlling metaphor and source domain, underline the supporting images, and find the one image that breaks the frame and the one cliché. Round 2, Write (10 min): individually or in pairs, learners choose a subject card and a source-domain card and write 120–150 words built on one controlling metaphor, with two or three supporting images (a simile, a personification, a metonymy) from the same domain. Round 3, Name the frame (7 min): swap; the reviewer reads the passage silently, then writes the controlling metaphor on the slip before talking to the writer, flags any image that breaks the frame and one cliché to refresh. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in four columns. Accuracy: "an extended metaphor" used for an idiom · "as brave like a lion". Precision: simile vs metaphor labels. Appropriacy: a sustained metaphor pushed one image too far. Effect: read (with permission) the freshest image of the day and ask the class what they saw.
Homework and module wrap-up5T–SShow online lesson 2.3; play one 🔊 model sentence ("Time is a thief, and it had already picked our pockets clean."). Set the homework (below). Slide 18 recap; learners tick the three "I can…" lists. Early finishers and the C7 warmer: Between the Lines board game.
Total907 + 5 + 8 + 10 + 7 + 5 + 7 + 8 + 23 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: metaphor vs simile. A simile keeps the two things apart and invites comparison (like, as); a metaphor fuses them and asks the reader to live inside the image. Metaphors can be sustained; similes usually can't.

"Her argument was a fortress." Was it a building? (No.) What do we understand? (It was very hard to attack.) "Her argument was like a fortress." Which is bolder? (The metaphor.) Which one could continue for a paragraph? (The metaphor: walls, siege, gates…)

Meaning: metonymy vs synecdoche. Metonymy names something by an associated thing (the front desk = the receptionists; Hollywood = the film industry). Synecdoche names it by a part (hands = workers; wheels = a car).

"The front desk called about your booking." Did a desk make a call? (No.) Is the desk part of the person? (No, it's associated with them.) So? (Metonymy.) · "We need more hands on the night shift." Are hands part of a worker? (Yes.) So? (Synecdoche.) Why say "hands"? (It focuses on the physical work.)

Meaning: conceptual metaphor. Whole families of ordinary words map one concept onto another. Knowing the family lets you coin new expressions that sound natural.

"She shot down my idea." "He defended his claim." "I won that argument." What is an argument in these sentences? (A battle, a war.) Is that decoration or the normal way to talk? (The normal way.) If ARGUMENT IS WAR, can I say "Her question ambushed him"? (Yes: it's on the map.) Can I say "Her question watered him"? (No: that's a different map.)
Dead, clichéd or live? "The foot of the hill." (Dead: nobody sees a foot.) "Think outside the box." (Cliché: we notice it, and we're tired of it.) "Hope is the thing with feathers." (Live: we see a bird.) Which one should you refresh? (The cliché.)

Form.

Simile frameslike + noun · as + adj + as · as if / as though + clausefear rose like cold water · as busy as a bee · he looked as if he'd seen the invoice
Metaphor framesnoun is noun · metaphorical verb · metaphorical adjective · of-phraseTime is a thief. · The economy staggered. · a half-baked plan · a sea of forms · a mountain of paperwork
Personificationhuman verb or adjective + non-human subjectthe wind howled · the old house groaned · a restless city
Conceptual metaphorTARGET (abstract) IS SOURCE (concrete)ARGUMENT IS WAR · TIME IS MONEY · IDEAS ARE FOOD · LIFE IS A JOURNEY · UP IS GOOD · UNDERSTANDING IS SEEING
Sustained metaphorone source domain, 4+ sentences, varied word classessummer (boom) → warm rain (cheap credit) → cold front (warning) → storm (crash) → shelter (survival)

Pronunciation. Descriptive passages are read slower than conversation, with pauses before each new image and stress on the image-carrying word, not the grammar word: "The library begins to | fall aSLEEP. | The lights | BLINK off | row by row, | like heavy EYElids." In the Studio, when writers choose to read aloud, coach one thing only: pause before the image, stress the image, let the last word of a sentence fall.

Drill script
  1. Name the family (ping-pong, fast): T: "I spent hours on it." → S: "Time is money." T: "That's hard to swallow." → "Ideas are food." T: "She demolished his case." → "Argument is war." T: "We've come a long way." → "Life is a journey." T: "My spirits sank." → "Up is good." T: "That explanation is a bit murky." → "Understanding is seeing."
  2. Keep the frame (individual): T starts a sustained metaphor, S adds one sentence in the same domain. T: "The negotiation was a chess game." → S: "Each side waited for the other to move first." / "She sacrificed a small point to win the big one." T: "The inbox was a flood." → "By noon I was treading water." T: "Our project is a garden." → "This month we're pulling up weeds."
  3. See the picture (fix the mix): T: "We'll burn that bridge when we come to it." → S: "We'll cross that bridge when we come to it." T: "Let's grab the bull by the horns before we drown." → "Let's grab the bull by the horns." / "Let's start swimming before we drown." T: "This is a virgin field pregnant with possibilities." → "This is a new field, full of possibilities."
Figurative Language Studio — how to run it
  • Round 1, Analyze (6 min): groups of 3, one imagery passage each (from the materials: F1 the morning commute as a river, F2 a deadline as a siege, F3 a restaurant kitchen as an orchestra). Each has one planted image from the wrong domain and one cliché. Tasks: name the controlling metaphor (THE COMMUTE IS A RIVER), underline every supporting image, circle the intruder and the cliché, and propose a replacement for each. Groups that finish early do Workbook Ex 5.
  • Round 2, Write (10 min): each learner (or pair) takes a subject card (a place, a mood, an experience or an argument: a hospital waiting room at night · a job interview · learning to drive · the first day in a new city) and a source-domain card (weather, the sea, a theater, a garden, a machine, a chess game, a kitchen, a river), or chooses their own pairing. Name the controlling metaphor in one line, then write 120–150 words: at least four sentences in the same domain and two or three supporting images. New identity cards include a "place I know well" for anyone who prefers not to describe their own life.
  • Round 3, Name the frame (7 min): swap. The reviewer is a cold reader: they read silently and write on the slip (1) the controlling metaphor and source domain, (2) the strongest image, quoted, (3) any image that breaks the frame, (4) one cliché to refresh. Only then do they talk to the writer. If the reviewer can't name the frame, the metaphor isn't controlling enough: that's useful information, not failure. Writers revise one sentence on the spot.
  • Share: two or three volunteers read (or you read them). The class names the frame before the writer says it.
  • Personal content stays optional: a passage about "a mood" can be invented, and a hospital or a new city can be anyone's. Nobody is asked to explain what a passage says about their life.
ICQs before the Studio
In Round 1, what are you looking for? (The controlling metaphor, the supporting images, one image from the wrong domain and one cliché.) · In Round 2, how many source domains? (One.) How many words? (120–150.) Can you mix in a second picture? (No, only images that fit the first.) Must you write about your own life? (No: any subject card, or invented.) · In Round 3, when do you talk to the writer? (After writing on the slip.) What if you can't name the frame? (Tell the writer; it means the metaphor needs to be stronger.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give the kitchen-as-orchestra passage (F3), the shortest, with the domain words pre-listed in the margin (conductor, tuning, section, crescendo, encore). For Round 2, offer a frame: "___ is a ___. In the morning, ___. By noon, ___. When evening comes, ___." and a word bank for the chosen domain. The learner may dictate while a partner writes. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 4 items 1–2. Target: a five-sentence passage in one domain and one named figure.

Stretch

Metaphors steer thinking. Give strong learners a short paragraph about a workplace disagreement written entirely in ARGUMENT IS WAR (attacked, defended, retreated, won) and ask them to rewrite it as ARGUMENT IS A DANCE or ARGUMENT IS BUILDING (laid the foundations, added a floor, reinforced). Then discuss: what changed besides the words? Who "wins" a dance? For writing: sustain a metaphor across two paragraphs, and deliberately end it with a single literal sentence for effect.

L1 watch-outs
  • Different maps: conceptual metaphors are partly universal (UP IS GOOD is very widespread) and partly language-specific. Speakers of Chinese may reach for TIME IS A RIVER, and some languages place the future behind the speaker. Invite comparison if learners want to share ("Is time money in your language?"), but don't make it a required cultural display.
  • Idiom blending: C2 learners often cross an L1 idiom with an English one ("to kill two flies with one stone," Spanish/German/Russian; "to put the cart before the oxen," Spanish/Portuguese; "the drop that made the glass overflow," from many European languages). These are mixed idioms, not mixed metaphors: correct them gently and note that the L1 image is perfectly vivid; it just isn't the English fixed form.
  • like vs as: "as brave like a lion," "like I said you" and "as a ghost" (for like a ghost) from Spanish como, French comme, Portuguese como, Italian come. Board: as + adj + as · like + noun · as + role (she worked as a nurse).
  • Metonymy with institutions: learners used to capital-for-government metonyms in their own press will understand Washington or Brussels, but may not know workplace metonyms (the front desk, the top floor, HR says). These are the more useful ones to teach.
  • Over-decoration: academic and literary traditions in some languages (for example Arabic, Persian, Spanish, Russian) prize rich figurative prose; in English workplace and academic writing, one precise figure beats five. Frame it as a genre difference, not a flaw.
Homework

Online lesson 2.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_06.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and read them aloud with pauses before each image; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "A Passage Built on One Metaphor" project (revise today's Studio passage rather than starting again, and keep the before/after of the freshness pass). Workbook 2.3: Exercise 5 (if not done in class), "My new words" and the journal (five conceptual metaphors caught in the wild this week).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Synecdoche · Q2 B "We'll burn that bridge when we come to it." · Q3 (new) A "We need to nip this problem in the bud before it spreads through the whole garden." Listening (Ex 1): 1 blink · 2 eyelids · 3 exhales · 4 belly · 5 fed · 6 curled; controlling metaphor: THE LIBRARY IS A (SLEEPING) ANIMAL.

Closing the Module

End of C6: Module 2 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "infer it, play with it, picture it": He's very punctual · Why is your team still behind? · reportedly (infer it: what's meant, what's assumed, what's implied) → a bit of a problem · Oh, fantastic · BAKERY RISES TO THE OCCASION (play with it) → she shot down my idea · time is a thief · the library begins to fall asleep (picture it). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C7 warmer (the Between the Lines game is ready for that) and in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 3: discourse markers of stance in 3.1 (admittedly, arguably, allegedly, frankly) are the explicit cousins of this module's distancing markers: make the link out loud ("reportedly in C4 was a stance marker in disguise"). Cohesion across long texts in 3.2 builds on the sustained metaphor: a controlling image is one of the strongest cohesive devices there is. Summarizing and paraphrasing in 3.3 must keep implied meaning: a summary of a faint-praise reference that says "the referee praises the candidate" has failed.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: stating an implicature and naming the flouted maxim, identifying a presupposition trigger and refusing a loaded premise, spotting slant words in a short text, telling sincere from ironic and understated statements in context, explaining a pun, correcting a malapropism, naming figures (metaphor, simile, metonymy, synecdoche, personification), sorting expressions into conceptual metaphors, and fixing a mixed metaphor.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 3 — Discourse Mastery: Structure & Cohesion

Three classes that lift learners from the well-made sentence to the well-made text. First, the small words that steer and color discourse: organizing markers (that said, turning to, in short), stance adverbials (admittedly, arguably, tellingly, ostensibly) and interactional markers (the thing is, mind you, let's face it), used to signal attitude on purpose and to read the attitude other writers smuggle in. Then the architecture of long texts: cohesion versus coherence, theme and rheme (given before new), lexical chains without "elegant variation," topic sentences and the one-sentence-per-paragraph skeleton. Last, the three condensing arts at the heart of C2 reading-into-writing: the faithful summary, the genuine paraphrase (not patchwriting) and the multi-source synthesis, organized by idea and attributed with reporting verbs that are fair to every source.

3 lessons Lessons 3.1–3.3 Classes C7–C9 Weeks 4–5 90 min per class CEFR C2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → a demanding workshop → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-3-discourse.html. Print materials/module-3/classroom-materials.html before C7. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Stance Workshop neutral reports and Stance Detective A/B cards (C7), the cut-up Wrenfield article and the Riverside Radio broken draft for the Cohesion & Coherence Lab (C8), the three library sources and the paraphrase integrity strips for the Synthesis Workshop (C9), the Thread It! board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home and shadow the intonation.
  • This module is a writing-and-reading lab. Each class spends roughly a quarter to a third of its time on one extended product that another learner reads, hears or checks: a neutral report rewritten in three stances (C7), a reconstructed and repaired long text (C8), a 200-word attributed synthesis of three sources (C9). Protect that time. If you're running late, shorten the controlled practice (set the rest for homework), never the workshop.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. At C1 learners met linking and signposting (C1 6.2), reference, substitution and ellipsis (C1 3.2), hedging (C1 3.3), cleft sentences and end-weight (C1 2.3), critical reading for bias (C1 6.1) and note-taking and summarizing a lecture (C1 7.1). Don't re-teach any of it. The C2 step is deliberate effect at the scale of the whole text: choosing the marker that plants exactly the attitude you intend, engineering flow so the reader never stumbles, and restating other people's thought so faithfully and so freshly that nothing is distorted and nothing is borrowed.
  • Feedback on four things. C2 learners rarely make the errors a C1 checklist catches; what they need is feedback on effect. In every workshop, take notes in four columns: precision (the word that is nearly right: ostensibly where apparently was meant, claims where notes is fair, Consequently where no consequence exists), appropriacy (the register seam: anyway in a report, furthermore in a chat, the thing is in a synthesis), naturalness (grammatical but no native writer would do it: three stance adverbials in one sentence, "Firstly… Secondly… Thirdly…" in a two-point email, five synonyms for one noun) and effect (did the reader get the attitude, the order, the attribution you intended?). In the feedback stage, board one or two items from each column, without names, and ask the class to explain the effect before anyone fixes it.
  • Fossilized and near-native at once. Many C2 learners write more elegantly than their teachers in one register and repeat the same fossilized habit in another: "On the other hand" with no first hand, "In my opinion, I think," "Actually" meaning "currently," a comma-less "Frankly I…," a synthesis that marches "Source A says… Source B says…". Name the habit privately and specifically ("You use moreover in speech about once a minute: try and on top of that today") and give it a one-class target. Praise the near-native range out loud; correct the fossil in writing, on a sticky note.
  • Peer review, every class. Each workshop ends with a structured peer-review step that has its own test: the "Name the attitude" + "Strip" tests (C7: can a reviewer name the stance of each version, and when every marker is stripped out, are the facts identical?), the "Skeleton" test (C8: one sentence per paragraph, read as a list: does it make an argument?) and the "Highlighter" + "Whose voice?" tests (C9: any run of three or more words borrowed from a source is highlighted; every claim is tagged with its source or "writer"). Reviewers always give one strength and one suggestion, in that order, and quote the text instead of summarizing it ("In line 4, claims makes you sound doubtful of Hsu").
  • Critique protocol at C2. Strong writers can be bruised by peer comments and can bruise others. Run every review as describe → ask → suggest: first describe what the text does ("Your second version reads as warmly approving because of remarkably and mercifully"), then ask a real question ("Did you intend mercifully to sound relieved?"), and only then suggest. No verdicts ("It's bad," "It's perfect") and no rewriting the writer's text for them.
  • Dominant speakers. C2 classes often include senior professionals, academics and translators who are used to being the best writer in the room. Give them the roles that require listening and restraint: in C7 the stance detective (who may only name the attitude and quote the evidence), in C8 the skeleton writer (who reduces each paragraph to one sentence and reads the list aloud without comment), in C9 the source checker (with the highlighter). Say it kindly: "Your ear is the best in the group. I need it on the evidence this round." In whole-class feedback, use "a new voice first."
  • Stretching the strongest. Each lesson has a stretch box. The direction for this module is controlled effect: moving stance adverbials into medial and final position for rhythm and irony (C7), mixing constant-theme and linear progression deliberately and delaying a point for a "reveal" (C8), and writing a synthesis whose own verdict is visible only in its ordering and emphasis, never in a loaded verb (C9). Ask strong learners to explain the effect of every choice, not just make it.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (organise, summarise, colour, scepticism, honour, travellers, lorries, pounds, pitch). Teach organize, summarize, color, skepticism, honor, travelers, trucks, dollars, field. Accept British forms whenever a learner uses them, and point out that précis keeps its accent in both varieties.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Steer it → build it → distill it. In C7 learners steer a stretch of discourse: organizing markers tell the reader where they are, stance adverbials tell the reader how to take it, and interactional markers keep a conversation reciprocal. They also learn to read the same devices working on them. In C8 they build a whole text that holds together: cohesion on the surface, coherence underneath, information flowing from given to new, one lexical chain, one through-line. In C9 they take other people's texts and distill them, into a gist, a paraphrase and a synthesis, without distorting a source or borrowing its words. Each class depends on the one before: stance language returns as reporting verbs in C9; flow and ordering return as "organize by idea."

Topic care: invented texts, assigned stances, low personal stakes

Stance and synthesis are practiced on invented, neutral texts: a museum's free evenings, open textbooks at a college, a parking app, an orchestra's pay-what-you-can concerts, pocket forests, a community radio station, fine-free libraries. Keep every example and discussion on language, not politics. Every stance in the workshop is assigned on a card: a learner writing the "skeptical" version is practicing a register, not declaring a belief. The C9 journal asks learners to look honestly at their own past paraphrasing. Treat patchwriting as a convention to learn, never as a moral failing: in several educational traditions, reproducing an authority's exact words is a mark of respect and diligence. Any "write about you" task allows a new identity card or an invented answer, and anyone can say "Pass."

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the four picture "register rooms" (book, notebook, email, coffee), organizing markers by function, stance adverbials color-coded by family, cohesion and coherence terms (C8) and reporting verbs by stance (C9). Cut them before C7.
  • For C7: one neutral report per group of 3 (4 reports, so neighboring groups differ), one three-column stance worksheet per group (in the workbook) and one set of Stance Detective A/B cards per pair.
  • For C8: one cut-up set of the Wrenfield article (7 paragraphs, labeled with scrambled letters) per group of 3, split into the A/B/C info-gap packets; one Riverside Radio broken draft per group; a fault log per group. Put each article set in an envelope.
  • For C9: the three library sources as A/B/C expert cards (one set per group of 3), one idea grid per group, the paraphrase integrity strips (one set per pair) and a highlighter per group.
  • Print the new identity cards (six professional profiles) and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • The Thread It! board game (1 board per group, 1 die, counters) is for early finishers in C9 and the C10 warmer.
  • Optional but powerful: bring two real published pieces on the same light topic, for example two reviews of the same restaurant, film or exhibition, one enthusiastic and one skeptical. Learners strip the stance markers from both in C7 and find that the facts are nearly identical.
