Lesson Plans

3.1 · C7

Discourse Markers & Stance

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • choose organizing markers that fit the function (open, add, concede, shift, close) and the mode, and switch a text's register by swapping its hinges (That said → Nevertheless; Anyway → Turning to…)
  • use stance adverbials from four families to plant a precise attitude with one word, distinguishing near-neighbors: admittedly (concede against yourself) vs arguably (defensible but contestable) vs undoubtedly (full commitment); apparently vs ostensibly vs reportedly
  • punctuate and position a stance adverbial so it comments on the whole clause (Frankly, I disagree vs She spoke frankly) and use medial and final positions for rhythm (The result, remarkably, held. · He said nothing, tellingly.)
  • use interactional markers (the thing is, I mean, mind you, let's face it) to manage rapport in speech, and keep them out of formal writing
  • read the stance that others encode (supposedly, so-called, needless to say, as everyone knows) and strip it away to test whether an argument lives in the facts or only in the framing
Materials
  • Workbook 3.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Stance Workshop frame, self-check)
  • Slides 3–7 · "Register rooms" picture cards · organizing-marker and stance-adverbial flashcards · Stance Workshop neutral reports (4) · Stance Detective A/B cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.1-A (the museum commentary) and 3.1-B (dictation)

Left: the joints (organizing). spoken → written: Right, so… → To begin with · On top of that → Furthermore · That said / Mind you → Nevertheless / Admittedly · Anyway → Turning to… · Long story short → In short. The marker announces the register.

Center: the color (stance). "The safety feature caused the accident." + Ironically, / Predictably, / Mercifully, (no one was hurt). Four families: epistemic admittedly · arguably · presumably · ostensibly · attitude frankly · remarkably · tellingly · viewpoint technically · broadly speaking · reportedly · focus notably · if anything · at least. Comma! Frankly, I disagree. / medial / final.

Right: the slant (reading). supposedly · so-called · needless to say · as everyone knows · of course. The Strip test: delete every marker. Are the facts the same? Where did the argument go?

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: rescue the metaphor7S–SReview C6 (2.3, metaphor and imagery). Board three flat or mixed sentences: "The new policy is a double-edged sword that will open the floodgates." · "Her argument was very strong." · "The project has problems." Pairs repair the mixed metaphor and give the flat ones one fresh, consistent image. Two pairs read theirs; the class judges "apt or overdone?" Note one precision item and one range item.
Lead-in: one word changes everything5T–SBoard: "The safety feature caused the accident." Add one word at a time in front: Ironically, · Predictably, · Apparently, · Tellingly, Ask after each: "Did any fact change? (No.) What changed? (The speaker's attitude; how we should read it.)" Elicit stance. "Today: the small words that steer a text and the small words that color it, and how to catch other writers using them on you."
Organizing markers & register9T–S → S–SSlides 3–4. Pairs get 12 organizing-marker flashcards and 90 seconds to place each under a picture "room" (coffee chat / work email / report / essay) and a function (open, add, concede, shift, close). Check on slide 4. Then the lesson's pair: "Right, so the plan didn't work. That said, we learned a lot. Anyway, what next?" → written version. Ask which words did all the mode-switching (only the joints). CCQs below.
Stance adverbials: meaning11T–SSlide 5. Present the four families with one example each; spend the time on near-neighbors (see CCQs): admittedly / arguably / undoubtedly, apparently / ostensibly / reportedly, curiously / ironically / tellingly. Then form: the comma, front vs medial vs final position, and the hopefully debate (fully standard; avoid only in the stiffest formal prose). Slide 6: the exhibition dialogue: learners find stance vs interactional markers.
Drill6T–S → SThree rounds (see Drill script): "Which attitude?" (T gives a neutral sentence + an attitude, S fronts the right adverbial with the right intonation), register switch, and move-the-adverbial for rhythm. Choral for intonation, then individual.
Listening: the museum commentary7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 3.1-A twice. First listening: "Would the speaker recommend the museum? How warmly?" (Yes, with reservations; amused, fair.) Second listening: learners write the eight markers and label each O (organizing), S (stance) or I (interactional). Pairs compare, then say which three words did most to reveal the attitude.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–4 alone (choose the stance, label function and register, signpost and color a bare paragraph), then check in pairs. Board-check only disagreements. Ex 5 (read the slant and strip it) is homework unless a pair finishes early.
Workshop: Stance Workshop + Stance Detective25S–S → T–SSlide 7. Round 1, Three stances (12 min): groups of 3 get one neutral report (about 80 words: a museum's free Thursday evenings, open textbooks at a college, a parking app, pay-what-you-can concerts). Each learner writes one version on the stance worksheet, skeptical, approving or judiciously balanced, then the group polishes all three together. Rules: every fact unchanged, nothing added, 3–5 markers per version, register consistent (a written report). Round 2, Peer review (6 min): swap with the next group. Reviewers run the "Name the attitude" test (label each version without looking at the headings and quote the two words that told them) and the "Strip" test on one version (cross out every marker: are the facts identical to the neutral report?). One strength and one suggestion. Round 3, Stance Detective (7 min): pairs, A/B cards: A speaks for 90 seconds on a topic card in a secret assigned attitude; B, the detective, names the attitude and quotes the evidence. Swap once. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns on the board. Precision: "Ostensibly, attendance rose" (the writer meant apparently; ostensibly implies the stated reason hides the real one). Appropriacy: "Anyway, the board will vote in March" in a written report. Naturalness: "Frankly, honestly, to be fair, the app arguably worked." Effect: a "balanced" version that reviewers read as skeptical. Learners explain the effect first, then fix.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 9 + 11 + 6 + 7 + 10 + 25 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: organizing markers. They change nothing in the propositional content; they tell the reader where we are in the discourse. Their register is their most visible feature.

"That said, we learned a lot." Does that said add a fact? (No.) What does it do? (Concedes / qualifies what came before.) Would you write it in a formal report? (Yes, it's neutral; nevertheless is more formal.) · "Anyway, moving on." Chat or essay? (Chat or a relaxed talk.) What would the essay say? (Turning to… / As for…)

Meaning: stance adverbials. A fronted word, fenced by a comma, comments on the whole clause and reveals the speaker's attitude to it. It is outside the grammar of the clause.

"Admittedly, my figures were incomplete." Is the speaker attacking someone? (No.) Whose position does the admission weaken? (Their own.) Why do it? (To look fair and win trust.) · "Arguably the finest novel of the decade." Is the speaker certain? (Fairly confident, but they accept someone could disagree.) Is arguably weaker than undoubtedly? (Yes, and safer.)
"Ostensibly, it was a courtesy call." Was it a courtesy call? (That was the stated reason.) Does the speaker believe it? (Probably not: there was another, hidden reason.) · "Apparently the deal collapsed." Does the speaker know for sure? (No: they heard it, or it seems so.) · "Reportedly, talks have stalled." Who says so? (A source; the speaker doesn't vouch for it.)
"Tellingly, he said nothing." Is silence important here? (Yes: it reveals something bigger.) Positive or negative? (It depends on context, but usually critical.) · "Curiously, sales rose." Surprised or angry? (Surprised, puzzled.) "Ironically, the diet made her hungrier." What's ironic? (The result is the opposite of the intention.)

Meaning: reading slant. supposedly and so-called plant doubt; needless to say and as everyone knows assume agreement; to be fair stages a concession. Strip them out and the "argument" often disappears.

"The so-called upgrade slowed every laptop in the office." Does the writer think it was an upgrade? (No: the label is ironic.) · "Needless to say, the old system worked better." If it's needless to say, why say it? (To present an opinion as obvious and pull the reader along.)

Form.

Front (default)adverbial + comma + clauseFrankly, the scheme was doomed.
Medialbetween subject and verb, fenced by commasThe result, remarkably, held.
Finalafter the clause, with a commaHe said nothing, tellingly.
Manner vs stanceno comma, after the verb = mannerShe spoke frankly. (in a frank way) vs Frankly, she lied. (I'm being blunt)
Phrase formsto + adj · adj + enough · -ly + speakingto be fair · interestingly enough · broadly speaking · strictly speaking
Reading framesso-called + noun · supposedly · needless to saythe so-called reforms · it was supposedly designed to…

Pronunciation. A fronted stance adverbial is its own intonation unit: a short pause after it and, usually, a fall-rise on the stressed syllable that says "there's more to this": adMITtedly↘↗ | the evidence is thin. Know the stress: ARguably, adMITtedly, preSUmably, osTENsibly, conCEIvably, unDOUBTedly, TELLingly, reMARKably, FRANKly. Medial and final adverbials are said on a lower, quieter pitch, like an aside: "The result, remarkably, held." Interactional markers are unstressed and quick (I mean, you know); stressing them sounds impatient ("LOOK, …").

Drill script
  1. Which attitude? (choral then individual; exaggerate the fall-rise): T: "The trial was small. (concede against yourself)" → S: "Admittedly, the trial was small." T: "No one was hurt. (relief)" → "Mercifully, no one was hurt." T: "The minutes don't mention the budget. (this reveals something)" → "Tellingly, the minutes don't mention the budget." T: "It's a fruit. (strict definition)" → "Technically, it's a fruit." T: "The venue will change. (a reasonable guess)" → "Presumably, the venue will change."
  2. Switch the room: T reads a spoken hinge, S gives a written equivalent: "On top of that" → "Furthermore / What is more." "That said" → "Nevertheless / Be that as it may." "Anyway, moving on" → "Turning to… / As for…" "Long story short" → "In short / In sum." Then the reverse, written → spoken.
  3. Move it: T: "Remarkably, the result held." → S (medial): "The result, remarkably, held." T: "Tellingly, he said nothing." → S (final): "He said nothing, tellingly." Ask: "Which feels more like an aside? Which feels like a verdict?"
  4. Strip it (reading): T reads, S says the bare fact: "Needless to say, the so-called improvement supposedly saved time." → "The change was meant to save time." "Surprisingly enough, the cheaper option, arguably, performed better." → "The cheaper option performed better."
Stance Workshop + Stance Detective — how to run it
  • Groups of 3 with one neutral report. Each learner takes one stance to draft first: skeptical (doubt, distance, a raised eyebrow: ostensibly, supposedly, predictably, needless to say, tellingly), approving (warmth, admiration: remarkably, encouragingly, mercifully, notably, if anything) and judiciously balanced (a scholar weighing evidence: admittedly, arguably, broadly speaking, that said, on balance). Draft alone for 5 minutes, then polish all three together for 7.
  • The rules are what make it C2: ☐ every number and fact identical to the neutral report ☐ nothing added (no invented quotes, no new facts) ☐ 3–5 markers per version, no stacking (never three in one sentence) ☐ at least one medial or final adverbial ☐ written-report register (no anyway, you know, let's face it). A learner who "cheats" by changing a fact (a mere 71% is fine; only 60% is not) must put it back.
  • Peer review (6 min): swap the three versions, headings folded under. Reviewers write the stance they read for each and quote the two words that told them; then they choose one version and run the Strip test, crossing out every marker and checking the bare text against the neutral report. Mismatches are the most useful feedback of the class: "We read your balanced version as skeptical because of ostensibly."
  • Stance Detective (7 min): pairs. A takes a topic card and a secret attitude card (grudging admiration, polite skepticism, wry amusement, enthusiastic but fair, weary disappointment, cautious optimism) and speaks for 90 seconds, spoken register, interactional markers welcome. B listens only for stance, then names the attitude and quotes two markers. Swap once. The detective role goes to your most dominant speaker first.
  • Share: one group reads its three versions aloud in random order; the class names each stance. Ask: "Which version would you trust most as a reader, and why?" (Usually the balanced one, because admittedly makes the criticism look fair.)
ICQs before the Stance Workshop
Can you change a number? (No.) Can you add a new fact or a quote? (No.) How many markers in each version? (Three to five.) Three in one sentence? (No.) Is this a chat or a written report? (A written report.) So is anyway OK? (No.) In the review, what do you write for each version? (The stance, and two words that show it.) What's the Strip test? (Cross out every marker and check the facts are the same.) · In Stance Detective, does B know A's attitude? (No, B guesses.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C2 speakers are far stronger orally than in writing (often learners with interrupted schooling who have lived and worked in English for years). Give them a marker strip sorted by stance (skeptical / approving / balanced, four words each) and let them write one version only, the one they choose, while the group writes the others. In Stance Detective they are often the best detectives: their ear for attitude is excellent. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–6, Ex 3 items 1–6. Target: one clean version with three well-placed markers, read aloud with the pause after each fronted adverbial.

Stretch

Stance by position and scarcity. Ask strong learners to write a fourth, wry version of their report in which the irony lives in a single final-position adverbial (The board, which had predicted disaster, voted to continue the trial, unanimously) and to explain why one well-placed word is more damning than three. Then the reverse skill: give them a real published review and ask them to rewrite it as a neutral report, cataloguing every stance device they removed. Finally, hopefully: write the same sentence for a text message, a university report and a conservative style editor.

L1 watch-outs
  • False-friend markers: actually for "currently" (Spanish actualmente, French actuellement, Portuguese atualmente, Italian attualmente, German aktuell); eventually for "possibly" (German eventuell, Spanish eventualmente, Dutch eventueel); also for "so / therefore" (German also); in fine for "finally" (French enfin). These fossilize even in near-native speakers; board them once and keep a tally.
  • "On the other hand" as "also": very common across L1s. In English it needs a genuine contrast, ideally with a first hand. For addition, use moreover, what is more, on top of that.
  • Formal markers in speech: learners from exam-focused systems (Chinese, Korean, Japanese, Russian, and many South Asian and Arabic-medium schools) often say Firstly… Secondly… Moreover… In conclusion in conversation. Praise the control, then point out the register: "Perfect in your report. At the coffee machine: and another thing… anyway, that's about it."
  • "Of course" and "naturally": Russian конечно, Spanish claro, Portuguese claro, Arabic ṭabʿan are neutral "yes, sure"; English of course can sound as if the listener's question was obvious. Offer sure, certainly, absolutely.
  • "In my opinion, I think…" and "Honestly speaking / Frankly speaking" as fixed openers (common in Chinese-, Hindi-, Urdu- and Arabic-influenced English, and in many exam-trained learners). Neither is wrong, but at C2 one stance marker per clause is enough.
  • Comma omission: languages without a strong comma convention for sentence adverbs (Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Thai, Vietnamese) produce "Frankly I disagree," "Hopefully it works." Tie the comma to the pause they already hear.
  • Stance adverbials that don't translate one-to-one: Spanish de hecho and French en fait map to "in fact," which English uses more sparingly and often for correction; German eigentlich, Dutch eigenlijk are wider than "actually." Encourage learners to find the function first, then the English word.
Homework

Online lesson 3.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_07.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them, pausing after each fronted marker; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Signpost a Commentary — Twice" project (any topic that is not political; a new identity card's "opinion I hold" works well). Workbook 3.1: Exercise 5 (read the slant and strip it), "My new words" and the journal (two sentences: the stance adverbial you most want to activate and one real sentence using it).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C It signals the stated reason, hinting the true reason is otherwise · Q2 B anyway · Q3 (new) B "The trial was, admittedly, small; nevertheless, the results were encouraging." Listening (Ex 1): 1 Right, so (O) · 2 To be honest (I / S) · 3 Admittedly (S) · 4 That said (O) · 5 Arguably (S) · 6 Mind you (I) · 7 frankly (S) · 8 Tellingly (S). Closing: "Anyway, long story short" (O). Attitude: amused, mildly skeptical, fair; recommends the museum with reservations.

3.2 · C8

Cohesion & Coherence Across Long Texts

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • distinguish cohesion (surface links you can point to) from coherence (a line of thought the reader infers), and diagnose a text that has one without the other
  • control information flow: open each sentence on given information (theme) and end on the new (rheme), using linear ("staircase") and constant-theme progression on purpose
  • build a lexical chain (repetition, synonym, superordinate, general word, collocation) that binds and develops a topic, and avoid the elegant-variation trap by repeating the anchor term
  • use meta-discourse sparingly (preview, topic sentence, internal summary, transition, cross-reference) and signpost the turns, not every step
  • reconstruct and repair a long text: choose an ordering principle, write topic sentences and test the whole with the one-sentence-per-paragraph skeleton
Materials
  • Workbook 3.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Lab frame, self-check)
  • Slides 8–12 · Cohesion and coherence flashcards · Wrenfield article cut-up sets (7 paragraphs, A/B/C packets, in envelopes) · Riverside Radio broken drafts · fault logs · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.2-A (the lecture opening) and 3.2-B (dictation)

Left: two virtues. Cohesion = on the page, you can point to it (reference, connectives, lexical links). Coherence = in the reader's mind, you infer it (order, relevance, a through-line). ✘ "The committee approved the budget. However, the budget is a document. Moreover, paper comes from trees." (cohesive, incoherent) · "The scheme failed. Nobody had been consulted." (coherent, under-cohesive). Ask: is the wiring missing, or the thought?

Center: flow. THEME (given) → RHEME (new). "The lecture introduced a new theory. This theory challenges the standard model. The standard model had gone unquestioned…" Linear A→B, B→C · Constant A→B, A→C. Chain: the drought → the dry spell → the water shortage · spaniel → dog → animal. ✘ novel / book / work / volume / tome.

Right: architecture. Topic sentence → development → link. Orders: chronological · general → specific · specific → general · problem → solution · claim → evidence · compare/contrast. Skeleton test: one sentence per paragraph, read as a list. Signpost the turns, not the steps.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: strip and restore8S–SReview C7. Read four sentences with stance (flashcards or slide): "Tellingly, the report never mentions the cost." · "The results, admittedly, were mixed." Pairs strip each to the bare fact, then restore it with a different stance and read it with the pause and fall-rise. Glance at homework journals; note one fossilized marker to target today.
Lead-in: glued, not reasoned5T–SSlide 8. Read the "budget… document… paper… trees" text with total conviction. "Any grammar mistakes? (No.) Any missing links? (No: however, moreover, therefore.) So what's wrong? (It doesn't make sense.)" Then "The scheme failed. Nobody had been consulted…" "What's wrong here? (Nothing logically; it's just bare.)" Elicit the two virtues. "Today: how to build a long text that has both."
Cohesion, coherence & theme / rheme12T–S → S–SSlides 8–9. The diagnostic question (wiring or thought?). Then theme / rheme with the staircase on the board: underline each rheme and draw the arrow to the next theme. Pairs fix the lesson's "sharp fall in productivity" paragraph (new information in the theme slot) in 3 minutes; compare with the model. Name the two progression patterns. CCQs.
Lexical chains, signposting & macro-structure10T–SSlides 10–11. Chains: repetition, synonym, superordinate, general word; the "novel / book / work / volume / tome" muddle and its fix. Meta-discourse moves with the phrase bank; the over-signposting warning. Then the six ordering principles and the skeleton test, modeled on the lesson's cycling paragraph (disjointed → re-engineered).

Second: learners complete the seven signposts and label each move (preview, sequence, internal summary, transition, cross-reference). Pairs compare: "Which signpost could you delete without loss?"
Controlled practice9S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–4 alone (reorder, improve the flow, diagnose cohesion or coherence), then check in pairs. Board-check disagreements only. Ex 5 (build the chain) is homework.
Workshop: Cohesion & Coherence Lab25S–S → T–SSlide 12. Round 1, Reconstruct (10 min): groups of 3; each learner gets a sealed packet (A, B or C) with two or three paragraphs of the Wrenfield article (about 700 words, 7 paragraphs, scrambled letters). No showing: learners read their paragraphs to themselves, then describe or read aloud openings and closings to negotiate the order. When agreed, lay them out and name the ordering principle and three clues (a reference, a summary noun, a theme picking up a rheme). Round 2, Diagnose & repair (9 min): the Riverside Radio draft has six planted faults. Groups log each one as cohesion or coherence and repair the draft in about 120 words. Round 3, Skeleton test (6 min): swap repairs with the next group. The skeleton writer reduces each of the other group's paragraphs (or, for the one-paragraph draft, each move) to one sentence and reads the list aloud; the group says whether it reads as one argument. One strength and one suggestion. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Precision: a summary noun that doesn't fit (This increase… for a fall). Appropriacy: "In this next part I will now…" in a board report. Naturalness: "the scheme… the initiative… the project… the green venture… the program" in five lines. Effect: an order that was coherent but buried the main point. Show the model order (K R D W M T H) and the clue that settled it.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.2 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick "I can…".
Total908 + 5 + 12 + 10 + 5 + 6 + 9 + 25 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: cohesion vs coherence. Cohesion is the visible wiring; coherence is the thought the wiring should carry. Editing the wrong layer is the commonest waste of an editor's time.

"The gallery reopened in March. Consequently, the café serves excellent coffee." Is there a connective? (Yes.) Is it true? (No: the coffee isn't a consequence.) Cohesion or coherence problem? (Coherence: a false link.) Will a better connective fix it? (No: we need a real relation, or no link at all.) · "The scheme failed. Nobody had been consulted." Can you follow it? (Yes.) What's missing? (A little wiring: because.)

Meaning: theme / rheme. The theme is the point of departure, usually given; the rheme is the news, usually at the end, where attention rests (end-focus, which learners met as end-weight at C1).

"The director unveiled a radical proposal. This proposal alarmed her own board." Where does sentence 2 start? (On this proposal, the news of sentence 1.) Given or new? (Given.) Where's the new information? (At the end: alarmed her own board.) · "…Alarm among her own board was the immediate reaction." What's wrong? (Brand-new information in the theme slot: the reader has to hold it in suspense.)

Meaning: lexical chains and elegant variation. A chain binds and develops; variation for its own sake confuses.

"The spaniel… the dog… the animal." Same creature? (Yes.) Is the lens getting wider or narrower? (Wider: specific to general.) · "The novel… the book… the work… the volume… the tome." How many books? (One, but the reader has to keep checking.) Fix? (Repeat novel once and let it do the rest.) Is repeating a key word bad style? (No: repeating the anchor is a virtue.)

Meaning: meta-discourse and the skeleton. The writer steps outside the content to orient the reader. Too much, and the text reads like its own table of contents.

"So far, then, the evidence suggests…" Is this new content? (No, a summary of what came before.) Why use it? (To consolidate before a turn.) · "Firstly, I will now begin by introducing the first point." How many signposts? (Too many for one step.) · If the one-sentence skeleton stutters, should you polish sentences? (No: fix the order or relevance first.)

Form.

Given → newtheme picks up the previous rheme…a sharp fall in productivity. This decline stemmed from…
Summary noun as themethis / that / such + general nounThis neglect · Such objections · That chaos, in turn,…
Moving new info to the endit-cleft · there is · passive · fronting given infoIt is this single thread, trust, that… · There followed a wave of resignations.
Lexical chainrepetition · synonym · superordinate · general word · collocationthe drought · the dry spell · the crisis · the whole business · reservoir, ration, parched
Meta-discoursepreview · topic sentence · internal summary · transition · cross-referenceThis essay will argue that… · A second objection is more serious. · So far, then,… · Against this backdrop,… · as we shall see

Pronunciation. Flow is audible. Given information is de-accented and the nuclear stress falls on the new: "The lecture introduced a new THEory. This theory challenges the STANdard model" (theory in sentence 2 is low and unstressed because it's given). When reading a long text aloud, use a pitch reset: start each new paragraph or topic on a noticeably higher pitch, and let the last sentence of a paragraph fall low. Signposts such as So far, then, and Against this backdrop, are their own intonation units with a pause after them.

Drill script
  1. Hand it on (the staircase): T says a sentence; S starts the next with a summary-noun theme that picks up the rheme. T: "Sales fell sharply in March." → S: "This decline…" T: "The council closed the pool for repairs." → "The closure…" T: "Staff proposed a four-day week." → "The proposal…" T: "The river flooded twice in one winter." → "These floods…" Stress the new information at the end of each S sentence.
  2. De-accent the given: choral, then individuals, with the given word low: "The lecture introduced a new THEory. This theory challenges the STANdard model. The standard model had gone unQUEStioned."
  3. Name the relation (coherence check): T reads two sentences; S says the relation or "none": "The bridge closed. Traffic doubled on Elm Street." → "Cause and effect: so / as a result." "The gallery reopened. The café serves good coffee." → "None: don't fake it."
  4. Pitch reset: two learners read the first two paragraphs of the Wrenfield article (the model order), the second starting noticeably higher. Class judges: "Could you hear the new paragraph?"
Cohesion & Coherence Lab — how to run it
  • Groups of 3, packets A, B and C (the paragraph split is on the materials sheet). In pairs, use the two-packet version. The rule is no showing in the first five minutes: learners may read a paragraph's first and last sentences aloud, or describe it, but not pass it over. This forces them to listen for cohesive clues (These gains, however,… · It is this unplanned expense… · Supporters of the scheme reply…).
  • Reconstruct (10 min): after five minutes, lay all seven paragraphs on the table and finalize the order. On the Lab frame they write: the order (letters), the ordering principle, and three clues that decided it. Any defensible order with a coherent skeleton earns credit, but the article was written with one clear order in mind (answer key).
  • Diagnose & repair (9 min): the Riverside Radio draft (a one-paragraph board report, about 160 words) has six planted faults: empty over-signposting, an irrelevant detail, a false connective, a buried main point with no topic sentence, elegant variation, and new information in the theme slot with an ambiguous This. Groups log each on the fault log as cohesion or coherence before rewriting, then repair it in about 120 words.
  • Skeleton test (6 min): swap repairs. The skeleton writer (give this role to a dominant speaker) writes one sentence per move and reads the list aloud without commentary. The rest of the group answers: Does it make one argument? Is anything redundant or missing? Then one strength and one suggestion, quoting the text.
  • Share: one group reads its repaired draft aloud with pitch reset; the class runs the skeleton test live on the board.
ICQs before the Lab
In Round 1, can you show your paragraphs at first? (No, read or describe them.) What do you write at the end? (The order, the principle and three clues.) Is there only one possible order? (The article has one best order, but explain yours.) · In Round 2, what do you label first? (Each fault: cohesion or coherence.) Rewrite before labeling? (No.) · In Round 3, what does the skeleton writer do? (One sentence per move, read aloud, no comments.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give the article paragraphs with the first sentence of each highlighted and the cohesive clue words boxed (These gains · this unplanned expense · then). The learner's job is to match "what each paragraph points back to" rather than read every word. In Round 2, give the draft with the six faulty sentences numbered and a menu of six fault names to match. Fewer items: Ex 2 set A only, Ex 4 items A–C. Target: the correct first and last paragraphs and three faults labeled correctly, explained aloud.

Stretch

Structure as strategy. Ask strong learners to re-order the Wrenfield article for a different effect: specific → general (open on the drought, the "reveal" later), or compare/contrast (critics and supporters point by point), and to write the new topic sentences so the joins still feel inevitable. Then ask them to mix constant-theme and linear progression in one paragraph on purpose and to explain where each serves. For exam candidates: the CPE Writing Part 1 essay rewards exactly this, a single through-line across two texts.

L1 watch-outs
  • Delayed main point: several rhetorical traditions build toward the point rather than opening with it: the Chinese and Japanese four-part qǐ-chéng-zhuǎn-hé / ki-shō-ten-ketsu structure, some Arabic and Persian persuasive prose, and much Romance-language academic writing. Learners may write a beautiful paragraph whose topic sentence arrives last. Frame the English preference for topic sentence first as a genre convention, not a better way of thinking; the "specific → general reveal" is a legitimate choice when it's deliberate.
  • Synonym fever: French, Spanish, Portuguese, Italian and Russian school writing often penalizes repetition, so learners produce the elegant-variation muddle with pride. Reassure them that repeating the anchor term is good English style.
  • Additive chains: Arabic wa, Persian va and oral narrative styles in many languages link long runs of clauses with and. In English, a run of ands hides the logical relations that coherence depends on.
  • Theme and word order: speakers of free word-order languages (Russian, Polish, Czech, Ukrainian, Finnish, Hungarian) put new information last through word order and may try the same in English ("In the corner stood a man" is fine; "The report wrote the manager" is not). Offer English's tools instead: passive, there is, clefts, a summary noun.
  • Topic-comment openers: Chinese, Japanese and Korean topic-prominence produces "As for the budget, it…," "Regarding the report, it…" at the start of every paragraph. One is fine; five read as a list.
  • Paragraph length and comma splices: German and Spanish academic writing tolerates very long paragraphs and sentences joined by commas. English readers expect one idea per paragraph and a period between independent clauses.
Homework

Online lesson 3.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_08.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them, de-accenting the given words; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Re-Engineer a Text" project (a new identity card's "text I wrote recently" is fine if learners prefer not to use their own writing). Workbook 3.2: Exercise 5 (build the chain), "My new words" and the journal (the skeleton of something they wrote, and one sentence on whether its weakness is order, relevance or a missing through-line).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Coherence · Q2 B "The director unveiled a radical proposal. This proposal alarmed her own board." · Q3 (new) B A coherence problem: one step is repeated, the drought is missing, and the critics get no reply; reorder and cut. Lecture opening (Ex 1): 1 will argue that · 2 I'll begin with · 3 then turn to · 4 finally ask · 5 So far, then, · 6 This raises a further question · 7 A very different picture emerges when. Through-line: the history of the city park is, at heart, a history of public health.

3.3 · C9

Summarizing, Paraphrasing & Synthesis

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • sort a dense source into main idea, supporting detail, example and aside, and reduce it to a faithful one-sentence gist that keeps its logical relations (although, but, because) and its proportions
  • paraphrase by rebuilding the sentence (change word class, voice, clause order, subject), and recognize patchwriting (same skeleton, swapped synonyms) as a form of plagiarism, even when cited
  • choose reporting verbs that carry the right stance (notes · argues · concedes · counters · claims) and distance a source only when the doubt is earned
  • write a synthesis organized by idea, not by source: a governing sentence, agreement, divergence, qualification, and a close that holds the sources in tension
  • check a text for integrity: every claim attributed, no run of borrowed words, no distortion of a source's certainty or scope
Materials
  • Workbook 3.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Synthesis Workshop frame, self-check)
  • Slides 13–17 · Reporting-verb flashcards (by stance) · Library sources A/B/C expert cards · idea grids · paraphrase integrity strips · highlighters · Thread It! board game · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.3-A (the radio panel wrap-up) and 3.3-B (gist it)

Left: the ladder. Summarize = same meaning, far fewer words · Paraphrase = same meaning, same length, new structure · Synthesize = several meanings, one account organized by idea. Summary moves: delete · generalize · supply a topic sentence · keep the logic (although… but…). ✘ "Some critics worry the reform may raise costs" → "The reform is a costly failure."

Center: rebuild, don't re-skin. Original: The company's reluctance to inspect its suppliers has allowed dangerous practices to flourish unchecked. ✘ patchwrite: The firm's unwillingness to examine its vendors has let hazardous practices thrive unrestrained. ✔ Because managers have declined to check on the firms they buy from, unsafe methods have been free to spread. Moves: word class · voice · clause order · subject · then synonyms.

Right: whose voice? neutral: notes, observes · committed: argues, contends, maintains · endorsing: shows, demonstrates, points out · reluctant: concedes, acknowledges · opposing: counters, disputes · doubting: claims, alleges. Synthesis: Both X and Y agree… · Whereas X…, Y counters… · Y qualifies X's claim… · Taken together,…

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: fix the flow6S–SReview C8. Board a three-sentence paragraph with new information in the theme slot and one elegant variation ("A burst pipe was the cause. Flooding of the basement archive followed the pipe's failure. The collection's rescue by volunteers took a week; the holdings were then dried."). Pairs repair it given-before-new with one anchor term. One pair reads it with pitch reset. Collect or glance at C8 journals.
Lead-in: what went wrong?4T–SSlide 13. Board the source "Some critics worry the reform may raise costs" and the summary "The reform is a costly failure." Ask: "Is it shorter? (Yes.) Is it faithful? (No.) What exactly changed? (some → all; may → is; worry → verdict.)" "Today: three ways to give back someone else's meaning without betraying it."
Summarizing: the précis9T–S → S–SSlide 13. Main idea / supporting detail / example / aside, and the four moves. Show the high-speed-line gist and ask what was kept (the concession, the charge, the counter-argument) and cut. Pairs write a one-sentence gist of the museum-admission paragraph (Workbook Ex 2, item 1) in 3 minutes; compare with the model. CCQs.
Paraphrase vs patchwriting9T–S → S–SSlide 14. The social-media original, the patchwrite and the genuine paraphrase. Learners name the moves in the good version (new subject, contrast frame, noun → adjective). Then "close the source" technique: pairs read a sentence, turn the card over, and rebuild it from memory of the meaning. CCQs, including "If I cite it, is patchwriting OK?" (No.)
Reporting verbs & synthesis moves8T–SSlides 15–16. The stance ladder of reporting verbs (notes → argues → points out → concedes → counters → claims) and the Halpern pair (shows / claims). Then weak (source-by-source) vs strong (idea-by-idea) synthesis, with the remote-work model and its moves: governing idea, agreement, divergence, tension.
Drill5T–S → SReporting-verb stance drill and the gist-in-ten-words round (see Drill script).
Listening: the radio panel6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 3.3-A twice. First: "Do the three guests agree on anything?" (Yes: the street is busy on weekends and change is needed.) Second: learners complete the table: who, which reporting verb, what view. Pairs discuss whether the host was fair to Dr. Lee (questions vs a loaded alternative).
Controlled practice6S → S–SWorkbook Ex 3–4 alone (judge and repair the paraphrase; choose the reporting verb), then check in pairs. Ex 5 (synthesize two opposing statements) is homework unless a pair finishes early.
Workshop: Synthesis Workshop27S–S → T–SSlide 17. Topic: Should public libraries abolish overdue fines? Round 1, Experts (6 min): groups of 3; each learner gets one source card (A: a library director; B: a city budget analyst; C: a literacy researcher). Read for 2 minutes, turn the card over, then give the group a 30-second oral gist from memory (a spoken paraphrase: no reading aloud). Round 2, Idea grid (5 min): with all three cards now face up, the group fills the grid: where the sources agree, diverge, and where one qualifies another; they agree a governing sentence. Round 3, Write (11 min): in pairs or alone, a 200-word synthesis (180–220) organized by idea, every claim attributed, fair reporting verbs, nothing copied. Round 4, Integrity review (5 min): swap. The source checker runs the Highlighter test (any run of three or more words from a source, other than names and set terms like overdue fines, is highlighted) and the "Whose voice?" test (label each sentence A, B, C, "all" or "writer"). Then the paraphrase integrity strips as a race for early finishers. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Precision: "Hsu claims the data are unreliable" (he says they're short-term and self-reported; notes or points out is fair). Appropriacy: "Source A says… Source B says… Source C says…" as the whole structure. Naturalness: "According to Ortega, she argues that…" (double attribution). Effect: a synthesis where the reader couldn't tell Nwosu's view from the writer's. Learners explain the effect first, then fix.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.3 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick "I can…" for all three lessons (module wrap-up below).
Total906 + 4 + 9 + 9 + 8 + 5 + 6 + 6 + 27 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: summary. A miniature of the argument, not a bag of surviving facts. Fidelity of proportion matters as much as fidelity of fact.

"Though the high-speed line has cut journey times, its huge overspend has led economists to argue the money would have helped more people elsewhere." What survived? (The concession, the charge, the counter-argument.) What vanished? (The doubled estimate, the commuters, the rural districts: detail and example.) If I write "The line cut journey times and cost a lot," what's lost? (The logic: though, and the economists' argument.) · Can I add "which was a mistake"? (No: that's my opinion, not the source's.)

Meaning: paraphrase vs patchwriting. A genuine paraphrase rebuilds the architecture; a patchwrite keeps it and swaps the furniture.

"The quick growth of social media has basically changed the way young individuals develop their political views." Same subject, verb, object and order as the original? (Yes.) What changed? (Only synonyms.) Is it a paraphrase? (No: patchwriting.) If I add "(Smith, 2024)", is it OK? (No: it's still the author's expression. Quote it or rebuild it.) · Which should you change first, the structure or the words? (The structure.)

Meaning: reporting verbs. The verb is where the writer's stance toward the source lives.

"As Halpern shows, the policy failed." Does the writer agree with Halpern? (Yes: shows endorses.) "As Halpern claims, the policy failed." (The writer doubts it.) "Ortega concedes that fines brought in some money." Is Ortega happy to say so? (No: it's a point against her own position.) · Is it fair to write "Hsu alleges…"? (Only if you can justify the doubt; otherwise it's bias.)

Meaning: synthesis. Organized by idea, not source. If the paragraph could be reordered source by source without loss, it isn't a synthesis.

"Source A says… Source B says… Source C says…" Is that a synthesis? (No: a list of summaries.) What's the first question to ask? (Where do they agree, disagree, or qualify each other?) Must I be neutral? (Fair, not necessarily neutral: your view shows through order and emphasis, openly marked.)

Form.

Gist framesIn essence,… · Boiled down,… · The author's central claim is that…In essence, the author argues that convenience has cost us our privacy.
Paraphrase movesword class · voice · clause order · subject · synonyms (last)reluctance to inspect → have declined to impose rules
AttributionAccording to X, … · As X points out, … · In X's view, … · X argues that …not both at once: ✘ According to X, she argues that…
Reporting-verb patternsverb + that · verb + noun · verb + noun + asHsu notes that… · Nwosu questions the assumption · Ortega describes fines as…
Synthesis connectorsagree · diverge · qualify · extend · concludeBoth X and Y concur that… · Whereas X…, Y counters that… · Y qualifies X's claim, noting… · Taken together,…
Tensepresent for what a text says (American academic default)Ortega argues (not argued) that… · past for what someone did: Nwosu interviewed 60 parents

Pronunciation. In a spoken synthesis the listener needs to hear whose voice is speaking. Put the attribution first, as its own intonation unit, and stress the name when the source changes: "ORtega | argues that…; HSU, | by contrast, | notes that…". When quoting aloud, drop your pitch slightly and slow down, or say "and I quote… end of quote." Know the stress on the verbs: conTENDS, conCEDES, mainTAINS, reFUTES, aLLEGES, aCKNOWledges, QUEStions; and verb vs noun: to disPUTE / a DISpute (both stresses are heard for the noun), to COUNter.

Drill script
  1. Pick the stance: T gives a view + the writer's attitude; S reports it. T: "Ortega: fines kept families away. (neutral)" → S: "Ortega notes that fines kept families away." T: "(you agree)" → "As Ortega points out, fines kept families away." T: "(you doubt it)" → "Ortega claims that fines kept families away." T: "Hsu: fine-free libraries report stable returns. (against his own position)" → "Hsu acknowledges that fine-free libraries report stable returns."
  2. Rebuild it (choral first, then individuals): T: "Critics attacked the plan." → S: "The plan was attacked by critics." T: "Because costs rose, the project stalled." → "The project stalled, since costs had risen." T: "The collapse of the talks surprised everyone." → "Everyone was surprised when the talks collapsed."
  3. Gist in ten words: T reads a two-sentence claim (from the materials sheet); S gives the gist in ten words or fewer, keeping the logical relation. Class checks: faithful? proportion kept? but / although kept?
Synthesis Workshop — how to run it
  • Groups of 3, one source each (A Ortega, library director · B Hsu, budget analyst · C Nwosu, literacy researcher). The sources are short (about 90 words each) and invented, and none is simply "right": Ortega has good local results but a stake in them; Hsu is careful about evidence but frames the issue as cost; Nwosu widens the lens to other barriers.
  • Experts (6 min): read your source for 2 minutes, underline its main claim and its hedges, then turn the card over. Each learner gives a 30-second oral gist from memory. Speaking without the text makes patchwriting almost impossible, which is the point. Listeners may ask one clarifying question each.
  • Idea grid (5 min): cards face up. Fill three rows: agree (for example: fines raise little money; returns stay stable or rise), diverge (whether the lost revenue matters; whether current evidence is good enough), qualify / extend (Nwosu: fines are one barrier among several). Agree a governing sentence that is not a list: "The case for fine-free libraries turns less on money than on who the library is for."
  • Write (11 min): 180–220 words. Checklist: ☐ governing sentence first ☐ agreement before divergence ☐ each source named at least once, with a fair reporting verb ☐ at least one whereas / by contrast and one qualifies / extends move ☐ a close that holds the views in tension (the writer's own view, if any, clearly marked) ☐ no run of three or more borrowed words ☐ nothing sharper or softer than the source said. Those who finish early count words and cut to exactly 200.
  • Integrity review (5 min): swap. The source checker highlights borrowed runs and writes A / B / C / all / writer beside each sentence. The writer must fix any highlight and any sentence whose voice the checker couldn't label. One strength and one suggestion, quoting the text.
  • Share: one synthesis read aloud with the name-stress technique; the class says whose voice is speaking at each point. Early finishers: paraphrase integrity strips, then Thread It!
ICQs before the Synthesis Workshop
In Round 1, do you read your source aloud? (No, you give the gist from memory.) How long? (30 seconds.) · How many words in the synthesis? (About 200: 180 to 220.) Organized by source or by idea? (By idea.) What comes first? (A governing sentence.) Can you use "claims" for a source you disagree with? (Only if you can justify the doubt.) What does the source checker highlight? (Three or more words copied from a source.) What does "writer" mean in the margin? (That sentence is my own view.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give these learners the shortest source (C) with its main claim already underlined, and a frame for the synthesis: "The debate over … turns on … · All three writers agree that … · Whereas Ortega …, Hsu … · Nwosu adds that … · Taken together, …". Their oral gist in Round 1 is often the clearest in the group; build on it. Fewer items: Ex 3 attempts A–C, Ex 4 items 1–5. Target: a 100–120 word synthesis with every sentence's voice labeled correctly by the checker.

Stretch

Exam precision and authorial voice. Ask exam candidates to write the synthesis in exactly 200 words and then a CPE-style evaluation paragraph that weighs the sources' evidence openly ("Of the three accounts, Nwosu's is the most cautious and, arguably, the most useful…"). Then the subtler task: rewrite the synthesis so the writer's view emerges only through ordering and emphasis, with every reporting verb neutral or fair, and have a partner guess the writer's view. Discuss when a distancing verb (claims, would have us believe) is earned.

L1 watch-outs
  • Different conventions of borrowing: in many educational traditions (for example, parts of East and South Asia, the Middle East and Eastern Europe), reproducing an authority's words shows respect and mastery, and quotation marks are used less. Explain the Anglo-American convention clearly and without judgment: in English academic and professional writing, you either quote exactly (with marks) or rebuild completely.
  • Preposition after reporting verbs: "discuss about," "mention about," "emphasize on," "stress on," "criticize about," "explain me" are common in South Asian, Arabic, Chinese and Spanish-influenced English, and often fossilized at C2. Drill discuss / mention / emphasize + noun, explain (to me) that.
  • Automatic backshift: learners trained on reported speech write "Ortega argued that fines were…" for a text that still argues it. In English academic writing the present tense is normal for what a text says.
  • "says" for everything, or the opposite: one learned verb (claims, often) used for every source, unaware of its distancing effect. Build a personal bank of six verbs with their stance.
  • Double attribution: "According to Hsu, he thinks that…" (common across L1s). One frame is enough.
  • Precision of certainty: modal systems don't map one-to-one (German sollen, Spanish conditional of rumor habría, French conditional aurait all mark "reportedly"). Learners may harden or soften a source's certainty in translation; check every may / suggests / shows against the original.
Homework

Online lesson 3.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_09.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow them, stressing the names; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Gist, Synthesise, Deliver" project (choose two texts on a non-political issue: two reviews of the same book or film, or two articles on remote work, sleep or city parks work well). Workbook 3.3: Exercise 5 (synthesize two statements), finish the 200-word synthesis if needed, "My new words" and the journal (rebuild one sentence of a past paraphrase from memory and note what changed; a new identity card's "source I relied on" may be used instead).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A It over-generalizes and distorts the source's cautious claim · Q2 C "Because the scheme was introduced so quickly, many people were left unsure how it worked" · Q3 (new) B "Okafor notes that…". Radio panel (Ex 1): Priya Raman · argues · weekend closure would bring shoppers and cut pollution · Tom Becker · concedes / insists · the street is congested / businesses need delivery access and a trial first · Dr. Lee · questions · the city's footfall figures (collected on one sunny weekend) · agreement: the street is overcrowded on weekends and something must change.

Closing the Module

End of C9: Module 3 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "steer it, build it, distill it": Admittedly, … that said, … tellingly, … (steer and color) → This decline… the pocket forests… so far, then,… (build a text that coheres) → In essence,… · Whereas Ortega argues…, Hsu notes… · Taken together,… (distill and attribute). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C10 warmer (the Thread It! board game is ready for that) and again in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 4: rhetoric and persuasion in 4.1 is stance turned up to full volume: the same adverbials (undeniably, tellingly) and reporting verbs (would have us believe) become persuasive weapons, so ask learners to spot where fair framing ends and manipulation begins. The C8 skeleton test is the best tool for planning a persuasive case in 4.1, and the C9 synthesis grid is the natural planning frame for the 4.3 review project, where learners must fairly represent the view they argue against.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: choosing stance adverbials for a stated attitude (including near-neighbors such as ostensibly / apparently / reportedly), labeling markers by function and register, reading slant, reordering a short text and naming its principle, repairing information flow and elegant variation, diagnosing cohesion vs coherence, a one-sentence gist that keeps the logic, telling paraphrase from patchwriting, choosing a fair reporting verb, and a short synthesis of two statements.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 4 — The Complete Communicator: Style & Rhetoric

Three classes that turn command into effect. First, a rhetoric and persuasion lab: the three classical appeals (ethos, pathos, logos), the named figures that make a line memorable (tricolon, anaphora, antithesis, the rhetorical question), the architecture of a case that survives attack (claim → grounds → warrant → concession → refutation) and the counterfeit moves a master refuses and names (straw man, false dilemma, ad hominem). Then a style and voice workshop: rhythm and sentence shape, economy (omit needless words), strong verbs, and voice, practiced by writing one small event in three distinct voices and critiquing each other's drafts. Last, Mastery in Action, the halfway review: a short speech, a live Q&A and a re-pitched written piece that orchestrate everything from Modules 1–4, scored on a checklist that feeds Progress Test 1 in C13.

3 lessons Lessons 4.1–4.3 Classes C10–C12 Weeks 5–6 90 min per class CEFR C2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → a demanding workshop (a speech, a set of stylistic rewrites, a performance) → feedback with delayed correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-4-rhetoric.html. Print materials/module-4/classroom-materials.html before C10. It holds the flashcards (appeals, figures, fallacies, sentence shapes, deadwood, editing passes and six picture event cards), the new identity cards, the Persuasion Lab topic and side cards with the speech spine sheet and device-hunt grid, the Style & Voice Workshop event cards, voice cards and Critique Circle card, the Mastery in Action topic, audience and challenge cards, the C12 teacher checklist (feeds C13), the Layer It! review game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home and shadow their rhythm, which matters more in this module than in any other.
  • Run these as workshops, and protect the workshop. Roughly a quarter to a third of every class is one extended product: a two-minute persuasive speech (C10), one event in three voices plus a critique circle (C11), a speech, Q&A and written re-pitch (C12). If you're running late, cut the controlled practice (set the rest for homework), never the workshop.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. At C1 (Lesson 4.3) learners met the rule of three, contrast, the rhetorical question and signposting for a three-minute talk, and in C1 Module 3 they learned plain English and the "Who does what?" test. Don't re-teach any of it. The C2 step is deliberate effect with restraint: naming a figure and choosing it for a reason, balancing the three appeals, conceding on purpose, spotting manipulation, and cutting the line you love when it serves only itself.
  • Feedback on four things, not one. C2 learners rarely make errors that block meaning, so "accuracy" alone gives them almost nothing. Take notes in four columns: precision (the nearly-right word or figure: a tricolon of four items, anaphora used for what is really epistrophe, very unique), appropriacy (right device, wrong room: soaring anaphora in a status update, a joke at a moment that needed gravity), naturalness (grammatical but no English speaker would say it: I will explain you why, as a conclusion) and effect (did it land? where did the room lean in, and where did it drift?). The effect column is the most useful at this level, and the one learners almost never receive. In the feedback stage, board one item from each column, without names.
  • Near-native in some areas, fossilized in others. Your strongest learners may argue brilliantly and still say discuss about, informations or since three years, errors they have made for twenty years without anyone telling them. Keep a private fossil list per learner (one line each). Once a week, give each learner one fossil, privately, with the correct form and one sentence of their own rewritten: "You said We need to discuss about the budget. At C2 it's discuss the budget. That one's worth fixing for good." One fossil at a time is fixable; a list of twelve is discouraging.
  • Peer critique with a protocol, every class. Unstructured peer feedback at C2 collapses into politeness ("Very good!") or into a showcase for the most confident critic. Each workshop has its own protocol: the Device Hunt + flat-reading test (C10: listeners quote the devices they heard, then the speaker reads their best line flatly: if it only works with the performance, question it), the Critique Circle (C11: the author stays silent; critics say what stayed with them, the author asks one question, critics ask neutral questions, and only then offer one darling to kill and one fix, with permission) and the re-pitch check (C12: could the new reader act on this?). Critics always quote the text instead of summarizing it.
  • Dominant speakers. C2 classes attract accomplished people used to holding a room: lawyers, academics, managers. Give them the jobs that need listening, not talking: the fallacy watcher and timekeeper in C10, the last critic in C11 (they may speak only after everyone else, and may not repeat a point), the challenger who must paraphrase before asking in C12. Say it kindly: "Your ear for this is excellent. I want you on the fallacy card this round." In whole-class feedback, use "a new voice first."
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and a few British words (recognise, generalisation, defence, honour, practised, per cent, summarising, humour, colour, sceptical, theatres, pound). Teach recognize, generalization, defense, honor, practiced, percent, summarizing, humor, color, skeptical, theaters, dollar. Accept British forms whenever a learner uses them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Persuade → sound like yourself → orchestrate. In C10 learners aim their language at an audience: an appeal chosen on purpose, a figure placed at a hinge, a concession that buys trust, and the critical armor to feel someone else's rhetoric working on them and still refuse it. In C11 they turn the same attention inward, to the sound of their own prose: rhythm, economy, strong verbs, and a voice consistent enough to be recognized. In C12 they do everything at once, for a real audience, and then re-pitch it for a different one. Every class ends with something another learner hears or reads: a two-minute speech, three short pieces in three voices, a speech with Q&A and a written re-pitch.

Trauma-informed: neutral topics, assigned sides, invented pathos

Rhetoric invites strong feelings. Every persuasion topic in this module stays in workplace, education, science, arts and culture, technology, language and community territory (a tool library, a meeting-free afternoon, handwriting, quiet carriages, museum opening hours). There are no topics about party politics, religion, immigration policy, war or national identity, and no learner is asked to argue about their own country or community. Sides are assigned on the card: learners argue the card, not their convictions, and may say so ("I've been assigned the case against"). Pathos is the risky appeal: model invented, low-stakes anecdotes (a neighbor waiting three months for a fence repair) and say explicitly that nobody should use a personal loss or hardship as their story. The C11 events are small everyday mishaps (a collapsed cake, a bus that left early), never accidents, illness or grief. The online 4.3 journal asks learners to write to "the person you were at the start"; for some newcomers that period was painful, so always offer "or a letter to a learner who is just starting out." Anyone can say "Pass," take a new identity card, or present only to you.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (Set A, one set for the board): appeals (3) and figures (7) for C10, fallacies (8) for C10, sentence shapes (5), deadwood culprits (7) and editing passes (6) for C11, and the six picture event cards (suitcase, umbrella, cake, bus stop, market, kitchen) for the C11 workshop.
  • For C10: one set of Persuasion Lab topic cards with side cards (Set C), one speech spine sheet per learner, one device-hunt grid per learner, and a phone timer per group of 3.
  • For C11: the event cards and voice cards (Set D, one event per pair; every learner gets the three core voices, plus a genre voice card for stretch), and one Critique Circle protocol card per group.
  • For C12: the Mastery in Action topic, side and audience cards and challenge starters (Set E), the teacher checklist with class grid and score slips (Set F), and the Layer It! board game (Set G: one board per group of 3–4, a die, counters) for the warmer.
  • Print the new identity cards (Set B) and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional but powerful: bring (or project) one short, genuinely admired speech excerpt from a non-political context, for example a commencement address, a scientist accepting a prize, or a museum director opening a new gallery. Avoid election speeches and anything tied to a current controversy. Two minutes of real rhetoric heard aloud does more than a page of definitions.
  • Tell learners at the end of C9 (or by message) to bring an idea they'd like to argue for in C12, within the topic territory above. They'll get a topic card if they don't have one.
