Lesson Plans

4.1 · C10

Rhetoric & Persuasion — The Art of Argument

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • identify the dominant appeal in a persuasive extract (ethos: credibility; pathos: emotion; logos: reason and evidence) and explain why a strong case braids all three
  • recognize and produce the core figures, tricolon, anaphora, antithesis and the rhetorical question, with parallel grammar and the right rhythm, and place them at the hinges of an argument (opening, pivot, close)
  • build a case along the classical spine (claim → grounds → warrant → concession → refutation → restated claim) and concede narrowly, refute decisively
  • name common fallacies (straw man, ad hominem, false dilemma, slippery slope, bandwagon, hasty generalization) and counter them calmly by redirecting to the actual claim
  • judge when high rhetoric fits the occasion (a speech, an op-ed) and when it grates (a report, a status update)
Materials
  • Workbook 4.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Persuasion Lab planner, self-check)
  • Slides 3–8 · Appeal, figure and fallacy flashcards (Set A) · Persuasion Lab topic and side cards, speech spine sheets, device-hunt grids (Set C) · phone timers · New identity cards (Set B)
  • Listening scripts 4.1-A ("Lend It, Don't Buy It": the tool-library speech) and 4.1-B ("Spot the counterfeit")

Left: where persuasion comes from. ETHOS: trust me (As someone who has… · conceding a fair point) · PATHOS: feel this (Picture, for a moment… · one telling detail) · LOGOS: see why (The figures are stark: · It follows that…). Braid all three: open on ethos, reason with logos, close on pathos.

Center: the shapes. TRICOLON sharpens the mind, softens the heart, and widens the world · ANAPHORA This city deserves… This city deserves… · ANTITHESIS not to spend more, but to own less · RHETORICAL Q Can we really afford to wait? · (EPISTROPHE · CHIASMUS · HYPOPHORA). Rule: few, and at the hinges.

Right: the spine. claim → grounds → warrant (This matters because…) → concession (I grant that…) → refutation (But this overlooks…) → restated claim. Concede narrowly, refute decisively. Counterfeits: straw man · ad hominem · false dilemma · slippery slope · bandwagon · hasty generalization. Test: Say it flat. Does it still hold?

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: synthesis in a sentence8S–SReview C9 (3.3, summarizing and synthesis). Board two short opposing views: "Video calls save hours of travel and let quieter colleagues speak up." · "Video calls drain energy, and the best ideas still come from corridor conversations." Pairs write (1) a one-sentence synthesis (While the first writer stresses…, the second counters that…) and (2) a twelve-word précis of the whole debate (Boiled down, the debate turns on…). Two pairs read theirs. Note one precise reporting verb and one clumsy one for later.
Lead-in: same claim, different force5T–SSlide 3. Read the flat claim ("We need to act now, because it will get worse and cost more if we wait…") in a neutral voice, then the figured version ("We can act now, or we can pay later…"). Ask: "Same content? (Yes.) Which one would you remember tomorrow? Why? What did the second speaker do?" Elicit: balance, repetition, a triple, an image of the future. "Today: the named techniques behind that feeling, and the ethics of using them."
Meaning: the three appeals10T–S → S–SSlide 4. Hold up the three appeal flashcards. Give pairs 3 minutes to sort six short extracts (Workbook Ex 2) by dominant appeal; check on the slide. Establish the balance point: pure logos convinces the intellect but rarely moves anyone to act; pure pathos collapses under scrutiny; pure ethos is assertion. Link to C1 (Lesson 1.1): loaded words are pathos in disguise (casualties vs slaughter). CCQs (below).
Form: the figures10T–SSlide 5. Present the four core figures with one invented example each, then show how great lines stack figures (Kennedy's Ask not… is both antithesis and chiasmus). Keep epistrophe, chiasmus and hypophora brief: they're for recognition and stretch. Stress the grammar: a figure only works if the parallel parts are grammatically parallel (to spend more… to own less, not spending more… to own less). CCQs.
Drill: the rhythm of figures5T–S → SChoral then individual (see Drill script): the tricolon's rise-rise-fall, antithesis with contrastive stress, the falling rhetorical question, anaphora with a steady beat. At C2 the drill is about delivery, not form: learners who can write a perfect tricolon often say it in a flat monotone that kills it.
Listening: "Lend It, Don't Buy It"12T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.1-A (a retired carpenter proposes a tool library at a neighborhood meeting) with conviction. First listening: "What is she proposing? Why should we trust her? What objection does she expect?" Second listening: learners complete the seven quoted lines and name the figure in each. Then slide 6 (the spine): pairs label the moves in order and find the one counterfeit line (the bandwagon: "everyone I've spoken to already agrees with me, so the matter is settled"). Ask: "Did it work on you the first time? Why is it dangerous in an otherwise honest speech?"
Controlled practice: fallacies and concessions8T–S → SSlide 7. Read Script 4.1-B ("Spot the counterfeit," six lines) once, pausing after each: learners call out the fallacy, and a volunteer counters it in one calm sentence. Then Workbook Ex 3 (rewrite with a named figure) items 1–2 alone. Ex 3 items 3–4, Ex 4 and Ex 5 are homework unless a learner finishes early.
Freer: Persuasion Lab23S → S–S → T–SSlide 8. Draft (9 min): each learner draws a topic card and a side card (Set C) and fills the speech spine sheet: the claim in one sentence, grounds, warrant, the concession (draft it first), the refutation, the close. They script only three lines in full (the opening, the pivot, the close) and mark at least three named figures and all three appeals. Deliver (9 min): groups of 3, two minutes each (timekeeper stops them kindly); the two listeners tick and quote on the device-hunt grid. Critique (5 min): the Device Hunt report (listeners quote what they heard, and where) and the flat-reading test (the speaker reads their proudest line in a flat voice; the group says whether it still holds). One speaker per group whose devices were all spotted performs for the class if time allows. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns on the board, no names. Precision: "a tricolon of four items," "anaphora" used for end-repetition. Appropriacy: pathos that outran the evidence; three rhetorical questions in a row. Naturalness: "I will explain you why," "Let's discuss about." Effect: the line the room reacted to, and why. Learners fix and discuss.
Homework set-up4T–SShow online lesson 4.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total908 + 5 + 10 + 10 + 5 + 12 + 8 + 23 + 5 + 4
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the appeals. Every persuasive utterance draws on credibility, emotion or reason, usually more than one. The question is never "which appeal?" but "which one dominates, and is the balance right for this audience?"

"As a former hospital administrator, I know what a stretched budget feels like." Which appeal? (Ethos: experience, shared understanding.) Is it evidence? (No: it's a reason to trust the speaker.) · "Behind every statistic is a family waiting for a fence to be fixed." Which appeal? (Pathos.) Is that dishonest? (Not if the statistic is real; it becomes dishonest if the feeling replaces the evidence.) · A speech that is only numbers: will people act on it? (Rarely: logos alone leaves the will unmoved.)

Meaning: the figures. A figure is a patterned arrangement of words that pleases the ear and stresses the sense. It should clarify the argument, not decorate it.

"We buy it, we use it once, and we store it for a decade." How many parts? (Three.) Same grammar in each? (Yes: we + verb + object.) What's the effect? (Complete, memorable, a little ironic.) · "I'm not asking you to spend more; I'm asking you to own less." What's opposed? (Spend more / own less.) Name? (Antithesis.) · "Isn't that what a neighborhood is for?" Does she want an answer? (No: the listener supplies "Yes.") · Five figures in one paragraph: more persuasive? (No: nothing stands out when everything is figured.)

Meaning: the strategic concession. Granting the opponent's strongest fair point makes you look reasonable, disarms the objection before it's raised against you, and makes your refutation land.

"I grant that some tools may come back damaged. But a small repair fund costs far less than forty-two households each buying a drill." Does she agree with the objection? (Partly: the fact is true.) Does she give up her claim? (No.) Which part is narrow? (The concession: some tools.) Which part is decisive? (The refutation: the cost comparison.) · A speaker who never concedes anything: how do we see them? (As partisan: we discount them.)

Meaning: fallacies. A fallacy looks like an argument but proves nothing. The master refuses them and names them in others, calmly.

"You can't trust his report on the parking lot. He can't even park straight." Is that about the report? (No: it attacks the person.) Name? (Ad hominem.) How do you answer? ("His parking isn't the question; the report is. What's wrong with its figures?") · "Either we cancel the party or we go over budget." Only two options? (No: a cheaper party, a potluck, a later date.) Name? (False dilemma.)

Form.

Tricolonthree parallel elements, often rising in length or weightReading sharpens the mind, softens the heart, and widens the world.
Anaphorathe same words open successive clausesThis city deserves schools that… This city deserves transport that…
Epistrophethe same words end successive clauses…of the people, by the people, for the people.
Antithesiscontrary ideas in balanced, parallel grammar: not X but Y · X, not Y · It is not that…; rather…This is not a setback; it is an invitation to begin again.
Chiasmusmirror order, A-B-B-AWe do not work to live well; we live well to work well.
Rhetorical question · hypophoraa question for effect · a question the speaker answers at onceCan we really afford to wait? · Why does that matter? I'll tell you why.
Concede → refuteI grant that… / There is force in the objection that… / To be fair to the other side,… → But this overlooks… / That may be so, yet it does not follow that…Admittedly, culture is harder to measure. Yet is it the office that builds it, or the people?

Pronunciation. Figures live in the voice. Tricolon: rise, rise, fall, with a small pause before the last item: "It's cheaper ↗ | it's cleaner ↗ | and it's already here ↘." Antithesis: contrastive stress on the opposed words, and a pause at the hinge: "not to spend MORE | but to own LESS." Anaphora: keep the repeated opening at the same pitch and let the variable part carry the stress, so the ear hears the pattern. Rhetorical questions normally fall at the end ("Isn't that what a neighborhood is ↘ for?"); a rising tone sounds like a real question and invites an answer. Hypophora: a real pause after the question, then a lower, slower answer.

Drill script
  1. Three beats (choral, then individual; clap the three beats if the class enjoys it): T: "We cannot wait, we must not wait, and we will not wait." → S repeats with rise-rise-fall. T: "Share the drill, share the ladder, share the know-how." → S. Then learners complete a tricolon from a stem: T: "Good design is…" → S: "…simple, honest, and built to last."
  2. Hinge it (antithesis, individual): T gives a flat pair; S makes it balanced and stresses the opposed words. T: "Prevention is better than reaction." → S: "We should not wait for the fire to fight it; we should build so it never starts." T: "Owning less, sharing more." → S: "I'm not asking you to spend more; I'm asking you to own less."
  3. Fall, don't rise: T reads a rhetorical question with a rising tone; S corrects it with a fall. "Can we really afford to wait?" · "Isn't that what a neighborhood is for?" · "If we do nothing today, what do we tell our children tomorrow?"
  4. Concede → refute (open pairs, fast): T reads an objection ("Won't the tools get stolen?"); S1 concedes narrowly ("I grant that a few may go missing"), S2 refutes decisively ("but the evidence from other libraries is that borrowers bring things back, because their neighbors are waiting").
Persuasion Lab — how to run it
  • Topics and sides. Ten neutral topics on Set C, each with a for and against side card: a meeting-free afternoon every week, handwriting in adult education, free museum evenings, a tool library, recipes in weights rather than cups, quiet carriages on commuter trains, a four-day school timetable for adult classes, repair cafés, rotating who chairs meetings, and learning a musical instrument as an adult. Learners draw a topic and a side; they argue the card, not their convictions. A learner may swap once, no reason needed.
  • Two minutes, not four. The online project asks for 3–4 minutes; in class, two minutes per speaker lets three speakers perform and be critiqued inside 23 minutes, and forces economy: roughly 250–280 words. The full version is homework.
  • Notes, not a script. The spine sheet has one line per move. Only three lines are scripted in full: the opening, the pivot (concession → refutation) and the close, because that is where figures belong. Speakers mark their figures in the margin (T for tricolon, A for anaphora, X for antithesis, RQ for rhetorical question).
  • Device Hunt. Each listener has a device-hunt grid (Set C): three appeals and seven figures, plus "concession" and "counterfeit move." They tick what they hear and quote a few words so the speaker knows where. After each speech, one minute: listeners report ("I heard antithesis in the close: not louder, but clearer"). The speaker then reveals their marked script: devices that were present but not heard are interesting data, not failures.
  • The flat-reading test. Each speaker reads their proudest line in a completely flat voice. If the line still makes a claim that holds, it's rhetoric serving an argument. If it only worked with the performance, question it. This is the online lesson's "If a line survives being read flatly, keep it" made into a routine.
  • Roles. In each group of 3: speaker, timekeeper (also listens), fallacy watcher (also listens; flags any counterfeit move, kindly). Rotate after each speech. Give the fallacy watcher and timekeeper roles to dominant speakers first.
  • If a learner freezes, the timekeeper says "Thirty seconds: your close." A strong close after a shaky middle still earns applause.
ICQs before the Persuasion Lab
Whose opinion are you arguing? (The card's.) · How long do you speak? (Two minutes.) · How many lines do you write in full? (Three: the opening, the pivot, the close.) · What must your concession do? (Grant a fair point, narrowly.) · What do listeners do while you speak? (Tick and quote devices on the grid.) · What's the flat-reading test? (Read your best line flat: does it still hold?) · Can you say "Pass"? (Yes.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C2 speakers argue far better aloud than on paper (often learners who have lived and worked in English for years with little formal schooling in it). Give them a spine strip with the six moves pre-printed and one frame for each ("I want to argue that… · Consider this: … · This matters because… · I grant that… · But… · So I come back to…") and accept one figure placed well (an antithesis in the close is enough). They can deliver from the strip without scripting anything. In the Device Hunt, let them tick without quoting. Fewer items: Workbook Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 4 items 1–3. Their ear is often the sharpest in the room for a line that "sounds fake": invite that judgment in the flat-reading test.

Stretch

Ask strong learners to stack figures in one line (antithesis + chiasmus: We do not share tools to save money; we save money because we share), to build a close with epistrophe, and to use hypophora at the pivot. Then the harder skill: restraint. Their two-minute speech may contain no more than four figures; they must say afterward which figure they cut and why. For the fallacy work, ask them to write a persuasive but fallacious paragraph (one hidden fallacy, dressed in an honest-looking tricolon) and challenge the group to find it. Discuss: the craft is neutral; the ethics are not.

L1 watch-outs
  • Where the claim goes: persuasive writing in Chinese, Japanese, Korean and Thai traditions often builds context first and states the position late, or leaves it implicit. English persuasion expects the claim early and restated at the end. Present it as a genre difference ("in this room, the claim comes first"), not as a flaw in their thinking.
  • Repetition and ornament: Arabic, Persian and Urdu rhetoric values parallelism, repetition and elaborate praise, and learners may produce beautiful anaphora three times in a row. Praise the control, then teach spacing: one anaphora per speech lands harder than three.
  • Syllable-timed rhythm: speakers of Spanish, French, Italian, Portuguese, Tagalog, Turkish and Cantonese may give every syllable similar weight, which flattens a tricolon and hides contrastive stress. Drill the stressed words and the pause before the last item.
  • Rhetorical questions with a rise: many learners (and some world Englishes) rise at the end of every question, so the audience starts to answer. Teach the fall.
  • False friends in argument: Spanish argumento and Italian argomento (plot or topic) → "the argument of the film"; French discuter de and Spanish discutir sobre → "discuss about"; actualmente / actuellement / aktuell → "actually" meaning "currently"; eventualmente / éventuellement / eventuell → "eventually" meaning "possibly"; "as a conclusion" (como conclusión, en conclusion) → In conclusion / In short.
  • "I will explain you why" (Spanish te explico por qué, Italian ti spiego, Russian объясню вам): explain takes to + person, or none: Let me explain why. A classic fossil at this level.
Homework

Online lesson 4.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_10.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and shadow the rhythm (rise, rise, fall; stress the opposed words); do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Expand the Persuasion Lab speech into the online project's 3–4 minute version on the same assigned topic (the online example motions are optional; stay with your card) and record it on a phone if possible. Workbook 4.1: finish Exercises 3–5, "My new words" and the journal (one persuasive line you heard this week in an advertisement, a speech or a meeting: name its appeal and its figure, and say whether it survives the flat-reading test).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C Antithesis and chiasmus · Q2 B Ad hominem · Q3 (new) B "I grant that some tools may come back damaged; but a small repair fund costs far less than every household buying its own." Listening (Ex 1b, Script 4.1-A): 1 saw (ethos) · 2 use / store (tricolon) · 3 asset / cost (antithesis, with a metaphor) · 4 I'll tell you why (hypophora) · 5 more / less (antithesis) · 6 share the know-how (anaphora + tricolon) · 7 neighborhood (rhetorical question). Counterfeit line: "everyone I've spoken to on this street already agrees with me, so the matter is really settled" (bandwagon, and a hasty generalization from her own conversations).

4.2 · C11

Style, Voice & Creative Command

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain style as the sum of consistent choices (word, register, figure, rhythm, stance) and voice as the personality those choices project
  • vary sentence length and shape on purpose (simple, compound, complex, periodic, loose), place a short sentence or a fragment for impact, and use the read-aloud test
  • cut deadwood (redundant pairs, empty intensifiers, throat-clearing, deadweight phrases, hedging overload, clichés) without sliding into terse "telegram prose"
  • free smothered verbs (make a decision → decide) and prefer the concrete, active and specific by default, breaking the default knowingly
  • write the same event in three distinct, sustained voices, and give and receive precise, quoted critique on style and voice
Materials
  • Workbook 4.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Three Voices frame, Critique Circle notes, self-check)
  • Slides 9–13 · Sentence-shape, deadwood and editing-pass flashcards + six picture event cards (Set A) · Style & Voice Workshop event cards, voice cards (3 core + 6 genre), Critique Circle cards (Set D) · New identity cards (Set B)
  • Listening scripts 4.2-A (the fire drill in three voices) and 4.2-B (rhythm dictation)

Left: the music. SIMPLE The room fell silent. · COMPOUND She spoke, and… · COMPLEX When she spoke,… · PERIODIC When she rose, when she drew breath, when at last she spoke, the room fell silent. (meaning held back) · LOOSE The room fell silent, all argument spent, every eye on her. (point first) · Long, long, long… then short. Hard. Fragment: For emphasis.

Center: economy. each and every every · very unique unique · It is important to note that (just say it) · due to the fact that because · make a decision decide · give consideration to consider · it seems that perhaps it might possibly (one hedge). Economy ≠ terseness: cut the dead, keep the living.

Right: voice. Voice = consistency: same formality, same appetite for image, same rhythm of thought, same relationship to the reader. Three voices today: THE REPORTER (spare, exact, impersonal) · THE LYRIC ESSAYIST (periodic, image-rich, reflective) · THE DEADPAN WIT (understatement, irony, the twist at the end). Edit in passes: cut → sharpen verbs → vary rhythm → check flow → read aloud → kill your darlings.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: figure it, name it8S–SReview C10. Hold up a figure flashcard and read a flat claim ("Libraries matter for many reasons."); pairs have 60 seconds to recast it with that figure. Three rounds (tricolon, antithesis, anaphora). Then one fallacy line from the fallacy cards: pairs name it and counter it. Ask two or three learners how their homework speech went: "Which device did your listener spot first?"
Lead-in: one event, three voices8T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.2-A: a fire drill interrupts a budget meeting, told by the Reporter, the Lyric Essayist and the Deadpan Wit. First listening: "Same facts? What changed?" Second listening: learners tick the feature grid for each voice. Pairs compare. "Nothing about the event changed. Everything about the writer did. That's style."
Rhythm and sentence shape10T–S → S–SSlide 9. Present the five shapes with one event ("the room fell silent"), then the long-then-short effect. Read the monotone and the rhythmically varied city passages from the online lesson aloud; ask what changed. Pairs do Workbook Ex 2 (name the shape) in 3 minutes. CCQs.
Economy and strong verbs8T–SSlide 10. The deadwood culprits (flashcards on the board) and the committee sentence cut from 42 words to 14. Learners call out each cut. Then the weak verb + noun pairs (conduct an investigation into → investigate) and the C1 link: this is nominalization run in reverse. Make the counterweight explicit: economy is not terseness. CCQs.
Drill: read it aloud5T–S → SScript 4.2-B and the Drill script: learners mark stresses and pauses, then read the rhythm models aloud, choral then individual. The read-aloud test is the lesson's most practical tool: make it physical.
Voice and pastiche6T–SSlide 11. Voice as a fingerprint: sentence length, word stock (plain or Latinate), figures (few or many), stance (distant or intimate), and the relationship to the reader. Show the three core voice cards. Pastiche: "Imitating a manner is an old and honorable exercise. You keep what fits and let the rest fall away." Introduce the genre voice cards for stretch (a noir detective, a nature-documentary narrator, a sports commentator…).
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 3 (cut to the lean) and Ex 4 (rewrite the monotone passage) alone, then compare in pairs. No single answer: pairs read both versions aloud and keep the one that moves. Ex 5 (voice fingerprints) is homework unless a learner finishes early.
Freer: Style & Voice Workshop28S → S–S → T–SSlides 12–13. Write (14 min): each pair draws a picture event card (Set D). Each learner writes the event in three voices (the Reporter, the Lyric Essayist, the Deadpan Wit), about 50–70 words each, on the workbook frame. Same facts in all three; nothing invented that changes what happened. Critique Circle (12 min): two pairs join (groups of 4). Each writer's pieces are read aloud by someone else, in random order and unlabeled; the group identifies each voice and names two features that gave it away. Then the four-step protocol on the Critique Circle card (see below), about 3 minutes per writer. Share (2 min): one Deadpan Wit and one Lyric Essayist version read to the class. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns, no names. Precision: "very unique," "each and every," a periodic sentence that loses its main clause. Appropriacy: the Reporter who slipped in an adjective of opinion; the Wit who explained the joke. Naturalness: "It was raining strongly" (heavily), "The bus made a delay" (was late). Effect: the short sentence that landed; the darling the writer was brave enough to cut.
Homework set-up4T–SShow online lesson 4.2 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Remind learners to bring an idea to argue for C12 (topic cards available). Learners tick "I can…".
Total908 + 8 + 10 + 8 + 5 + 6 + 8 + 28 + 5 + 4
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: style and voice. Style is the pattern that many small, consistent choices make; voice is the personality a reader hears in that pattern. Neither is more "correct"; both are judged by fitness and consistency.

Two writers report the same fire drill; one uses exact times and no adjectives, the other uses images and long sentences. Is one of them wrong? (No: different choices.) What makes each one a voice? (Consistency: every sentence leans the same way.) If the Reporter suddenly writes "like people released from a sentence," what happens? (The voice breaks: we stop trusting the persona.)

Meaning: periodic and loose sentences. A periodic sentence withholds the main clause until the end and builds suspense; a loose sentence delivers the point first and elaborates, which feels relaxed.

"When she rose, when she drew breath, when at last she spoke, the room fell silent." When do we get the main point? (At the end.) Effect? (Suspense, weight.) · "The room fell silent, all argument spent, every eye turned toward her." Main point where? (First.) Which one feels more conversational? (The loose one.) · Five periodic sentences in a row? (Exhausting: vary.)

Meaning: economy. Every word earns its place. The goal is not the fewest words but no dead ones.

"Every applicant must submit a complete application." What did we cut from "each and every one of the applicants is required to submit a full and complete application"? (Redundant pairs, padding.) Did we lose meaning? (No.) · "Her long experience let her help the newer team members." Could we cut to "Experienced; helped newcomers"? (Shorter, but telegram prose: it lost grace and rhythm.) · "The samples were contaminated." Passive: wrong? (No: the doer is unknown or irrelevant. Break the default knowingly.)

Meaning: kill your darlings. The line you're proudest of is the one to interrogate hardest.

A writer loves the line "the moon, that pale accountant of the night." Does it serve the piece about a delayed bus? (Probably not: it's ornament.) What do you do with it? (Cut it; save it for another piece.) Is that a loss? (A small one, and the piece gets stronger.)

Form.

Sentence shapessimple · compound · complex · periodic (main clause last) · loose (main clause first, then elaboration) · fragment (deliberate)At the end of the lane stood the old house. (fronted, periodic) · The children saw to that. (short) · They told stories. (short)
Deadwood → cureredundant pairs · empty intensifiers · throat-clearing · deadweight phrases · hedging overload · clichésfirst and foremost → first · really important → important · due to the fact that → because · at this point in time → now
Weak verb + noun → strong verbmake a decision · reach a conclusion · give consideration to · conduct an investigation into · provide assistance to · put forward a suggestiondecide · conclude · consider · investigate · help · suggest / propose
Specific over generalexact nouns and verbs do the work of adjectives and adverbswalked slowly and tiredly → trudged · a dog → a whippet
Editing passes1 cut · 2 sharpen verbs · 3 vary rhythm · 4 check flow (old before new) · 5 read aloud · 6 kill your darlingsone kind of flaw per pass

Pronunciation. The read-aloud test is the pronunciation focus. Read long sentences in chunks, with a slight fall at each comma and breath at the dashes; let the voice drop and slow on the short sentence that follows ("Then everything ↘ changed."). A fragment needs a full stop in the voice: a clear pause before and after. English prose rhythm rewards sentences that end on a strong, stressed, concrete word ("…and we did not sleep"), not a weak preposition or abstraction. Read the Deadpan Wit version flatly: deadpan is a delivery as much as a style, and a smile in the voice gives the joke away.

Drill script
  1. Long, then short (choral, then individual): T reads a long accumulating sentence and pauses; learners say the short one in chorus. T: "The negotiations dragged on for months, through draft after weary draft, concession traded for concession until no one could recall who had wanted what." → Ss: "Then everything changed." T: "The rain fell." → Ss: "It fell hard." → T: "It fell all night," → Ss: "and we did not sleep."
  2. Cut it (individual, fast): T: "each and every" → S: "every." "first and foremost" → "first." "due to the fact that" → "because." "in the event that" → "if." "make an improvement to" → "improve." "at this point in time" → "now." "very unique" → "unique."
  3. Free the verb: T: "The committee will conduct an investigation into the matter and then reach a decision." → S: "The committee will investigate and decide." T: "We need to provide assistance to new members." → S: "We need to help new members."
  4. Three voices, one line (open pairs): T gives a fact ("The bus left two minutes early."); S1 says it as the Reporter, S2 as the Lyric Essayist, S3 as the Deadpan Wit. E.g.: "The 7:42 bus departed at 7:40." · "It slid away from us, lit and indifferent, two minutes before its promise." · "The bus, keen as ever, left without us."
Style & Voice Workshop — how to run it
  • The events (Set D, picture cards): a suitcase that went to the wrong city; an umbrella that turned inside out at the worst moment; a birthday cake that collapsed at an office party; a bus that came early and left without us; the last loaf at a Saturday market; a smoke alarm during a dinner for friends. Each card lists four fixed facts (time, place, what happened, how it ended). All three versions must keep the facts; voices change the telling, not the event.
  • The three core voices (voice cards): the Reporter (short declaratives, exact numbers and times, no opinion adjectives, third person or impersonal, past simple), the Lyric Essayist (first person, at least one periodic sentence and one image, reflective stance, a quiet closing line), the Deadpan Wit (understatement and irony from Module 2, flat delivery, the twist in the last few words, never explains the joke). 50–70 words each. The workbook frame has three boxes with a checklist under each.
  • Pastiche (stretch): six genre voice cards (the noir detective, the nature-documentary narrator, the sports commentator, the formal nineteenth-century letter-writer, the breathless friend on the phone, the minimalist). Early finishers write a fourth version in one of them. Pastiche imitates a manner, not anyone's actual sentences.
  • The Critique Circle (groups of 4, about 3 minutes per writer; the protocol card is in Set D): 0. Blind reading. Someone else reads the writer's three pieces aloud, in random order and unlabeled; the group names each voice and two features that gave it away. 1. What stayed with me. Each critic quotes one phrase that stayed with them and says why (no suggestions yet). 2. The writer's question. The writer, silent until now, asks one question about their own draft ("Did the Wit version land, or did I explain it?"). 3. Neutral questions. Critics may ask questions without opinions hidden inside ("Why did you choose slid?", not "Don't you think slid is too much?"). 4. One darling, one fix, with permission. "Would you like a suggestion?" If yes: one line that may be a darling (quote it) and one specific fix. The writer says "Thank you," not a defense.
  • Roles against dominance. The last critic speaks only after the others and may not repeat a point already made. The reader in step 0 is never the writer. Put the most dominant critic in the last-critic role.
  • What you're listening for as you circulate: voices that are really registers only (the Essayist that is merely formal), a Wit who uses sarcasm aimed at people rather than situations (redirect: irony at the situation, never at a person or a group), and precise, quoted critique. Note two examples of excellent critique language to praise in feedback.
ICQs before the workshop
How many versions do you write? (Three.) · Can you change what happened? (No: same facts, different telling.) · About how long is each version? (50–70 words.) · Who reads your pieces aloud in the circle? (Someone else.) · Before step 4, can critics make suggestions? (No: first what stayed with them, then questions.) · What do you say when you receive a suggestion? ("Thank you." No defending.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a two-voice version of the task: the Reporter and the Deadpan Wit only, 30–40 words each, with sentence starters on a strip (Reporter: "At 3:15 on Friday, … · … minutes later, …" · Wit: "It was, I'm told, … · I am pleased to report that …"). The picture event card is the prompt; the facts can be said aloud and written with a partner as scribe. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–3, Ex 3 items 1–2. The read-aloud test suits these learners perfectly: often their ear for rhythm is better than their eye.

Stretch

Ask strong learners to (1) write a fourth version in a genre voice (pastiche) and then a fifth that diverges from it: keep one habit of the imitated manner and replace the rest with their own; (2) write one paragraph that shifts voice on purpose once, and justify the shift; (3) run the six editing passes on one of their pieces and keep all drafts, noting which pass changed the most. Discuss with them why a voice is "easier for a reader to hear than for a writer to sense."

L1 watch-outs
  • Long sentences joined by commas: Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, Arabic, Greek and Russian prose tolerates long chains of clauses joined by commas. In English this becomes the comma splice ("The bus left early, we missed it, it was raining"). The fix is a stylistic choice: a full stop, a semicolon, or a subordinator, and each one changes the rhythm.
  • Fragments as "mistakes": learners trained in exam writing (and some teachers) treat every fragment as an error. Frame the deliberate fragment as a tool for effect in narrative, essay and speech, and still out of place in a report or an exam answer that asks for formal prose.
  • Intensifiers and redundant pairs: very unique, more perfect, return back, repeat again, free gift are common across many L1s (Spanish volver atrás, French répéter encore). Treat them as deadwood, not grammar.
  • Hedging overload: learners from high-context cultures (Japanese, Korean, Thai, Indonesian) may stack hedges for politeness ("it seems that perhaps it might possibly be…"). Keep one hedge, and keep the politeness in the relationship, not the claim.
  • Nominal style as "good style": German, Russian and Romance academic traditions reward long noun chains. Remind them of the C1 "Who does what?" test: free the verb.
  • Deadpan and irony across cultures: understatement reads as humor to some listeners and as sincerity to others. If a learner doesn't find the Wit funny, that's not a failure of English; ask what signals would make the irony visible in their language.
Homework

Online lesson 4.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_11.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and read them aloud for cadence; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. The online project (a 200-word draft edited in two passes, all three drafts kept) can use one of today's events or a new one. Workbook 4.2: Exercise 5 (voice fingerprints), a clean final version of your favorite voice from the workshop after the critique, "My new words" and the journal (what the critique circle told you about your own voice, and one habit of your writing you'd like to keep). Bring an idea to argue for in C12.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Having decided, we announced it publicly. · Q2 C Break the monotony with variety: fuse a couple into one longer sentence and drop in a short one for impact. · Q3 (new) B Every volunteer should arrive early if possible. Listening (Ex 1, Script 4.2-A): A (Reporter) = exact times and numbers, impersonal, short sentences, no figures · B (Lyric Essayist) = periodic opening sentence, metaphor and simile, a fragment ("Then the stairs."), first person, closing twist ("Nothing had been decided. Something, I think, had been.") · C (Deadpan Wit) = understatement and irony, exact times, first person, a comparison that is really a joke ("faster than we have reached any decision this year"), closing twist ("nowhere").

4.3 · C12

Review Project — Mastery in Action

90 min
A task-based review, not a new-language class

There is no new language today. The class is built around one combined task in three parts: a three-minute persuasive or analytical speech to a small group, two minutes of unscripted questions, and a 150–180-word written re-pitch of the same argument for a different reader (an audience card), which must fairly report the strongest challenge raised in the Q&A. The focus is orchestration (several layers from Modules 1–4 working together without showing off) and adaptation (the same substance, re-pitched for a new room). You listen and read with the teacher checklist (Set F). Language work comes from the tasks: a short focus-on-form clinic between them. The checklist results feed C13 (Progress Test 1): they are your spoken and written evidence for Modules 1–4 and tell you who goes to which catch-up station.

Objectives — learners can…
  • structure and deliver a three-minute speech with a clear arc (hook, turn, landing) and the spine from C10 (claim, grounds, concession, refutation)
  • orchestrate at least six deliberate layers: a mot juste, a register or tone shift, a fixed expression or strong collocation, a moment of implicature or understatement, a figure (metaphor or rhetorical figure), a stance marker, and an appeal, and keep them invisible in delivery
  • handle unscripted questions gracefully: paraphrase the question, answer "the question beneath the question," concede before countering, and adapt register to the questioner
  • re-pitch the argument in writing for a different audience and mode (register, density, idiom, rhetoric), reporting the strongest challenge fairly with a precise reporting verb
  • self-assess against the eight-dimension mastery rubric with specific evidence: two strengths and one refinement
Materials
  • Workbook 4.3 (Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the speech planner, the written re-pitch page, the mastery rubric self-assessment, the Module 4 "I can" checklist)
  • Slides 14–18 · Layer It! review game (Set G) + dice + counters · Mastery in Action topic, side and audience cards and challenge starters (Set E) · Teacher checklist: descriptors, class grid and score slips (Set F) · a phone timer per group
  • Listening scripts 4.3-A (the model speech and Q&A: a decision log for every team) and 4.3-B ("Two rooms," for the clinic or a C13 station) · optional: phones for recording

Left: the arc. HOOK a question, an image, an understatement → CLAIM My proposal is modest: → GROUNDS + WARRANT → TURN Admittedly,… That said,… → LANDING the restated claim, a figure, a quiet last line. Q&A: If I read your question rightly,… · That's a fair challenge. What I'd say is… · Let me answer the question beneath the question.

Center: six layers, one voice. M1 the mot juste · a register shift · a fixed expression (by and large, part and parcel) · M2 implicature / understatement (was, shall we say, memorable) · a metaphor (Memory is a generous storyteller and a poor accountant.) · M3 a stance marker (Frankly, To my mind, Admittedly) · cohesion (This is where…, For all that…) · M4 an appeal + a figure. Restraint: keep one layer in reserve.

Right: the re-pitch. Same substance, new room. EXPERT / WRITTEN: Latinate, hedged, dense, logos-forward, one controlled image. LAY / SPOKEN: plain, warm, short sentences, a story, "you." Report the challenge fairly: One colleague questioned whether… · It was objected that… Then respond. Misreading the room is the C2 error.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Layer It!10S–S (groups of 3–4)Review C1–C11 with the Layer It! board game (Set G). Roll, move, do the square aloud; the group judges with the cheat sheet. Squares cover all four modules (MOT JUSTE, REGISTER, FIXED, IMPLY, IRONY, IMAGE, STANCE, THREAD, DISTIL, FIGURE, FALLACY, CUT, VOICE, CONCEDE, RE-PITCH). Stop after 10 minutes; nearest the finish wins. Walk around and note which squares (which modules) cause trouble: early evidence for C13.
Orchestration and adaptation10T–S → S–SSlides 14–15. (a) Orchestration (5 min): the annotated extract ("freedom, like fire, warms only those who tend it"): pairs find five layers in one sentence before you reveal them. Point: the layers were there to be felt, not spotted. (b) Adaptation (5 min): one idea, two rooms (register, density, idiom, rhetoric, stance). Workbook Ex 4 item 1 in pairs. CCQs.
Model: listen7T–SRead Script 4.3-A (a hospital pharmacist proposes a one-line decision log for every team, then takes two questions; ask two confident learners to read the questioners). First listening: "What's the proposal? What's the strongest objection, and how does he answer it?" Second listening: Workbook Ex 1: note the hook, the turn and the landing, and tick the layers you hear. Slide 16 shows the Q&A moves.
Plan the speech10SSlide 17. Learners fill the speech planner (Workbook 4.3): notes, not a script, except the hook and the last line. Topic from Set E or their own idea within the module's topic territory; side assigned or chosen. The planner has a layer strip: each learner plans at least six layers across Modules 1–4, and marks one they will keep in reserve. Then two likely questions and a first answer to each. Walk around: check every plan has a turn (a genuine concession).
Speech circles + Q&A + checklist22S–S (groups of 4) · T–SGroups of 4. Each speaker speaks for up to 3 minutes (planner turned over; a glance is fine), then takes 2 minutes of questions: each listener asks one, using a challenge starter (Set E), and the challenger must paraphrase what they heard before asking. A timekeeper in each group. Listeners write on the back of their challenge card: the speaker's claim in one sentence and the strongest challenge I heard, and hand it to the speaker (it feeds the written re-pitch). You sit with one group at a time and score speaking S1–S5 and module ticks.
Focus-on-form clinic5T–SBoard four sentences you heard (no names): two to fix, two to upgrade. E.g., "We must discuss about this" → discuss this · "I will explain you why" → Let me explain why · "It's a no-brainer" → fine in the speech, re-pitch it for the board: The case would appear compelling · "It is very, very important" → one precise word: crucial / pivotal. Learners fix in pairs. Keep it short and positive.
Written re-pitch + peer check14S → S–SEach learner draws an audience card (Set E) that is different from the room they spoke to: a specialist panel, a hospital or museum board, a newspaper's general readers, a volunteers' newsletter, a skeptical manager, a class of teenagers. They write 150–180 words on the workbook page: the same argument, re-pitched for that reader, including the strongest challenge from the Q&A, reported fairly (One colleague questioned whether…) and answered. 10 minutes writing; 4 minutes of peer check with the re-pitch check on the page (Could this reader act on it? Is the register consistent for them? Is the challenge reported fairly? One line that fits this reader perfectly; one that belongs to the old room). Collect the pieces: you score W1–W4 after class.
Self-assessment + wrap7S → T–SLearners score themselves on the mastery rubric (Workbook 4.3, eight dimensions, 1–5) and write two strengths and one refinement, each with a specific example ("reached for placate where appease would have carried the overtone I wanted"). Then content first: "Which argument moved you most? Did anyone change your mind, even a little?" Two sentences from your notes, no names: one range example, one effect example. Celebrate: halfway through C2.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 4.3; record the full 5–6 minute version of the speech with three unprepared questions from a friend or family member (or recorded questions you draw from the challenge starters), then self-critique against the rubric. Tell learners plainly about C13: "Next class is Progress Test 1 on Modules 1–4, plus catch-up time. Today's checklist helps me plan that time for you." Hand out score slips in C13, not today.
Total9010 + 10 + 7 + 10 + 22 + 5 + 14 + 7 + 5
Hearing everyone

In 22 minutes of circles you can score about eight speakers well (two groups). Choose one of these for the rest, and tell the class which one before the circles: (a) learners record their speech and Q&A on a phone during the circles and play it for you during the C13 catch-up time; (b) the rest present to you at a station in C13; (c) in a large class, split the class: half speak in C12 while the other half act as challengers, and the second half speak in C13. The written re-pitches give you evidence for everyone. Anyone who prefers to speak only to you, not a group, may do so. That is a normal choice, not a problem.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review focus)

Meaning: orchestration.

"Freedom, like fire, warms only those who tend it." How many layers? (A simile, a mot juste in tend, an argument carried by an image, a balanced rhythm.) Did you notice them the first time? (Probably not: you felt them.) · A sentence with a metaphor, an idiom, a tricolon and a register drop all at once: stronger? (No: the layers fight. Restraint is mastery too.)

Meaning: adaptation.

"There is reason to suspect a degree of cognitive atrophy." Which room? (Expert, formal, written.) Same idea for a community meeting? ("Lean on it too hard and your own thinking goes soft.") What changed besides the words? (Density, rhetoric, the relationship to the listener.) · A perfect extended metaphor for a panel that wants data: error? (Not a grammar error: a misreading of the room, which at C2 is the real failure.)

Meaning: the question beneath the question.

"Isn't this just more bureaucracy?" What's the real worry? (That the log becomes the work, or that it will be used to blame people.) How does the speaker show he heard it? ("If I read your question rightly, the worry is…") Does he concede anything? (Yes: "That's a fair challenge.") Then? (He counters: one line, ninety seconds.)

Meaning: speech vs written re-pitch.

In the speech you said, "Honestly? It's a no-brainer." Your audience card says "hospital board." Keep it? (No: re-pitch it: "The case would appear compelling.") · Your card says "teenagers." Now? (Perhaps keep the energy, lose the cliché: "Honestly, this one's easy.") · How do you report the challenge? (Fairly, with a reporting verb: "One colleague questioned whether…") · Can you leave the challenge out if it was strong? (No: answering it is what makes the piece credible.)

Form: the review table.

Module 1 · The mot justeconnotation, force, specificity, semantic prosody (1.1) · register agility and tone (1.2) · binomials, strong collocation, fixed expressions (1.3)not "very tired" but spent · I regret to inform you… ↔ Look, I'll level with you… · by and large, part and parcel
Module 2 · Between the linesimplicature and presupposition (2.1) · irony, understatement, deadpan (2.2) · metaphor, sustained image, no mixed metaphors (2.3)She's… punctual. · Well, that could have gone better. · Her argument was a fortress.
Module 3 · Discoursestance adverbials and discourse markers (3.1) · cohesion across long texts, theme/rheme, signposting (3.2) · summary, paraphrase, synthesis, reporting verbs (3.3)Admittedly,… that said,… · Such objections, however, miss the point. · While X stresses…, Y counters that…
Module 4 · Style & rhetoricappeals, figures, concession and refutation, fallacies (4.1) · rhythm, economy, strong verbs, voice (4.2)not to spend more, but to own less · long, long… then short
Q&A movesIf I read your question rightly,… · That's a fair challenge; what I'd say is… · Let me answer the question beneath the question. · I take your point, and I'd go further… · Granted, and yet… · Let me leave you with this.If I read your question rightly, the worry is blame.
Reporting the challengequestioned whether · objected that · raised the concern that · countered that · conceded thatOne participant questioned whether the log would be kept up.

Pronunciation. Everything from C10 and C11 now at speaking pace: pause after the hook; slow down for the claim; contrastive stress in antithesis; a falling tone on rhetorical questions; a lower, slower voice for the landing. Understatement is delivered flat and slightly slower ("It was, shall we say, memorable."). In the Q&A, the paraphrase takes a level, unhurried tone (you're checking, not accusing); the concession takes a warm fall ("That's a fair challenge ↘"), then a fresh, higher start on the counter ("What I'd say is…").

Drill script
  1. Layer it (individual): T gives a flat line and names a layer; S adds it. T: "The meeting was bad. Understatement." → S: "The meeting was not, shall we say, our finest hour." T: "Memory isn't reliable. Metaphor." → S: "Memory is a generous storyteller and a poor accountant." T: "I think we should try it. Stance marker." → S: "To my mind, it's worth a trial." T: "It happens every month. Fixed expression." → S: "It's part and parcel of every month-end."
  2. Two rooms (open pairs): T gives an idea; S1 says it for experts, S2 for a community meeting. T: "Screens may affect children's sleep." → S1: "There is growing evidence that evening screen use may delay sleep onset in children." → S2: "Put the tablet away an hour before bed and they'll sleep better, by and large."
  3. Paraphrase, then answer: T asks a challenge ("Won't people just ignore the log?"); S paraphrases before answering: "If I read your question rightly, the concern is that it won't be kept up. That's fair. What I'd say is…"
  4. Report it fairly: T gives a challenge in direct speech ("This will just create more paperwork!"); S reports it for a written piece: "One colleague objected that the log would add to the paperwork." / "It was questioned whether the benefits would outweigh the administrative burden."
Mastery in Action — how to run it
  • Three minutes, not six. The online capstone asks for 5–6 minutes; in class, three minutes plus two minutes of questions per speaker keeps four speakers inside 22 minutes and still shows arc, layers and adaptation. The full version is homework (a phone recording).
  • Topics (Set E): every team should keep a decision log; handwriting still deserves a place in adult education; cities should protect "third places" (libraries, cafés, parks) as essential infrastructure; translation apps will never replace learning a language; museums should let visitors handle more of the collection (replicas); every professional should explain their work to a twelve-year-old once a year; silence (quiet carriages, quiet floors) is a public good worth protecting; online reviews do small businesses more harm than good; science should be taught through its failures as well as its successes; mentoring should count as real work in performance reviews. A learner's own topic is welcome if it stays in this territory. Side: for, against, or "yes, but" (a nuanced position), on the card or chosen.
  • The six layers. The planner's layer strip lists: a mot juste moment, a register or tone shift, a fixed expression or strong collocation, implicature or understatement, a figure (metaphor or rhetorical figure), a stance marker, an appeal. Six of the seven, planned; one in reserve. Learners mark them in the plan and aim to make them invisible in delivery.
  • Q&A. Each listener asks one question or challenge with a starter (Set E: "Isn't there a risk that…?" · "How would that work for…?" · "You said…, but surely…?" · "What would you say to someone who…?" · "Where does the evidence for… come from?"). The challenger paraphrases before asking ("So your claim is that… Isn't there a risk that…?"). The speaker concedes before countering at least once. The timekeeper stops the Q&A after two minutes, kindly. "That's beyond the scope of this proposal, but…" is allowed once.
  • Challenge notes feed the re-pitch. After each speech, listeners write the speaker's claim in one sentence and the strongest challenge they heard on the back of their card and hand it over. The speaker now has an outsider's version of the argument and the objection to report.
  • The written re-pitch is an adaptation, not a summary: the same argument for a different reader (the audience card), 150–180 words, with register, density, idiom and rhetoric chosen for that reader. It must report the strongest challenge fairly and answer it. For formal readers: no contractions, no idioms, hedged claims. For lay or young readers: plain, warm, concrete, still accurate.
  • If a Q&A gets heated, the timekeeper (or you) says: "Great challenge. One sentence to answer, then we move on." Challenge the idea, never the person.
The teacher checklist — how to score it (feeds C13)

Speaking: five criteria, 0–3 points each, /15. Writing: four criteria, 0–3 points each, /12. 0 = no response · 1 = not yet C2 (a strong C1 performance) · 2 = C2 · 3 = strong C2 (an educated, articulate speaker at their best). Score the whole spoken performance across the speech and the Q&A, and the written re-pitch separately. Tick the module boxes (M1–M4) when you hear or read a move from that module used well (precise, appropriate, natural). The full descriptors, the class grid and the score slips are in the classroom materials (Set F).

CriterionWhat 3 points looks like
S1 · ArchitectureA hook, a claim early, grounds with a warrant, a genuine concession and refutation, a landing that restates the claim with force; within time.
S2 · Range & precision (Modules 1–4)Six or more layers from at least three modules, used naturally; the mot juste; strong collocations; figures placed at the hinges, none showing off.
S3 · Accuracy & naturalnessGrammar and word choice so secure they never draw attention; no fossilized errors, or they are self-repaired.
S4 · Adaptation & interactionRegister fits the room and shifts on purpose; in the Q&A, paraphrases, answers the question beneath the question, concedes before countering, stays gracious.
S5 · Delivery & effectRhythm and stress serve the meaning (pauses, contrastive stress, falling rhetorical questions); the audience is moved or convinced, not merely informed.
W1 · Content & re-pitchThe argument fully re-pitched for the new reader; the strongest challenge reported fairly and answered; 150–180 words.
W2 · Architecture & cohesionA clear arc; reference, signposting and theme/rheme make it one text; the joins don't show.
W3 · Register, style & economyRegister, density, idiom and rhetoric consistent for this reader; no deadwood; strong verbs; a recognizable voice.
W4 · Accuracy & usageComplex sentences controlled; punctuation serves rhythm and structure; errors are rare and never affect meaning.

Feeding C13. Record each learner's scores and module ticks on the class grid. In C13, after Progress Test 1, send learners to a catch-up station for any module with no tick and for their lowest criterion: no M1 tick → precision station (connotation clines, register rewrites, binomials and collocations from your Module 1 materials); no M2 tick → pragmatics station (implicature, irony and metaphor tasks from your Module 2 materials); no M3 tick → discourse station (stance markers, a cohesion repair, a précis from your Module 3 materials); no M4 tick → rhetoric and style station (Workbook 4.1 Ex 3–5 and 4.2 Ex 3–4, the device-hunt grid); S4 or W1 ≤ 1 → re-pitch station (Workbook 4.3 Ex 4 + Script 4.3-B, "Two rooms"); S5 ≤ 1 → shadowing station (the 🔊 model sentences in online lessons 4.1–4.3); S3 or W4 ≤ 1 → editing station (the learner's own re-pitch, rewritten with your codes, plus their fossil from your fossil list). If Progress Test 1 has its own speaking and writing tasks, keep this grid anyway: it is your evidence of extended performance for Modules 1–4, and it lets you give each learner one kind, specific next step.

ICQs before the speeches and the re-pitch
Speeches: How long do you speak? (Up to three minutes.) · Can you read your notes? (Only a glance.) · How many layers do you plan? (Six, one in reserve.) · What must a challenger do before asking? (Paraphrase the claim.) · What do listeners write on the card? (The claim in one sentence and the strongest challenge.) · Do you have to speak to the group? (No: you can speak only to the teacher.)
Re-pitch: Who is your reader? (The one on my audience card.) · Same reader as the speech? (No: a different room.) · How many words? (150–180.) · Is it a summary of the speech? (No: the same argument, adapted for the new reader.) · What must you include? (The strongest challenge, reported fairly, and my answer.) · What do you do in the last four minutes? (Peer check with the re-pitch check.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a speech strip ("Have you ever…? · My proposal is simple: … · Consider this: … · Admittedly, … That said, … · So let me leave you with this: …") and a reply strip ("If I understand you, your worry is… · That's fair. What I'd say is…"). A two-minute speech with a hook, a concession and a landing, and three layers placed well, is a complete task. For the re-pitch, give a frame for the audience card ("I'm writing to propose… · The main reason is… · Some colleagues have asked whether… · My answer is… · I'd suggest we…") and accept 100–120 words. Score on the same checklist: a clear, well-judged short performance can earn full marks on S1, S4 and S5.

Stretch

Ask strong learners to place a layer from each module at a hinge (hook, turn, landing) and to keep two layers in reserve, then explain afterward which ones they withheld and why. In the Q&A, they answer at least one question in a deliberately different register from the speech, matched to the questioner. In the re-pitch, choose the audience card furthest from the original room (a speech for a board → a piece for teenagers), use one sustained metaphor that survives the change of reader, and add one concessive structure (For all that…, …). Homework: the 5–6 minute version with no notes.

L1 watch-outs
  • Over-formal by default: speakers of Romance languages, Greek and Russian may deliver the whole speech in the formal register and read the room as "always formal." Praise the control, then ask for one deliberate drop into warmth. At C2, a register shift on purpose scores; an unbroken formal wall does not.
  • Idioms in the written re-pitch: learners who have worked hard on fixed expressions may carry them into a formal piece. "Spoken: yes. Board report: re-pitch it."
  • "According to me…" (French selon moi, Spanish según yo, some Indian English): according to is for other sources. Offer To my mind…, In my view…, I'd argue that….
  • Irony that doesn't travel: understatement can be heard as sincerity by listeners from more literal traditions. In mixed groups, the speaker needs a tiny signal (a pause, a flat tone, "shall we say"). Discuss it as a C2 skill: making irony legible to a mixed audience.
  • Directness in the Q&A differs. Some learners counter without conceding ("No, this is not correct."); others concede so fully that their position disappears. Teach the middle path, concede, then counter, as a technique, not a personality change.
  • Fossils under pressure: in extended speech, the old errors return (discuss about, since three years, informations, I will explain you). Note them for the fossil list, not for the public clinic, unless they're common to the whole class.
Homework

Online lesson 4.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_12.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and deliver them as a master would; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. The capstone project: record the full 5–6 minute version of today's speech, handle three questions you didn't prepare (a friend's, or three drawn from the challenge starters), and self-critique against the mastery rubric, with two strengths and one refinement, each with an example. (The online project suggests "a topic you actually care about": stay in the module's topic territory, or keep today's.) Workbook 4.3: Exercises 2, 3 and 5, a clean copy of your re-pitch if you want more feedback, and the journal (the online letter to the person you were at the start of your English journey, or a letter to a learner who is just starting out). Review the Module 1–4 "I can…" lists before C13.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B Keep the substance, but re-pitch register, density, idiom and rhetoric for each room. · Q2 A Irony and understatement (with a stance hedge). · Q3 (new) B "The benefits would appear to outweigh the modest costs involved." Model speech (Ex 1, Script 4.3-A): hook = "Raise your hand if you have ever left a meeting certain that something had been decided…" · turn = "Admittedly, nobody went into healthcare to fill in forms. That said…" · landing = "A decision log won't make us wiser, but it will make us honest: about what we chose, why we chose it, and when we'll look again." Layers heard: mot juste (not a transcript but a ledger), register shift ("In plain English: we stop arguing about what we argued about."), fixed expression (by and large, part and parcel), understatement (was, shall we say, memorable), metaphor + antithesis (Memory is a generous storyteller and a poor accountant.), stance markers (Frankly, Admittedly, To my mind), ethos (eleven years in hospital pharmacies), logos (the ward trial, hedged: roughly half), tricolon (the landing). Q&A moves: paraphrase ("If I read your question rightly…"), concede ("That's a fair challenge"), counter (one line, ninety seconds), the question beneath the question (blame).

Closing the Module

End of C12: Module 4 wrap-up and the halfway point

Recap on slide 18, "persuade, sound like yourself, orchestrate": ethos, pathos, logos; not to spend more, but to own less; I grant that… but this overlooks… (persuade) → long, long… then short; cut the dead, keep the living; kill your darlings (sound like yourself) → six layers, one voice; the same substance for a new room; the question beneath the question (orchestrate). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you; it becomes a C13 catch-up station.

Watch for in Module 5: the rhetoric and style work returns in new forms. Lesson 5.2 (reading literature) needs the C11 vocabulary of style (periodic sentences, voice, the fragment) to talk about why a passage works; Lesson 6.1 (op-eds, reviews, articles) is persuasion in print, where the appeals, the concession and the fallacy check from C10 apply directly; and Lesson 8.2 (public speaking) builds the C10 delivery drill into full performance. Keep the device-hunt grid and the Critique Circle card: both work again in C17 and C24.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: naming the dominant appeal, identifying tricolon, anaphora, antithesis, chiasmus and the rhetorical question, rewriting a flat claim with a named figure, naming a fallacy and countering it, completing a concession and refutation, identifying periodic and loose sentences, cutting deadwood and freeing smothered verbs, rewriting a monotone passage for rhythm, and judging the adaptation of one message to two audiences.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 5 — Interpreting the Real World

Three workshop classes that take learners out of the textbook and into English as it is actually spoken, written and broadcast. First, varieties of English: the audible features that distinguish accents (rhoticity, the bath vowel, t-flapping, the glottal stop, rhythm), British/American vocabulary, spelling and grammar tells, and World Englishes (Indian, Nigerian, Singaporean, Philippine and more), with an honest, respectful conversation about accent attitudes. Second, literature and complex texts: a close-reading seminar on short public-domain passages (Austen, Dickens, Thoreau, Dickinson, Frost, Jerome) that moves from technique to effect to theme. Third, authentic media: slang and its currency, headlines, memes described in words, cultural allusions, fast connected speech and sarcasm. The module starts right after Progress Test 1, so C14 opens by handing the tests back in a warm, low-stress way.

3 lessons Lessons 5.1–5.3 Classes C14–C16 Weeks 7–8 90 min per class CEFR C2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer (in C14, the test return) → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → a demanding workshop → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-5-real-world.html. Print materials/module-5/classroom-materials.html before C14. It holds the flashcards (British/American picture pairs, feature cards, literary-technique cards, slang-currency cards), the new identity cards, the Varieties Lab transcripts, info-gap email and attitude-discussion cards, the Close-Reading Seminar text cards and role cards, the Media Decoding Lab station cards, the Decode It! board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons and an audio control for voice and speed, so learners can hear the dialogues and model sentences again at home.
  • These are workshops, not lectures. Each class protects one extended performance: the Varieties Lab (C14: feature detection on transcripts, a British/American info gap and a structured discussion of accent attitudes), the Close-Reading Seminar (C15: annotation, a role-based seminar and a written interpretation) and the Media Decoding Lab (C16: four stations on headlines, memes, allusions and slang). If you are running late, cut controlled practice (set it for homework), never the workshop.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. C2 learners met irony and humor in C2 2.2, figurative language in 2.3, register agility in 1.2 and rhetoric in 4.1. Don't re-teach any of it. This module applies those tools to material nobody simplified for them: an unfamiliar variety, a nineteenth-century sentence, a headline pun, a sarcastic line at speed. The C2 step is interpretive confidence: saying what something means, why, and on what evidence, while tolerating what remains uncertain.
  • Feedback on four things. In every workshop, take notes in four columns: precision (the exact word or term: rhotic, not "strong r"; theme, not "topic"; ironic, not "funny"), appropriacy (fit for the room: current slang in a report, "broken English" in a respectful discussion, a literary term dropped without an effect), naturalness (grammatical but not what an expert would say: "the poem wants to say," "the author makes the imagery of cold," "this slang is very actual") and effect (did the interpretation persuade? did the gloss help a newcomer?). Board two or three items per column, without names. At C2 the effect column is where learners grow most.
  • Near-native and fossilized at once. Many C2 learners are superb readers who have never heard Nigerian English, or fluent speakers who still say "the author wants to say" or "on the TV." Their fossilized errors are often invisible to them because nobody corrects fluent people. Correct them privately and specifically (a sticky note with the exact phrase and a better one), and praise precisely too: "juxtaposes was exactly right there" is useful; "great English!" is not.
  • Peer review, every class. Each workshop ends with a structured test: the "Evidence or assumption?" test (C14: every claim about a variety must point to a word, a structure or a described sound, never to a stereotype or "it just sounds like…"), the "Show me the words" test (C15: every interpretive claim must quote the text) and the "Gloss it for a newcomer" test (C16: could someone who doesn't share the culture understand the gloss, and is it accurate and respectful?). Reviewers always give one strength and one suggestion, in that order, and quote rather than summarize.
  • Dominant speakers. Seminars attract confident talkers, and literature classes attract learners who have read everything. Use the built-in roles: in C14 the fair-language monitor (listens for the loaded words: "heavy accent," "broken English," "proper English"); in C15 the evidence keeper (may only ask "Where does it say that?") or the discussion leader who speaks last; in C16 the newcomer (plays someone who doesn't share the reference and must honestly say whether a gloss worked). Say it kindly: "You read closely. I need that skill on the evidence card this round." In whole-class feedback, use "a new voice first."
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spelling (recognise, colour, humour, labelled, practise) and, in 5.3, British informal words (quid, flat, gutted, knackered). Teach American forms, accept British forms, and in C14 make the point explicitly: consistency within one document matters more than which variety you choose. The quoted literary texts keep their original spelling (neighbourhood, traveled): don't "correct" a public-domain quotation.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Hear it → read it → decode it. In C14 learners train their ears on variety: they decompose an unfamiliar English into features, recognize that every variety is complete and valid, and practice talking about accents without locating the fault in the speaker. In C15 they slow down and read a demanding text for what it does, not only what it says, and defend a reading with the words on the page. In C16 they speed back up: slang, headlines, memes, allusions and sarcasm at native speed, with strategies for the part that sails past. The skill underneath all three is the same: notice the signal, name the feature, infer the meaning, and stay comfortable with what you can't yet catch.

Accent, identity and respect: the rules for this module

Accent is personal. Some of your learners have been corrected, mocked, passed over at work or asked "Where are you really from?" because of how they sound; some speak an outer-circle variety (Indian, Nigerian, Philippine, Singaporean English) as a first or home language and have been told it isn't "real" English. So: (1) Learners are never asked to perform, demonstrate, imitate or be assessed on their own accent, or anyone else's, and no one is asked to "say it in your accent" or to be the class example of a variety. (2) You don't perform accents either. Read every transcript in your own natural voice; the features are in the words and grammar, and the sound features are described in brackets, not acted. Mock accents slide into caricature fast. For real sound, use authentic recordings of speakers talking about their own lives (for example, the International Dialects of English Archive, dialectsarchive.com), chosen and previewed by you. (3) Discuss attitudes, not people in the room. The attitude cards are general statements; personal stories are welcome but always optional, and anyone can say "Pass." (4) Pronunciation drills in this module are about intonation, stress and connected speech in the learner's own voice (reading a model sentence, a sarcastic line, a fast-speech reduction), never about acquiring a different accent.

Content choices: literature, media and slang

The C15 seminar uses six short, public-domain passages (all published before 1929 and in the US public domain) chosen to be rich but gentle: comedy, irony, fog, hope, a walk in the woods. Avoid swapping in texts about violence, war, bereavement or abuse without warning; the online lesson's own passage is about grief, so offer it as optional homework, not as the seminar text. In C16 every slang item, headline and meme is invented or generic and no slang is offensive; don't take requests to "explain the rude ones" in class (offer a quiet word or a good dictionary instead). Where a slang term comes from a specific community, most often African American English or LGBTQ+ ballroom culture, name the origin respectfully; understanding a community's words is fine, mocking or performing them is not. No headline or meme topic touches party politics, religion or immigration policy.

Before the Module

  • Mark Progress Test 1 before C14. Write a short, positive comment on every paper: one strength + one thing to refine, phrased as an upgrade, not a deficit ("Your stance markers are precise; next, vary your cohesive devices across paragraphs"). Collect 4–5 common errors from different papers for the C14 board (no names). Note which learners scored below about 60% so you can offer a quiet 1:1 chat at the break.
  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): British/American picture pairs and the feature cards (C14); literary-technique cards (C15); slang-currency, allusion and fast-speech cards (C16). Cut them before C14.
  • For C14: one set of Varieties Lab transcripts (8 cards, 2 per group of 3–4), one Feature Detective grid per group, the info-gap email (A/B) for each pair and one set of attitude cards per group.
  • For C15: one text card per group of 3 (six texts in three pairs), a set of seminar role cards per merged group of 6, and highlighters or two colors of pen.
  • For C16: four Media Decoding stations (headlines, memes in words, allusion hunt, slang currency) set up on four tables, plus a phone timer. The Decode It! board game (1 board per group, 1 die, counters) is for early finishers in C16 and the C17 warmer.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking. This module's personal prompts ("which English shaped yours?", "a show you watch") can touch identity and history; a card is always an equal option.
  • Optional but powerful: preview and queue two or three short authentic recordings of speakers from different varieties talking about ordinary topics (an archive clip, a university lecture, a cooking segment), each under a minute. Use them in C14 after the transcripts, for listening, never for imitation.
  • Check the slang. The "current" slang column in C16 was current when this kit was written. Before C16, spend five minutes checking which terms have already dated; that drift is itself the lesson, and learners love hearing "this one is already a bit cringe."

5.3C16 · wk 8Authentic media: slang register and currency (dated / established / current); cultural references and allusion (myth, history, film, idiomatic shorthand); headlinese (dropped articles, present for past, to + infinitive for future, short punchy verbs, puns); memes described in words; fast connected speech (elision, assimilation, linking, weak forms, gonna / whatcha / didja); sarcasm and tone over text; coping strategiesdecode invented headlines, text-described memes and allusions and gloss them accurately for a newcomer, sort slang by register and currency and choose the right register for a context, transcribe fast-speech reductions, and read sarcasm from tone and situation

Lesson Plans

5.1 · C14

Varieties of English — Accents, Dialects & Global Englishes

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • reflect on their Progress Test 1 results and set one personal goal
  • describe the three circles of English and explain why outer-circle varieties (Indian, Nigerian, Singaporean, Philippine English) are established standards, not learner errors
  • name and recognize the main sound features that distinguish varieties (rhotic / non-rhotic, the broad or flat bath vowel, t-flapping, the glottal stop, stress-timed / syllable-timed rhythm) and use them to decompose an unfamiliar voice
  • recognize British/American vocabulary pairs and false friends (first floor, pants, table a motion, quite good, momentarily, homely), spelling patterns (-our/-or, -re/-er, -ise/-ize, -ce/-se, -ll-/-l-) and grammar tells (gotten, have got, collective-noun agreement, present perfect vs simple past), and keep one variety consistently in a document
  • talk about accents and accent attitudes precisely and respectfully, reframing loaded language ("heavy accent," "broken English") and applying the intelligibility principle and accommodation
Materials
  • Marked Progress Test 1 papers (in folders or face-down) · sticky notes · Workbook "My goal after Progress Test 1" box
  • Workbook 5.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Varieties Lab frame, self-check)
  • Slides 3–9 · British/American picture flashcards and feature cards · Varieties Lab transcripts (8) + Feature Detective grids · info-gap email A/B · attitude cards · New identity cards · (optional) 2–3 previewed authentic recordings
  • Listening scripts 5.1-A (the delayed train) and 5.1-B (dictation)

Left: the sound map. r: rhotic (car, hard: US, Canada, Scotland, Ireland) vs non-rhotic ("cah": southern England, Australia) · bath: broad "ah" (S. England, Australia) vs flat a (US, N. England) · t: flap ("wader": US) vs glottal stop ("wa'er": London) vs crisp · rhythm: stress-timed (bouncy) vs syllable-timed (even: India, Nigeria, Singapore). Listen for 2–3 features, not "the accent."

Center: words, spelling, grammar. lift/elevator · flat/apartment · queue/line · first floor ≠ first floor · pants ≠ pants · table it (UK: discuss now / US: postpone) · colour/color · centre/center · organise/organize · travelled/traveled · Have you got…? / Do you have…? · the team are / the team is · Have you eaten yet? / Did you eat yet? · gotten (US). One document = one variety.

Right: World Englishes and respect. prepone · do the needful · out of station (India) · go-slow · no wahala (Nigeria) · comfort room · open the light (Philippines) · can or not? · lah (Singapore) · arvo · heaps (Australia). ✘ "heavy accent," "broken English" → ✔ "an accent I'm less familiar with," "a variety I need more exposure to." Intelligibility, not imitation.
