Total907 + 5 + 10 + 10 + 5 + 6 + 8 + 29 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: theme vs subject. The subject is what happens; the theme is the concern the text keeps returning to. A good theme statement is specific, arguable and supported.

"The subject is a fishing trip; the theme is the silence between father and son." Which one could you photograph? (The subject.) Which one could a reader argue about? (The theme.) · "The poem is about the sea." Theme or subject? (Subject.) · "The poem is about love." Arguable? (Not really: it's too broad. Everyone agrees, and nobody learns anything.) · "The poem treats memory as something that both preserves and distorts." Theme? (Yes: specific, and someone could disagree.)

Meaning: narrator vs author; unreliability; ironic voice. The voice telling the story is constructed. An unreliable narrator reveals more than they intend; an ironic narrative voice says one thing and means another.

"I was perfectly calm — anyone would have done as I did, and only a coward would say otherwise." Was he calm? (Probably not.) What gives it away? (He protests too much; he attacks imaginary critics.) Is the author a coward-hater? (We don't know: the author built the voice so we'd see through it.) · Austen: "It is a truth universally acknowledged, that a single man in possession of a good fortune must be in want of a wife." Is it really universal? (No.) Who actually wants the marriage? (The families with daughters.) So is the sentence sincere? (No: it's ironic.)

Meaning: technique → effect → theme. Naming a device is not interpretation. The interpretation is what it does, and why that matters.

"There is imagery of cold." Interpretation? (Not yet: it's only a label.) What's missing? (The effect, and the theme it serves.) · "The cold imagery makes the reunion feel like a funeral, which foreshadows the estrangement." How many steps? (Three: technique, effect, theme.)

Form.

Strip → spine → restoreBracket the extras: [relative clauses] [participle phrases] [asides between dashes or commas] → find subject + verb → put the pieces back one by one → paraphrase in one plain sentenceI went to the woods [because I wished to live deliberately, …] → I went to the woods + why
Inversionadjective / adverbial first, then verb, then subject (poetry and formal prose)Sore must be the storm = The storm must be sore (severe).
Verbless sentencesnoun phrases as sentences for effect: stillness, a list, a camera panningFog everywhere. Fog up the river…
Archaismold words, old meanings; infer from context and modern relativeswanted wear = lacked wear · hence = from now · fancy = think
Discussion framesthe passage evokes… · the image juxtaposes X and Y · this detail foreshadows… · the tone shifts from… to… · the effect is to… · which suggests / reinforces… · I'd read it slightly differently: …The repeated "Fog" creates a sense of suffocation, which suggests a city lost in its own confusion.

Pronunciation. Reading literature aloud is a skill in itself; this is about pausing and stress, never accent. For verse, pause at punctuation and dashes, not at every line end (Dickinson's commas in "Yet, never, in extremity" are breaths). For long prose sentences, chunk at the clause boundaries you found when you stripped the sentence, and let the voice rise through the suspended part and fall on the landing. Word stress in the metalanguage: ALlusion → alLUsion (second syllable), Irony, SYMbolism, juxTApoSItion, foreSHAdowing, inTERpreTAtion, MOtif → moTIF (both are heard; American usually stresses the second).

Drill script
  1. Name it (fast): T: "an early hint of what's to come" → S: "foreshadowing." T: "a glancing reference to a myth" → "allusion." T: "a gap between what's said and what's meant" → "irony." T: "two things side by side for contrast" → "juxtaposition." T: "a recurring image" → "a motif."
  2. Build the chain (card up, individual): T holds up IMAGERY + "dust, a slowing clock" → S: "The imagery of dust and a slowing clock evokes stillness, which suggests a life that has stopped with her." T: IRONY + Austen → "The opening is heavy with irony: the 'universal truth' is really the families' wish, which exposes marriage as a matter of money." T: JUXTAPOSITION + "guest / his own life" → "The simile juxtaposes 'guest' and 'his own life,' and the effect is to make the familiar feel strange."
  3. Push back politely (pairs): A makes a claim from the text cards; B responds with one of three frames: "I'd read it slightly differently: …" · "That's persuasive, but what about the line…?" · "Where exactly does it say that?"
Close-Reading Seminar — how to run it
  • The texts (all public domain; see materials Set D): A1 Austen, Pride and Prejudice (1813), opening · A2 Jerome K. Jerome, Three Men in a Boat (1889), the British Museum · B1 Dickens, Bleak House (1853), the fog · B2 Thoreau, Walden (1854), "I went to the woods…" · C1 Dickinson, "Hope is the thing with feathers" (published 1891) · C2 Frost, "The Road Not Taken" (1916), stanzas 2–4. Six groups of 3. With fewer learners, use pair A or C only.
  • Round 1, Annotate (10 min): read the text three times (surface; slowly for word choice; aloud, quietly, for rhythm). Annotation key: underline images · circle key words and repeated words · [bracket] interruptions in long sentences · → arrows from pronouns to referents · ? in the margin for anything uncertain. Then answer the four prompts on the card and write, on the worksheet, one arguable theme sentence and one technique → effect → theme chain with a quotation.
  • Round 2, Seminar (14 min): merge paired groups (A1 + A2, etc.). Hand out role cards: discussion leader (opens with a question, keeps turns fair, speaks last with a summary), evidence keeper (may only ask "Where does it say that?" and "Which word?"), alternative reader (must offer one different, defensible reading), connector (links the two texts: "Both narrators…"). Remaining learners are contributors. Group 1's text: 1 min read aloud + 6 min discussion; then Group 2's.
  • Round 3, "Show me the words" peer review (5 min): each group gives its theme sentence and chain to the other group in the merge. Reviewers check: Is the theme arguable (could someone disagree)? Does every claim quote specific words? Is there an effect, not just a label? One strength, one suggestion, quoting the text.
  • Model answers for every text are in the materials (for you, folded back) and in the answer key. Accept any reading the words support. "Two well-supported readings can coexist" is a core message of this class.
ICQs before the seminar
In Round 1, how many times do you read? (Three.) What do you write at the end? (One arguable theme and one chain.) What must the chain include? (A quotation, an effect and the theme.) · In Round 2, can the evidence keeper give opinions? (No: only "Where does it say that?") Who speaks last? (The discussion leader.) What does the alternative reader do? (Offers a different reading, with evidence.) · Is there one right answer? (No, but every answer needs the words.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give learners who read more slowly the Dickinson text (short lines, everyday words) or the Frost text, with the three glossed words (abash, extremity, wanted wear) in the margin. Offer a sentence-frame strip: "The ___ evokes ___, which suggests ___." In the seminar, the evidence-keeper role suits them well: asking "Where does it say that?" is powerful and needs no long turn. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 3 items 1–2. Target: one chain, spoken aloud with a quotation.

Stretch (C2+)

Free indirect discourse: in Austen, whose voice is the "truth universally acknowledged"? The narrator's, society's or Mrs. Bennet's? Ask strong learners to find the exact word where irony enters (property). With Frost, ask them to argue both readings (the celebratory popular one and the ironic one) and decide which the text supports better, citing "really about the same" and "with a sigh." Or: write a 100-word pastiche of the Dickens fog paragraph about their own city's weather, using verbless sentences and anaphora, then explain one effect.

L1 watch-outs
  • "The correct interpretation": learners schooled in traditions where the teacher or the textbook provides the meaning (common in many exam systems) may wait for your answer. Hold back: "What do the words let you say?" Praise defended disagreement.
  • Narrator = author: common everywhere, but reinforced where literature is taught mainly through the author's biography. Keep asking, "Does the text endorse this voice, or invite us to judge it?"
  • "Irony" as coincidence or bad luck: from everyday English and many L1 calques. Keep the definition tight: a gap between what is said and what is meant (or expected).
  • "The author wants to say" (Spanish quiere decir, Russian хочет сказать, German will sagen): upgrade to suggests, implies, invites us to see. Also "the poem speaks about" (Spanish habla de, Portuguese fala de): the poem explores / is concerned with.
  • Periodic sentences: speakers of verb-final languages (Japanese, Korean, Turkish, German subordinate clauses) are often comfortable waiting for the verb; learners from SVO languages may panic. Strip → spine helps both.
  • False friends in literary terms: Spanish argumento / French argument / Italian argomento (= plot or topic, not argument); French caractère (≠ character in a story, which is personnage); Spanish/Portuguese novela (can mean a TV soap, but also a novel). Check the word, not the look.
Homework

Online lesson 5.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_14.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back with clear chunking; do Exercises 1–2, the Quick Quiz and the "Close Reading" project (150–250 words; any seminar text, or a short public-domain poem or passage you choose; the online passage about a house after a loss is also available if you prefer it). Workbook 5.2: Exercise 5 (Frost), "My new words" and the journal (a passage that resisted an easy reading, and what opened up when you slowed down).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B The failure of communication within a family · Q2 A An unreliable narrator · Q3 (new) B It's ironic: the "universal truth" is really the neighborhood families' wish to marry their daughters to a rich man. Seminar contribution (Ex 1): 1 extended metaphor · 2 evokes · 3 juxtaposes · 4 the effect is to · 5 inversion · 6 the tone shifts · 7 I'd argue.

5.3 · C16

Decoding Authentic Media — Slang & Cultural Reference

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • classify slang and informal expressions by register (where you can use it) and currency (dated, established, current), explain why slang dates fast, and choose plain English when in doubt
  • notice that a cultural reference is present (the "why did that land?" reflex) and decode common allusions from myth, history, literature and film (Achilles' heel, a Trojan horse, meet your Waterloo, Catch-22, Groundhog Day)
  • decode headlinese (no articles, present simple for past, to + infinitive for future, short verbs like axe, probe, back, slam, puns and double meanings) and memes described in words
  • recognize the processes of fast connected speech (elision, assimilation, linking, weak forms, reductions: gonna, wanna, whatcha, didja, lemme, dunno, shoulda) and catch the load-bearing words
  • read sarcasm from delivery and situation (tone over text), and use coping strategies: ride the gist, use context, subtitles as a bridge, rewind, look up selectively, tolerate ambiguity
Materials
  • Workbook 5.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Media Decoding Lab frame, self-check, "Look back at my goal" box)
  • Slides 15–18 · Slang-currency, allusion and fast-speech flashcards · Media Decoding station cards (4 stations) · phone timer · Decode It! board game · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 5.3-A (the coffee-shop run-in) and 5.3-B (fast-speech dictation + sincere or sarcastic?)

Left: slang has a date and a room. DATED: groovy (1960s–70s) · rad (1980s) · da bomb (1990s) · on fleek (2010s) → ESTABLISHED: ghost someone · the vibe · binge-watch · FOMO · a hot take · (UK) gutted, knackered → CURRENT (check!): no cap · it slaps · lowkey / highkey · the ick · main-character energy. Understand widely. Use sparingly. Never in a report.

Center: references. Achilles' heel · a Trojan horse · Pandora's box · the Midas touch · meet your Waterloo · cross the Rubicon · Catch-22 · Big Brother · Kafkaesque · Groundhog Day · "Houston, we have a problem." Headlinese: CITY BACKS BIKE PLAN = The city has backed… · MAYOR TO UNVEIL… = will unveil · CHEF GRILLS MAYOR = pun.

Right: speed and tone. What are you going to do? → Whatcha gonna do? · Did you see it? → Didja see it? · Let me know → Lemme know · I don't know → I dunno · should have → shoulda · next day → "nex day" · ten bikes → "tem bikes." Sarcasm: "Oh, great. Another meeting." Words vs situation disagree → trust the situation and the tone.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: one-line readings7S–SReview C15. Board three lines: "Fog everywhere." · "Sore must be the storm." · "I shall be telling this with a sigh." Pairs name one technique in each and give its effect in one sentence ("The verbless sentence creates stillness, which…"). Two pairs share. Collect or glance at the close-reading homework.
Lead-in: why did that land?5T–SSlide 15. Board an invented headline: CHEF GRILLS MAYOR OVER FOOD-TRUCK RULES. "Did the chef cook the mayor? (No!) What happened? Why is it funny? What else is missing from this sentence?" Elicit: pun (grill = cook / question hard), no articles, present tense for a past event. "Today: the English nobody simplified for you."
Slang: register & currency10T–S → S–SSlide 15. The three bands (dated / established / current) with the slang cards. Pairs place six cards on a timeline and say where each could be used (text to a friend, team chat, email to a manager, report). Name community origins respectfully where relevant (no cap and lowkey spread from African American English). CCQs. Key message: understand widely, use sparingly.
References & headlines8T–SSlide 16. Allusion cards: learners give the meaning and, if they know it, the story behind it. Then the four rules of headlinese on three invented headlines. Then one meme described in words ("a cartoon dog sits calmly with a cup of coffee in a burning room and says 'This is fine'"): "When would someone share this? What's the attitude?" CCQs.
Fast speech + drill8T–S → SSlide 17. The five processes, each with one example read naturally, then slowly. Drill (see Drill script): full form → fast form and back, choral then individual. Then sarcasm: you read "Oh, great. Another meeting." sincerely and sarcastically; learners say which is which and what signaled it (pitch, stress, lengthening). Learners try the sarcastic reading in pairs: intonation practice in their own voice.
Listening: the coffee-shop run-in7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 5.3-A at natural speed with reductions (two voices, or with a confident learner). First: "Who did Jordan run into, and how did Jordan really feel?" Second: learners answer the six decoding questions. Pairs compare.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–4 alone (headlines, slang register, allusions), then pairs. Board-check disagreements only. Ex 5 (fast-speech full forms) is homework unless a pair finishes early.
Freer: Media Decoding Lab22S–S → T–SSlide 18. Four stations on four tables; groups of 3–4 rotate every 4 minutes (16 min): 1 Headlines (rewrite as full sentences, find the pun), 2 Memes in words (what's the joke, what's the attitude, when would you share it?), 3 Allusion hunt (a short invented article with five allusions to find and gloss), 4 Slang & register (sort by currency; rewrite a friend's text as an email to a manager). At each station, the group writes one gloss for a newcomer on the station sheet. Last 6 min: the "Gloss it for a newcomer" test: each group reads the glosses left at its final station and checks: accurate? understandable without the culture? respectful? One strength, one suggestion. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Precision: "it's a joke" → "it's a pun on grill." Appropriacy: current slang in the manager email. Naturalness: "This slang is very actual" → "current / up-to-date"; "on the TV" → "on TV." Effect: praise the clearest newcomer gloss.
Module wrap-up & goal check5T–S · SSlide 19: "hear it, read it, decode it." Learners tick the three "I can…" lists and read their Progress Test goal again in the "Look back at my goal" box (see Closing the Module). Private; no one reports.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 5.3 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below).
Total907 + 5 + 10 + 8 + 8 + 7 + 8 + 22 + 5 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: slang has a register and a date. Slang marks belonging, mood and generation more than information. It is the fastest-changing layer of English: that's why textbooks avoid it and media is full of it.

"No cap, that album slaps." Formal or informal? (Very informal.) Would you write it in an email to a client? (No.) Is it wrong? (No: it's right for a friend, wrong for a report.) · "That's groovy." Current? (No: 1960s–70s; now it's retro or ironic.) · Is ghost still slang? (Barely: it's near-standard now, even in newspapers.) · A 45-year-old accountant says "It's giving main-character energy" in a meeting. What's the risk? (It may sound forced or stale; plain English is safer.)

Meaning: references do invisible work. The words are clear; the meaning depends on shared knowledge. The skill is noticing a reference is there, then deciding whether to look it up.

"The new policy turned out to be a Trojan horse." Is there a horse? (No.) What's the meaning? (Something that looks like a gift but hides a threat.) Where does it come from? (Greek myth: the siege of Troy.) · "Every day at this job is Groundhog Day." Meaning? (Every day is exactly the same.) Source? (A 1993 film.) · Everyone laughs and you don't know why. What probably happened? (A reference or an in-joke passed.) Should you stop the conversation? (Usually not: note it, ride the gist, look it up later.)

Meaning: sarcasm lives in the delivery.

The car breaks down. "Well, this is going great." Going great? (No: the opposite.) How do you know? (The situation, and the flat or exaggerated tone.) Can you look sarcasm up in a dictionary? (No.) · "Oh, brilliant, a 7 a.m. meeting — said no one ever." What does "said no one ever" do? (It marks the first part as sarcastic.)

Form.

Headlineseno articles or be · present simple for the past · to + infinitive for the future · past participle = passive · short verbs: axe (cut), back (support), probe (investigate), slam (criticize), vow (promise), bid (attempt), quit (resign), eye (consider) · punsLIBRARY FINES AXED = Library fines have been abolished. · MAYOR TO UNVEIL PARK PLAN = The mayor will unveil…
Fast speechelision (sounds dropped) · assimilation (sounds change) · linking · weak forms · reductionsnex(t) day · tem bikes · a-napple · fish 'n' chips · gonna, wanna, gotta, whatcha, didja, wouldja, lemme, dunno, shoulda, kinda
Asking about a referencepolite, low-stakes ways to askSorry, is that a reference to something? · I think I missed the joke; what's the story there? · Is that from a show?
Coping strategiesride the gist · use context and reactions · subtitles as a bridge · rewind · look up selectively · tolerate ambiguity"I didn't catch every word, but the gist was…"

Pronunciation. Recognition first. Learners don't need to say "whatcha," but they must hear it. If they want to use reductions, gonna / wanna / kinda in casual speech are safe; whatcha, didja are fine but optional; never write them in formal text. Sarcastic intonation (in the learner's own voice): a flat, low pitch ("Great.") or an exaggerated rise-fall with lengthening ("Oh, grea-a-at"), stress on the "wrong" word ("That's just what I needed"). Practice both sincere and sarcastic readings of the same line so learners feel the contrast; this is intonation, not accent.

Drill script
  1. Hear it fast (you say the fast form, learners give the full form, choral then individual): "Whatcha gonna do?" → "What are you going to do?" · "Didja see it?" → "Did you see it?" · "Lemme know." → "Let me know." · "I dunno." → "I don't know." · "You shoulda told me." → "You should have told me." · "Wouldja mind?" → "Would you mind?"
  2. Headline it: T: "The city council has approved the new bike lanes." → S: "COUNCIL BACKS BIKE LANES." T: "The museum will open a new wing." → "MUSEUM TO OPEN NEW WING." T: "Scientists are investigating a strange signal." → "SCIENTISTS PROBE MYSTERY SIGNAL."
  3. Name the allusion: T: "Math was always her ___ heel." → S: "Achilles'." T: "Opening that file was like opening ___ box." → "Pandora's." T: "He can't get the job without experience, and can't get experience without the job: it's a ___." → "Catch-22."
  4. Sincere or sarcastic? T reads "Oh, perfect." / "Thanks a lot." / "I just love waiting." with either intonation → S: "Sincere!" / "Sarcastic!" and name the cue.
Media Decoding Lab — how to run it
  • Set up four stations before class (materials Set E). Each station has a card with its task and a station sheet where every group writes one "gloss for a newcomer": a one- or two-sentence explanation that someone new to the culture could understand.
  • Groups of 3–4 with roles that rotate at every station: reader, scribe, time-keeper and newcomer (plays a person who has never met the reference and says honestly whether the gloss makes sense). The newcomer role is ideal for a dominant speaker: they may only ask questions.
  • Station 1, Headlines: six invented headlines. Rewrite three as full sentences; identify the pun or double meaning in two.
  • Station 2, Memes in words: four memes described in text. For each: what's the joke, what's the attitude (irony? self-mockery? frustration?), and when would someone share it?
  • Station 3, Allusion hunt: a short invented newsletter article with five allusions. Find, gloss and give the source of each.
  • Station 4, Slang & register: sort twelve cards into dated / established / current, then rewrite a slang-heavy text to a friend as a two-sentence email to a manager.
  • Rotation: 4 minutes per station. Ring a bell at 3:30. Groups may not finish every item; that's fine (ride the gist!).
  • "Gloss it for a newcomer" test (6 min): at its final station, each group reads the glosses the other groups left. Check: accurate? understandable without the culture? respectful (no mockery of the community a word comes from)? One strength, one suggestion.
ICQs before the Media Decoding Lab
How long at each station? (Four minutes.) Must you finish everything? (No.) What do you leave at every station? (One gloss for a newcomer.) What does the newcomer do? (Asks questions, says honestly if the gloss works.) At Station 4, who is the email for? (A manager: no slang.) At the end, what three things do you check in a gloss? (Accurate, understandable, respectful.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Headlines and memes are short, which helps. Give learners who read slowly the headline-rules strip (no articles · present = past · to = future · short verbs) and let them focus on Stations 1 and 4. The allusion article is the densest text: pre-teach two allusions from it (Achilles' heel, Trojan horse) during the reference stage. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 5 items 1–5. Target: two headlines rewritten as sentences and one clear newcomer gloss.

Stretch (C2+)

Why slang dates: ask strong learners to explain the life cycle (in-group → spread → mainstream → adopted by brands and parents → "cringe" → retro) with one example. Or: write three headlines of their own for real, light news from the week (workplace, science, sport, arts) with at least one pun, and explain each pun. Or: find a reference in a show they watch and write a 60-word gloss for a classmate from a different country, then test it on them. Link to C2 2.2: which comic mode (deadpan, understatement, satire, wordplay) does each meme use?

L1 watch-outs
  • Dated textbook idioms as "cool English": many learners were taught "It's raining cats and dogs," "the bee's knees," "groovy" as living idioms. Place them honestly on the timeline; praise the curiosity, not the usage.
  • Sarcasm read literally: learners from communication cultures where sarcasm is rare in public or signaled differently (for example, some East and Southeast Asian contexts), and speakers of tonal languages (Mandarin, Cantonese, Vietnamese, Thai), where pitch carries word meaning, may not attach attitude to pitch in English. Contrast the two readings explicitly.
  • British understatement ("not bad at all," "a bit of a problem") heard as literal: link to C2 2.2 and 3.3 in C1. In media, a flat "Brilliant." is often the harshest line in the scene.
  • Reductions in writing: learners who pick up gonna, wanna, kinda from media sometimes write them in emails. Clear rule: recognize everywhere, write only in texts to friends, never in formal writing.
  • "Actual" = current (Spanish actual, French actuel, German aktuell, Russian актуальный): "This slang is very actual" → current, up to date, topical. Also "on the TV" → on TV, and "in the internet" → on the internet.
  • Allusion sources: allusions to Greek myth and Shakespeare may be familiar to learners from European schooling and unfamiliar to others; allusions to American film and TV depend on exposure, not education. Make it clear that not knowing a reference is never a sign of low English.
Homework

Online lesson 5.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_15.html. Listen to the fast exchange and the five model sentences with 🔊, first at normal speed and then slowed down; do Exercises 1–2, the Quick Quiz and the "Decode a Real Clip" project (3–5 minutes of media you enjoy: once without subtitles, once with; five items looked up and classified as slang, reference, idiom or fast speech). Workbook 5.3: Exercise 5 (fast speech), "My new words," the journal, and the "Look back at my goal" box. Note that the online dialogue uses British informal words (quid, flat); the kit's version uses American ones (bucks, apartment): a nice variety check from C14.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C The opposite: things are going badly (sarcasm) · Q2 B Note that a reference likely passed, keep following the gist, and look it up later · Q3 (new) B "The new schedule is working really well for the team." The coffee-shop run-in (Ex 1): 1 Marcus, at a coffee shop · 2 cut off all contact without explanation · 3 no: it was awkward; "cool cool cool" is ironic · 4 I bet · 5 no lie / honestly · 6 something awkward happened at a birthday party; the details are never given (a shared reference).

Closing the Module

End of C16: Module 5 wrap-up

Recap on slide 19, "hear it, read it, decode it": rhotic · a flap · a glottal stop · prepone · go-slow · first floor ≠ first floor · intelligibility, not imitation (hear it) → theme is arguable · narrator ≠ author · strip → spine → restore · technique → effect → theme · Where does it say that? (read it) → slang has a date and a room · a Trojan horse · MAYOR TO UNVEIL… · whatcha gonna do? · trust the tone (decode it). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C17 warmer (the Decode It! board game is ready for that).

Check the C14 goals. At the end of C16, invite learners to read their Progress Test goal again (the "Look back at my goal" box in Workbook 5.3): "Did you practice it? What will you do next?" This is private: no one reports to the class.

Watch for in Module 6: 6.1 (op-eds, reviews and articles) is where learners produce the media they decoded in C16: headlines and a hook for a review, an allusion used deliberately, a register that suits the publication. Make the link aloud: "Last week you decoded headlines; this week you write one." The close-reading habits from C15 (every claim quotes the text) become the evidence discipline of the long-form writing in 6.2, and the spelling-consistency point from C14 returns in 6.3 (usage and punctuation). In Module 7, C14's respect for variety underpins cross-cultural pragmatics (7.1), and C16's sarcasm and humor return with more care about taboo.

Final assessment (C26) covers all eight modules. From this module, expect: naming and glossing variety features from a transcript (without judging them), British/American false friends and spelling consistency, a short close reading (theme, one technique → effect → theme chain, with quotation), decoding a headline and an allusion, placing slang by register, writing the full form of fast-speech reductions, and reading sarcasm in context.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 6 — Writing with Authority

Three writing workshops for learners who already write correctly and now need to write well. First, the op-ed, review and feature lab: writing for a reader who owes you nothing and can leave at any line (the hook, the line, the concession, the grounded verdict, the close that lands, and a voice matched to the publication). Then the long-form and research-writing lab: architecture over 1,500+ words (the through-line, the argued outline, signposting across sections, and literature-review moves that synthesize sources instead of listing them), with a plan and one section drafted in class. Last, a clinic on the fine points: semicolons, colons and dashes, restrictive vs non-restrictive clauses, parallelism, dangling modifiers, rule vs myth, and US vs UK conventions, ending in an Editing Race.

3 lessons Lessons 6.1–6.3 Classes C17–C19 Weeks 9–10 90 min per class CEFR C2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → a demanding workshop → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. In a writing module the "drill" is short and oral: learners say the working moves of the genre aloud until they sound like reflexes, because prose that cannot be read aloud comfortably is rarely good prose.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-6-writing-authority.html. Print materials/module-6/classroom-materials.html before C17. It holds the flashcards, the writer identity cards, the Commission cards and Pitch cards (A/B), the annotated model texts and the Write–Critique–Revise rubric (C17); the four Source Packs, the Supervision cards (A/B) and the outline planner (C18); the Style-Sheet cards (A/B), the Rule, Convention or Myth? sort and the Editing Race passages and scoreboard (C19); and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home, and each ends in a writing project that extends the in-class workshop.
  • Protect the workshop. Every class gives about a third of its time to one extended product: a drafted, critiqued and revised piece for a named publication (C17); an argued plan for a 1,500–2,500-word piece plus one drafted section that synthesizes sources (C18); a timed editing race in which every correction must be justified by name (C19). If you're running late, cut the controlled practice (set the rest for homework), never the workshop. Writing classes die when writing is always "for homework."
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. These learners met essay structure, hedging, cohesion and summary at C1 (C1 Modules 3 and 6), and in this course they have already worked on discourse markers, long-text cohesion and synthesis (C2 3.1–3.3) and rhetoric and voice (C2 4.1–4.2). Don't re-teach topic sentences or linking words. The C2 step is authority: the reader's trust earned by a clear line, grounded judgment, a plan that carries them, and a surface so clean that nothing snags.
  • Feedback on four things. At C2, accuracy is rarely the most useful column. In every workshop take notes under precision (the exact word or mark: concedes vs claims, a semicolon where a colon was needed), appropriacy (right for this publication and reader: a broadsheet op-ed that opens "Hey guys," a research section that says "I reckon"), naturalness (grammatical but not how an expert writer would put it: "It is important to note that," "In today's fast-paced world," "the author says" six times) and effect (does it work on a reader: a hook that grabs, a verdict that persuades, a signpost that orients). Board 2–3 items per column, without names, and ask the class to improve each one.
  • Useful feedback for near-native writers. C2 learners are often near-native in one area and fossilized in another (flawless argument, but informations; elegant syntax, but a comma splice in every paragraph). Generic praise ("Very good, very fluent") wastes their time. Always (1) quote the exact words, (2) name the effect on a reader ("I stopped trusting the verdict here because it isn't anchored to a detail"), and (3) offer one alternative, not a rewrite. For fossilized errors, agree on a personal "watch list" of two or three items per learner and check only those in the homework; C2 writers improve fastest when they proofread for their own patterns.
  • Peer critique, every class. Each workshop has a structured critique protocol with its own test: the "Where did you stop?" test (C17: the reader marks the exact line where their attention dipped, then scores the rubric), the "Read the claims in order" test (C18: a partner reads only your section claims aloud; if they can be reordered without loss, you have a list, not an argument), and the "Name it" rule (C19: no correction scores unless the team can name the point at issue). Critics always give one strength and one suggestion, in that order, quoting the text. Writers may only say "thank you" or ask a question until all feedback is in: no defending the draft.
  • Dominant speakers. C2 classes often include academics, editors and senior professionals who are used to being the expert in the room. Give them roles that need restraint: in C17 the editor in the pitch (who may ask only three questions) or the timekeeper-reader who marks where attention dipped; in C18 the supervisor with the checklist (who must not rewrite the outline, only question it); in C19 the judge for another team's claims. Say it kindly: "Your eye is excellent. I want it on someone else's draft this round." In whole-class feedback use "a new voice first."
  • Topics stay safe. Op-eds and research questions in this module stay in arts, food, technology, city life, work culture, education and science: no party politics, religion or immigration-policy debates. Every Commission card with an argument has an assigned side, so nobody has to reveal a personal opinion. Learners who prefer not to write personally can take a writer identity card (a food critic, a tech columnist, a city-life blogger…) and write in that persona's voice.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (organisation, criticise, sceptical, rigour, marshalled, honour, centre, flat, full stop, humour). Teach organization, criticize, skeptical, rigor, marshaled, honor, center, apartment, period, humor, and accept British forms whenever a learner uses them consistently. Lesson 6.3 turns the difference into content: learners compare US and UK conventions (quotation marks, the Oxford comma, dash spacing, collective nouns, that / which) and learn that the professional rule is pick one house style and hold it.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Earn the reader → carry the reader → never let the reader snag. In C17 learners write for someone free to leave: every sentence must earn the next, and the voice must fit the room (a broadsheet, a food blog, a trade magazine). In C18 the problem is distance: over 1,500+ words the reader can no longer see the whole, so the writer must build an architecture (a through-line, argued sections, signposts) and bring sources in without surrendering the argument to them. In C19 the scale shrinks to the single mark: a comma that rewrites a family, a dangler that makes the rain walk to work, a semicolon that balances two thoughts. Authority is all three at once: a reason to read, a structure to follow, and a surface the careful reader trusts.

Trauma-informed: persona, assigned sides, private drafts

Audience writing loves the personal anecdote, and anecdotes drift toward difficult moments. In class, never require a true personal story. Offer three routes for every anecdote: a real one, an invented one, or one from a writer identity card. Keep model texts light (lunch at your desk, a noisy food hall, night buses, a museum's free evenings). Drafts are read by one partner or a small group, never displayed to the whole class without the writer's consent; ask "May I share this line?" before boarding anything. Some learners come from places where publishing an opinion carried real risk: make it explicit that op-ed practice is a craft exercise on an assigned side, not a statement of belief. In C19, never board a learner's own error with their name, and never make a learner's accent or L1 "the example" of a punctuation habit.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the six "publication rooms" picture cards (book, restaurant, tv, coffee, park, notebook), genre-move cards (C17), attribution-verb cards color-coded by stance and signposting cards (C18), and punctuation-mark and usage-test cards (C19). Cut them before C17.
  • For C17: one set of Commission cards (8), cut so each learner draws one and the three members of a group hold different cards; Pitch cards (a writer card per learner and the four editor cards); one Write–Critique–Revise rubric per learner; the annotated model op-ed, review and feature for your own reference (set C). Colored pens help: the critic writes in a different color.
  • For C18: one Source Pack per group of 3 (4 different packs, each with four short fictional sources); an outline planner and a section-draft frame per learner; Supervision cards A/B per pair.
  • For C19: Style-Sheet cards A/B per pair; a set of Rule, Convention or Myth? cards per group; the four Editing Race station passages (one copy of each per team, placed at four "stations" around the room), the answer strips for you, and a scoreboard on the whiteboard.
  • Print the writer identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional but powerful: bring in one real review and one real op-ed (from a local paper, a food blog, a trade magazine) on light topics. Learners enjoy finding the hook, the line and the concession in a real published piece, and seeing that professionals do exactly what the lesson teaches. Choose pieces free of party politics.

Module at a Glance

LessonClassFocusLearners can…
6.1C17 · wk 9Writing for an audience: reader vs marker; the op-ed (hook → line → concession → rebuttal with evidence and anecdote → close that lands); the review (minimal description → grounded evaluation → calibrated verdict; no spoilers, no mush); the popular article / feature (headline, front-loading, subheadings, one reader); voice and matching the publicationpitch, draft, critique and revise a 250–350-word op-ed, review or feature for a named publication so that a peer reader does not stop, the line or verdict is clear and grounded, and the voice fits the outlet
6.2C18 · wk 9The long form: why length changes writing in kind; the through-line and the argued outline ("read the claims in order"); informative headings; signposting (backward, forward, locational); integrating sources (paraphrase vs quotation vs patchwriting; attribution verbs that encode stance); literature-review moves (consensus, divergence, building on, the gap); terminological consistency; the abstractplan a 1,500–2,500-word research-style piece whose section claims form a necessary sequence, and draft one 250–300-word section that synthesizes at least two sources with precise attribution verbs and opens with a backward and a forward signpost
6.3C19 · wk 10Fine points: rule vs convention vs myth; semicolon, colon, em dash, the three volumes of aside, the comma splice; restrictive vs non-restrictive (that / which, the commas that carry meaning); the Oxford comma; parallelism; dangling and misplaced modifiers (only, almost); agreement with tricky subjects, who / whom, fewer / less, the subjunctive; US vs UK conventionsfind, correct and justify by name the usage and punctuation faults in an unfamiliar text under time pressure, leave the myths alone, apply one house style consistently, and use punctuation deliberately for rhythm and effect

Lesson Plans

6.1 · C17

Writing for an Audience — Op-eds, Reviews & Articles

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain how writing for a free reader differs from writing for a marker (opening, voice, evidence, sentence rhythm, close) and diagnose "essay register in disguise"
  • open with a hook that makes the second sentence unavoidable (a vivid particular, a provocation, a counter-intuitive claim, an anecdote) and cut throat-clearing (In today's fast-paced world… · This article will discuss…)
  • state an op-ed's line in one breath, concede the strongest objection fairly (The obvious reply is that… · None of this is to deny that…) and rebut it with one statistic and one story
  • write a review that moves from minimal description to grounded evaluation to a calibrated verdict (What makes it remarkable is… · Where it falls short is… · For all its flaws…), without spoilers and without mush
  • match headline, structure and voice to a named publication and its reader, and close on a line that lands rather than summarizes
Materials
  • Workbook 6.1 (New words, Genre toolkit, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Write–Critique–Revise frame, self-check)
  • Slides 3–7 · Publication-room picture cards · Genre-move flashcards · Commission cards (8) · Pitch cards A/B · Write–Critique–Revise rubric (1 per learner) · annotated models (set C, teacher copy) · Writer identity cards · colored pens
  • Listening scripts 6.1-A (the editor's call) and 6.1-B (dictation)

Left: the contract. Marker = captive, paid to read every word. Reader = free, can leave at any line. → Earn the next sentence. Delete: In today's fast-paced world · This article will discuss · It is important to note that.

Center: three genres. OP-ED: hook → line (one breath) → concede the best objection → rebut (1 statistic + 1 story) → close that lands (echo the hook). REVIEW: describe (minimal, no spoilers) → evaluate (every judgment anchored to a detail) → verdict (calibrated: a flaw in the rave, a strength in the pan). FEATURE: specific headline → payoff early → short paragraphs → subheadings → one reader.

Right: the room. Broadsheet · lifestyle blog · trade magazine · popular science · cultural column. Name the outlet → picture the reader → choose the voice. Rubric: Hook · Line/verdict · Grounding · Voice fit · Close · Precision (1–4 each). Test: Where did you stop?

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: headline decoding7S–SReview C16 (5.3, authentic media, slang and cultural reference). Board three invented headlines that play on idiom or reference ("Food Hall Serves Up a Storm," "City Pulls the Plug on Night Buses — Then Plugs It Back In," "Is Your Inbox Eating Your Lunch?"). Pairs explain the wordplay and predict what each article argues. Ask: "Which one would you click? Why?" The headlines become today's lead-in. Note one precision and one naturalness example.
Lead-in: the reader who can leave5T–SSlide 3 (the six publication rooms). Read two openings aloud (slide 4): "This article will discuss the topic of urban green spaces and their various benefits…" and "There is a park bench near my apartment that has, over three years, quietly done more for my mental health than any app I've downloaded." Ask: "You're on a train, half-distracted. Which one do you keep reading? What did the second writer do that the first didn't?" Elicit: a concrete image, a promise, no announcement. "Today: writing for someone who owes you nothing."
Meaning: three genres, one discipline11T–S → S–SSlides 4–5. The op-ed arc (hook → line → concede → rebut → land); the review's three movements and the two opposite dangers (the timid describer, the reckless judge); the feature's front-loading and headline. Keep it brisk: these learners know the ideas; the point is the moves. Pairs sort the genre-move flashcards by genre in 90 seconds. CCQs (below).
Listening: the editor's call6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 6.1-A (an editor calls a writer about a restaurant review draft). First listening: "Will the review be published? What's the main problem?" (Yes, after revision; it's a recap with no verdict.) Second listening: learners note the five changes the editor asks for. Pairs compare.
Close reading: the annotated model8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2. Learners read the model op-ed "Give Us Back the Lunch Hour" alone and label seven moves in the margin (hook, line, concession, rebuttal, evidence, anecdote, close), then write its line in one sentence. Pairs compare; board-check only disagreements. Ask: "Where is the voice strongest? Where does it disappear into the argument, and why is that good?"
Drill: the moves, aloud5T–S → SSee Drill script. Choral then individual: concede-then-turn with contrastive stress, the praise move, the measured criticism, the calibrated verdict, then a quick "hook it" round.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 3 (hook clinic) items 1–2 and Ex 4 (calibrate the verdict) alone; check in pairs. Ex 5 (match the publication) is quick and oral. Leave Ex 3 items 3–4 for homework.
Freer: Write–Critique–Revise Lab30S–S → S → S–SSlide 7. Groups of 3. Pitch (4 min): each learner draws a Commission card (outlet, reader, genre, topic, assigned side, word count). In pairs with Pitch cards, the writer pitches the piece in three sentences (hook, line or verdict, why this reader will care); the editor may ask only three questions. Draft (12 min): alone, each learner writes the opening and body of a 200–250-word piece for the commission (they finish to 250–350 words at home). Critique (8 min): swap within the group. The reader runs the "Where did you stop?" test (a small mark at the exact line where attention dipped), then scores the rubric and writes one strength and one suggestion, quoting the text. Revise (6 min): writers rewrite the hook, the close and the paragraph where the reader stopped. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns on the board. Precision: "falls short to its ambition" → falls short of. Appropriacy: "one might argue" in a lifestyle blog. Naturalness: "In this article I will talk about…" Effect: a hook that promised a story and never delivered it (clickbait). With permission, read one excellent revised hook aloud.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.1 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" list.
Total907 + 5 + 11 + 6 + 8 + 5 + 8 + 30 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the contract. An academic marker is paid to read every word; a reader of an op-ed, review or feature is not. Everything else (the hook, the voice, the varied rhythm, the landing) follows from that one fact.

"Your reader owes you nothing." Can they stop reading in the middle? (Yes, at any line.) Who is the marker? (Someone paid to read the whole thing.) So what is the job of every sentence? (To earn the next one.) · "In today's fast-paced modern world, technology is changing fast." Is it wrong? (No, it's grammatical.) Does it make the second sentence unavoidable? (No: it's throat-clearing.)

Meaning: the line and the concession. An op-ed argues one thing, sayable in a breath. Conceding the strongest objection early is not weakness; it earns a skeptical reader's trust.

"The obvious reply is that busy people are simply busy. That's true, and it's exactly the problem." Is the writer agreeing with the objection? (Partly: they grant it, then turn it.) Why grant it? (So a skeptic feels understood.) Could you persuade someone you seem not to have understood? (No.) · Is an op-ed a balanced essay? (No: it's a case made with conviction, fairly.)

Meaning: the calibrated verdict. Unrelieved praise reads as a press release; unrelieved scorn reads as a grudge. Authority comes from a grounded strength in a pan and an honest reservation in a rave.

"For all its flaws, it is a far better film than most that are flawless." Did the reviewer like the film? (Yes, mostly.) Does the film have problems? (Yes.) Which is more credible: this or "It's perfect, go now"? (This: the praise is believable because the criticism is there.) · "The food was amazing and the vibe was great." What's missing? (Grounding: which dish, what detail?)

Form: the genre toolkit.

Hook typesvivid particular · provocation · counter-intuitive claim · anecdote · the meta-turnThe last video store in town closed on a wet Thursday, and no one came to say goodbye. · We need to talk about how we talk about failure.
Concede → turnIt's tempting to think…, but · The obvious reply is that… · None of this is to deny that… · Granted, …None of this is to deny that the food hall is loud; it is to insist that loud is not the same as bad.
ReframeThe real question is not X but Y. · Here is what we too often forget: … · If that sounds alarmist, consider…The real question is not whether we can afford free museum evenings but whether we can afford empty museums.
Evaluate (review)What makes X remarkable is… · Where it falls short is… · It never quite earns its… · The result is more X than Y.What makes the album remarkable is its restraint. · The result is more museum than meal.
VerdictFor all its flaws, … · worth the price of admission for … alone · a must-read / must-see · where lesser work would…, this…Worth the trip for the flatbread alone.
Featurespecific headline + standfirst (the one-line summary under it) · payoff early · one idea per paragraph · subheadings"Why your 6 a.m. alarm may be sabotaging you" (not "Some thoughts on sleep")

Pronunciation: prose lives in the ear. Teach the read-aloud test as a revision tool. Concede-then-turn sentences depend on contrastive stress and a fall-rise on the concession: "It's TEMPTing to think the problem is NEW ↘↗ | but the EVidence points the OTHer way ↘." The praise move puts the nucleus on the evaluative word: "What makes the book reMARKable | is not its ANswers | but the SHARPness of its QUEStions." Short "punch" sentences after long ones need a real pause before them. Watch op-ed (OP-ed, two syllables, not "oped") and verdict (VERdict).

Drill script
  1. Concede, then turn (choral, then individual; exaggerate the fall-rise): T: "remote work / lonely" → S: "It's tempting to think remote work is simply freedom, but for many people it's lonely." T: "food hall / loud" → "None of this is to deny that the food hall is loud; it's to insist that loud isn't bad." T: "phones at concerts" → "The obvious reply is that people paid for the ticket, and they can film what they like. And yet…"
  2. Praise move: T: "the novel / restraint" → S: "What makes the novel remarkable is its restraint." T: "the café / its bread" → "What makes the café remarkable is its bread." T: "the app / what it leaves out" → "What makes the app remarkable is what it leaves out."
  3. Measured criticism: T: "final act / ambition" → S: "The final act falls short of its ambition." T: "the second half / its promise" → "The second half never quite earns its promise."
  4. Calibrated verdict: T: "flawed but good film" → S: "For all its flaws, it's a far better film than most that are flawless." T: "overpriced but one brilliant dish" → "Worth the price of admission for the flatbread alone."
  5. Hook it (individual, fast): T reads a flat opening ("Libraries are important for communities.") → S offers a hook of a named type ("vivid particular, please"). Accept anything concrete; praise the ones that make you want the second sentence.
Write–Critique–Revise Lab — how to run it
  • Groups of 3; each member holds a different Commission card (outlet, reader, genre, topic, assigned side where relevant, 250–350 words). Topics: a new downtown food hall (review, lifestyle magazine), phone-free concerts (op-ed, arts section), night buses (feature, city magazine), a smart kitchen gadget (product review, tech blog), free Thursday evenings at the art museum (op-ed, local paper), the four-day week at a design studio (feature, HR trade magazine), a neighborhood bakery's sourdough (review, food blog), reply-all culture (op-ed, workplace column). Anyone may write as a writer identity card persona.
  • Pitch (4 min): in pairs with Pitch cards A/B. A (writer) pitches in three sentences: the hook, the line or verdict, and why this outlet's reader will care. B (editor) has the outlet's reader profile on the card and may ask only three questions (give this role to a dominant speaker). Then swap. The third member of the group joins a pair as a second editor.
  • Draft (12 min): silent writing, 200–250 words: the hook, the line or verdict, the body (op-ed: concession + rebuttal with one statistic and one story; review: minimal description → grounded evaluation → verdict; feature: payoff early, one subheading). No dictionaries for the first 8 minutes: write first, polish later. The close can be a placeholder.
  • Critique (8 min): pass the draft to the left. The reader (1) reads once at normal speed and puts a small mark at the exact line where attention dipped, the "Where did you stop?" test; (2) scores the six rubric criteria 1–4; (3) writes one strength and one suggestion, quoting the text, in a different color. Writers listen and may only say thank you or ask a question.
  • Revise (6 min): writers rewrite three things only: the hook, the close, and the paragraph where the reader stopped. Two volunteers read "before" and "after" hooks aloud (with permission).
  • Keep it honest: a score of 2 is useful information, not a failure. At C2, the most valuable critique is usually about effect ("I stopped here because the example is general") rather than grammar.
ICQs before the Lab
In the pitch, how many sentences? (Three.) How many questions can the editor ask? (Three.) · Can you write your own opinion on an op-ed card? (Write the assigned side; it's a craft exercise.) Must the anecdote be true? (No: real, invented or from an identity card.) · In the critique, where do you put the mark? (Where my attention dipped.) Can the writer argue with the critic? (No: only thank you or a question.) · What do you revise? (The hook, the close, and the paragraph where the reader stopped.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C2 speakers are far stronger orally than on the page (often learners who acquired English through work and immersion rather than schooling). Let them draft aloud first: they tell the piece to a partner for two minutes, the partner notes the best sentence, and that sentence becomes the hook. Give them a move frame strip (Hook: … · The line: … · The obvious reply is that… · And yet… · For all its flaws… · Close: echo the hook) and reduce the target to 150 words. In the critique they make excellent "Where did you stop?" readers: honest attention is the whole test. Fewer items: Ex 3 items 1–2, Ex 4 items 1–3.

Stretch

Voice under constraint. Ask strong writers to produce the same opening for two outlets on the same Commission card (the broadsheet and the lifestyle blog) and to annotate every choice that changed (person, sentence length, metaphor, humor, hedging). Then set the harder genre problem: a mixed review whose verdict is genuinely balanced but still actionable ("Go for the bread; skip the mains"), or an op-ed whose close echoes the hook with a twist. For editors and academics: write the standfirst (one-line summary under the headline) and cut the draft by 20% without losing a single move.

L1 watch-outs
  • The exam-essay opening: learners trained for IELTS, TOEFL or national exams often open with a memorized frame ("Nowadays, with the development of technology…," "It is a well-known fact that…"). It is a genre transfer, not an error: praise the discipline, then show that a free reader leaves at that sentence.
  • Delayed thesis: many Chinese, Japanese and Korean writing traditions build toward the main point (and ki-shō-ten-ketsu structures use an unexpected turn before the conclusion). English op-eds and features front-load. Frame it as a reader-responsibility difference: in English audience writing, the writer does the work of making the point findable.
  • Ornate openings and long periodic sentences: formal Spanish, Portuguese, Arabic and Persian prose often values elaborate, rhythmic introductions. At C2 this can be a real asset for voice; the fix is proportion and one short sentence after a long one.
  • Reluctance to use "I": learners schooled in French, German, Russian or many Asian academic traditions may feel the first person is unprofessional. In audience writing it is expected, used with judgment. Conversely, learners from some US-influenced blogging cultures may overuse it.
  • Evaluation adjectives: interesting, nice, amazing, great, very good carry no grounding. Push for the anchored detail and precise evaluative language (restrained, overworked, generous, uneven, assured).
  • False friends in reviews: Spanish actual / French actuel ("the actual menu" = current), sensible ("a sensible performance" = sensitive), German sympathisch ("a sympathetic chef" = likable), eventual (= possible). Circle and ask "Is that what you mean in English?"
Homework

Online lesson 6.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_16.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back with the fall-rise on each concession; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the project: finish the in-class piece to 250–350 words for the named outlet, revised against the rubric and read aloud once. Workbook 6.1: Exercise 3 items 3–4, "My new words" and the journal (a hook and a close you admire from real writing, copied exactly, and one sentence on why each works).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B To make the second sentence unavoidable · Q2 A Anchoring each verdict to a specific detail, and noting a flaw even in a rave · Q3 (new) C "Go for the flatbread, forgive the service, skip the desserts: for all its flaws, Ember is the most interesting new kitchen downtown." Listening (Ex 1): 1 cut the plot-style recap of the evening to two sentences · 2 anchor "amazing" to a dish · 3 give a clear verdict · 4 add one reservation (the noise / the wait) · 5 replace the opening "In this review I will…" with a hook.

6.2 · C18

The Long Form — Extended & Research Writing

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain why length changes writing in kind (the reader can't see the whole; the writer can't hold it) and plan before drafting
  • write a through-line that makes a contestable claim, and build an argued outline of 5–7 sections whose one-line claims, read in order, form a necessary sequence, with informative headings
  • orient a reader across sections with backward, forward and locational signposts (Having established that… · The following section turns to… · The remainder of this report is organized as follows)
  • integrate sources by paraphrasing by default and quoting by exception, avoid patchwriting, and choose attribution verbs that encode stance (demonstrates · claims · concedes · suggests · draws on)
  • use literature-review moves to synthesize rather than list sources (consensus, divergence, building on, identifying a gap, positioning your own contribution), and hold one term per concept across the piece
Materials
  • Workbook 6.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Architecture Lab frame, self-check)
  • Slides 8–13 · Attribution-verb flashcards (color-coded by stance) · Signposting cards · Source Packs A–D (1 per group of 3) · outline planners · section-draft frames · Supervision cards A/B · Writer identity cards
  • Listening scripts 6.2-A (the supervision meeting) and 6.2-B (dictation)

Left: architecture. Through-line (a claim someone could disagree with) → 5–7 section claims, not topics → headings a skimmer can read the argument from. Test: read the claims in order. Could you reorder them without loss? → a list ("and also"), not an argument. Repair: the reverse outline (one sentence per paragraph: what does it actually do?).

Center: signposts at every seam. Backward: Having established that… · As noted in Section 2… Forward: This section argues that… · The following section turns to… Locational: The remainder of this report is organized as follows… · At this point it is worth stepping back… One term per concept (no drift).

Right: sources serve the argument. Paraphrase (default) · quote (the wording is the point) · patchwriting (swapped synonyms = plagiarism). Stance verbs: demonstrates (accept) · argues (a position) · claims (distance) · concedes (against interest) · suggests (tentative) · draws on (extends). Lit-review moves: There is broad agreement that… · Whereas A…, B… · Building on… · Little attention has been paid to… · This study therefore… Shopping list ✘ → synthesis ✔.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: hook swap7S–SReview C17. Learners read their revised hook (from the homework) to a new partner, who must say the piece's line or verdict after hearing only the hook and the next sentence. Swap. Two volunteers share. Ask: "What did the hook promise? Did the second sentence keep the promise?" Collect or glance at the finished pieces; give individual written feedback on them before C19 (quote, name the effect, one alternative).
Lead-in: page nine5T–SSlide 8 (book, notebook, desk, read). "A 1,000-word essay: the reader can hold the whole in view. A 10,000-word report?" Read the supervisor exchange on slide 10 briefly, or tell it: "I got lost around page nine." Ask: "Whose fault is that, the reader's or the writer's? What changes when a piece gets long?" Elicit: structure replaces good sentences as the main problem.
Meaning: architecture11T–S → S–SSlides 9–10. The through-line; the argued outline vs the topic list (the "car-free centers" example from the online lesson); the "read the claims in order" test; informative vs generic headings; three directions of signposting. Pairs turn two generic headings into informative ones ("Background" → "Why the old model broke down"). CCQs (below).
Meaning: sources and the literature review11T–S → S–SSlides 11–12. Paraphrase vs quotation vs patchwriting (show one original and a patchwritten version side by side); attribution verbs by stance; then the key move of the lesson: the shopping-list review ("Smith (2021) found… Lee (2022) found… Okafor (2023) found…") vs the synthesized review ("Although there is broad agreement that…, studies diverge on…; little attention has been paid to…"). Pairs label the moves in the synthesized model. CCQs.
