Freer: Architecture Lab26S–S → S → S–SSlide 13. Groups of 3, one Source Pack each (A: background music and concentration at work · B: public libraries as community hubs · C: food-delivery apps and neighborhood restaurants · D: open-plan offices and collaboration). Plan (9 min): each learner writes a through-line and 5–7 section claims with informative headings for a 1,500–2,500-word piece on the pack's question, using the outline planner. Supervision (4 min): in pairs with Supervision cards: the supervisor reads the writer's section claims aloud in order and asks the card's questions; could they be reordered? Is the through-line contestable? Draft (10 min): each learner drafts one section (250–300 words, usually the literature-review section) that synthesizes at least two sources with precise attribution verbs and opens with a backward and a forward signpost. Reverse-outline check (3 min): swap; the partner writes one sentence per paragraph saying what it actually does and ticks the frame's checklist. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Precision: "Lee claims that the data demonstrate…" when the writer accepts the finding (→ Lee demonstrates…). Appropriacy: "I reckon the sources kind of disagree." Naturalness: "According to Smith, Smith says…" or "the author says" five times. Effect: a section with no signpost where a reader got lost; a shopping-list paragraph. Learners fix and name the move.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.2 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick "I can…".
Total907 + 5 + 11 + 11 + 5 + 6 + 9 + 26 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the argued outline. An outline is not a list of topics; it is the argument in miniature. Each section states what it claims. If the claims, read in order, tell a coherent story that could not be reordered, the piece will hold.

"1. History of cars in cities. 2. Traffic. 3. Pollution. 4. Businesses." Is that an argument? (No, a list of topics.) Could you swap sections 2 and 3? (Yes, nothing breaks.) What does that tell you? (There's no through-line.) · "The business objection, granted: retailers fear lost customers." Is that a topic or a claim? (A claim: it says something someone could check or dispute.)

Meaning: signposting. The writer can hold the whole piece in mind; the reader can't. Signposts tell the reader where they have been, where they are going, and where they are in the whole.

"Having established that remote work raises individual output, we now turn to the hidden cost." Which way does "Having established" look? (Backward.) And "we now turn to"? (Forward.) Why both at the start of a section? (To connect what's settled to what's next.) · Does a 600-word op-ed need "The remainder of this report is organized as follows"? (No: too heavy for the length.)

Meaning: attribution verbs and synthesis. The verb that introduces a source is never neutral: it tells the reader how far you accept the claim. A literature review is not a list of summaries; it shows how the sources relate and where the gap is.

"Okafor claims the app doubled sales." Does the writer fully accept that? (No: claims keeps a skeptical distance.) "Okafor demonstrates…"? (The writer accepts it as shown.) "Even Okafor concedes the sample was small." Is that against Okafor's interest? (Yes: an admission.) · "Smith found X. Lee found Y. Park found Z." Do we know how the three studies relate? (No: it's a shopping list.) "Whereas Smith and Lee report gains, Park's larger study finds none." Now? (Yes: they diverge, and we know why it matters.)
Paraphrase or patchwriting? Original: "Background music with lyrics significantly impaired performance on reading tasks." Version: "Background songs with words considerably damaged performance in reading activities." (Patchwriting: same structure, swapped synonyms; it counts as plagiarism even with a citation.) What's a real paraphrase? (Lyrics seem to get in the way of reading, Rossi (2021) found, though instrumental music did not.)

Form.

Stance verbsaccept · position · distance · admission · tentative · extension · neutraldemonstrates / shows / establishes · argues / contends / maintains · claims / asserts / alleges · concedes / acknowledges / grants · suggests / proposes / speculates · draws on / builds on · notes / observes / points out
Lit-review: consensusThere is broad agreement that… · Most studies concur that… · A growing body of work suggests…A growing body of work suggests that libraries are becoming community hubs (Garcia 2020; Nwosu 2022).
Lit-review: divergenceWhereas A…, B… · However, studies diverge on… · In contrast to…, …Whereas Garcia (2020) reports rising visits, Chen (2023) finds that book loans continue to fall.
Lit-review: building on / gapBuilding on…, … · Little attention has been paid to… · What remains unclear is…What remains unclear is whether new visitors ever borrow books.
PositioningThis report therefore… · The present study addresses…This report therefore asks what happens to the loan desk when the library becomes a hub.
Tense in reportingpresent for a source's current argument; past for a specific study's finding; present perfect for a body of researchRossi argues… · Rossi's 2021 study found… · Several studies have shown…

Pronunciation. Attribution verbs are often read but rarely heard, so learners guess the stress. Drill: conTEND, mainTAIN, deMONstrate, esTABlish, asSERT, alLEGE, acKNOWLedge, SPECulate, proPOSE. Noun–verb stress pairs: an ABstract / to abSTRACT, a REcord / to reCORD, a SUBject / to subJECT. Synthesis = SYNthesis; synthesize = SYNthesize. Research is accepted with either stress in US English (REsearch / reSEARCH); don't correct it. When presenting an outline aloud, pause at each seam and use a rising "list" tone on section claims until the last, which falls.

Drill script
  1. Stance ping-pong (choral, then individual): T: "You accept it as proven: Rossi, the effect." → S: "Rossi demonstrates the effect." T: "You're skeptical: the brochure, a total cure." → "The brochure claims a total cure." T: "An admission: even the author, a small sample." → "Even the author concedes the sample was small." T: "Tentative: the paper, a possible link." → "The paper suggests a possible link." T: "Extension: the model, earlier work." → "The model draws on earlier work."
  2. Finish the signpost: T: "Having established that visits are rising, …" → S: "…we now turn to the question of what visitors actually do." T: "The remainder of this report…" → "…is organized as follows." T: "This point…" → "…is developed further in Section 4."
  3. List → synthesis (individual): T: "Garcia: visits up. Chen: loans down." → S: "Whereas Garcia reports rising visits, Chen finds that loans continue to fall." T: "Most studies: noise hurts reading. Nobody tested older workers." → "Most studies concur that noise hurts reading, but little attention has been paid to older workers."
  4. Stress check: T: "an abstract" → S: "an ABstract." T: "to abstract" → "to abSTRACT." T: "contend / acknowledge / speculate" (choral).
Architecture Lab — how to run it
  • Groups of 3, one Source Pack per group; each pack holds four short fictional sources (author, year, type, key finding, one quotable line, a limitation). Say clearly that they are invented for practice and must not be cited in real work. Learners may also plan a piece from their own field instead (academics and professionals often prefer this); they still use the pack for the drafted section, or bring two real sources they know well.
  • Plan (9 min): on the outline planner: the through-line in one sentence (a claim someone could disagree with), then 5–7 sections, each with an informative heading and a one-line claim, and a word budget per section adding up to 1,500–2,500 words. Mark where each source enters and the attribution verb it will get.
  • Supervision (4 min): pairs with Supervision cards. The supervisor reads the writer's section claims aloud in order, without the headings, then asks the card's four questions (Is the through-line contestable? Could any two sections swap? Where is the strongest objection? Which term will you hold constant?). The supervisor may not rewrite; only question. Swap roles. Give the supervisor role to a dominant speaker first.
  • Draft (10 min): each learner drafts one section of 250–300 words (the literature-review section is recommended). Frame checklist: ☐ opens with a backward and a forward signpost ☐ synthesizes at least two sources (consensus, divergence, or gap) ☐ at least three different attribution verbs, each chosen for stance ☐ at least one paraphrase and at most one short quotation ☐ one key term held constant ☐ ends by pointing forward.
  • Reverse-outline check (3 min): swap drafts. The partner writes one sentence per paragraph describing what it does ("summarizes Garcia; contrasts Chen; names the gap") and ticks the checklist. A paragraph whose sentence is "summarizes a source" and nothing more = a shopping-list flag.
  • The rest of the piece is not the homework; the online project is the outline and an abstract. Learners who want to write the full piece can bring it to C25 (the review project) or the 8.3 masterwork.
ICQs before the Lab
Is your through-line a topic or a claim? (A claim.) How many sections? (Five to seven.) What goes next to each heading? (A one-line claim.) · In the supervision, what does the supervisor read aloud? (The claims, in order.) Can the supervisor rewrite your outline? (No, only ask questions.) · How many sections do you draft today? (One.) How many sources must it bring together? (At least two.) Are the sources real? (No, invented for practice.) · In the check, what do you write for each paragraph? (What it does.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Long-form planning is a visual task: let these learners plan on sticky notes, one claim per note, and physically reorder them on the desk; the "read the claims in order" test becomes a real move they can see. Give them a move strip of five literature-review frames to copy and complete (There is broad agreement that… · Whereas…, … · Building on… · Little attention has been paid to… · This report therefore…). Reduce the plan to 4 sections and the draft to one 120-word synthesis paragraph using two sources. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 4 items 1–2.

Stretch

The whole piece in miniature. Ask strong writers to write the abstract (150–200 words: context → problem → method → findings → meaning) for their planned piece before leaving class, then check it against the outline: any finding in the abstract that no section delivers is a structural hole. Researchers can add a positioning paragraph that states the gap and the contribution in two sentences, and test terminological drift in their own real draft (circle every name for the key concept). Offer the harder synthesis: three sources that agree on the finding but disagree on the cause.

L1 watch-outs
  • Source use and "respectful copying": in some educational traditions (for example, parts of China, the Gulf, South and Southeast Asia, and Eastern Europe), reproducing an authority's exact words is a sign of respect and learning. Treat patchwriting as a convention to learn, not a moral failing, but be completely clear that in English-medium academic and professional writing it counts as plagiarism.
  • Reader-responsible prose: Japanese, Korean and Chinese academic writing often expects the reader to infer connections; learners from these traditions may drop signposts because stating them feels condescending. The supervisor dialogue ("I knew where I was, so I assumed the reader did") is for them.
  • Digressive architecture: German, Russian and some Spanish-language academic traditions tolerate (and sometimes admire) digressions and long theoretical preambles. The "read the claims in order" test shows where a digression breaks the sequence.
  • "Says" and "according to" as the only reporting tools, and the double attribution "According to Smith, he says…" (common from Spanish según, Arabic wafqan li, Chinese 根据…说). Push for stance verbs.
  • Tense of reporting: learners from languages without the present perfect (Chinese, Russian, Arabic) or with different reporting norms may write "Many studies showed" for a body of research. Teach the three-way choice on the board plan.
  • Elegant variation: French and Spanish school writing often penalizes repetition, so learners rename the key concept every paragraph (knowledge-sharing → informal learning → tacit exchange). In long-form English, a load-bearing term stays constant.
Homework

Online lesson 6.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_17.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and repeat the signposts until they feel like reflexes; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the project (the full outline plus a 150–200-word abstract, built on today's plan). Workbook 6.2: Exercise 5 (shopping list → synthesis), revise the drafted section using the partner's reverse outline, "My new words" and the journal (a reverse outline of a long piece you have read, and whether it has a real through-line).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C You could not reorder the sections without breaking the argument · Q2 C Last, and it should summarize the whole, delivering the actual findings · Q3 (new) B "Whereas Garcia (2020) reports a sharp rise in library visits, Chen (2023) finds that loans continue to fall; what remains unclear is whether new visitors ever borrow books." Listening (Ex 1): problems: the reader gets lost (no signposts) · Section 3 has drifted off the through-line · the literature review is a list of summaries; fixes: open each section with a backward + forward signpost · cut or reframe Section 3 so it serves the through-line · regroup the sources by where they agree and disagree, and end on the gap.

6.3 · C19

Fine Points of Usage & Punctuation — Clinic & Editing Race

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • tell a meaning-bearing rule (danglers, restrictive commas, the comma splice) from a register convention (whom, the subjunctive were) and a myth (split infinitives, final prepositions, starting with And), and choose accordingly
  • use the semicolon (two balanced independent clauses; lists whose items contain commas), the colon (delivering what the clause promised) and the em dash (an emphatic aside or turn), and choose among commas, dashes and parentheses as three volumes of aside
  • distinguish restrictive and non-restrictive clauses (that / which, commas or none) and explain the change of meaning; use the Oxford comma where ambiguity threatens
  • repair faulty parallelism, dangling and misplaced modifiers (only, almost, even), agreement with tricky subjects, who / whom, fewer / less, between you and me and the mandative subjunctive
  • apply one house style consistently (US or UK quotation marks, dash spacing, the Oxford comma, collective nouns, -ize / -ise), and use punctuation deliberately for rhythm
Materials
  • Workbook 6.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Editing Race score sheet, self-check)
  • Slides 14–19 · Punctuation-mark and usage-test flashcards · Style-Sheet cards A/B · Rule, Convention or Myth? sort cards · Editing Race station passages 1–4 (one copy each per team) + answer strips (teacher) · scoreboard on the whiteboard · a timer
  • Listening scripts 6.3-A (hear the punctuation) and 6.3-B (restrictive or not?)

Left: the marks. ; two independent clauses, balanced (The rain stopped; the match resumed.) · list items containing commas. : delivers the promise (She had one goal: to win.) — a complete clause before it. — emphatic aside / turn, sparingly. Three volumes: , neutral · — loud · ( ) quiet. ✘ comma splice: It rained, we left. → ; · . · and.

Center: meaning-bearing. My brother who lives in Berlin is a doctor. (several brothers) vs My brother, who lives in Berlin, is a doctor. (one brother) · that = identifies (US edited prose) · , which , = adds · Oxford comma: my parents, the chef, and the porter. · Danglers: Walking to work, the rain soaked me. → Walking to work, I… · only / almost right before what they limit · Parallel: install, configure, and maintain.

Right: rule · convention · myth. Tests: he/him → who/whom · drop the other person → you and me · count it? → fewer · whose action? → dangler · read it aloud → splice. Conventions: whom, subjunctive were, that/which strictness. Myths: split infinitive, final preposition, And/But at the start. US / UK: "double" + ,. inside / 'single' + logical · word—word / word – word · Oxford comma usual / optional · the team is / is or are · Mr. / Mr · -ize / -ise or -ize. Pick one; hold it.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: signpost relay7S–SReview C18. Groups of 3: each learner states their through-line from C18, then the group builds a spoken relay for one learner's piece: A gives a backward signpost, B a forward one, C a locational one. Then one learner turns two "shopping-list" sentences on the slide into one synthesized sentence. Return the C17 pieces with your written feedback.
Lead-in: two commas and a family4T–SSlide 14. "My brother who lives in Berlin is a doctor." / "My brother, who lives in Berlin, is a doctor." Ask: "How many brothers in each?" Let the class argue; then read both aloud (Script 6.3-B item 1) so they hear the pauses. "Two commas just rewrote a family. Today: the small marks that carry meaning, and the 'rules' that don't."
Clinic 1: punctuation12T–S → S–SSlide 15. Semicolon (two uses), colon (what comes before must be a complete clause), em dash; the three volumes of aside (commas / dashes / parentheses) using the same sentence; the comma splice and its three cures; the Oxford comma that prevents a misreading. Restrictive vs non-restrictive: that without commas, which with them (strict in US edited prose, looser in British). Pairs punctuate four board sentences two ways and explain the difference. CCQs (below).
Clinic 2: usage11T–S → S–SSlide 16. Danglers and misplaced only / almost (let learners laugh at the picture each faulty sentence paints: the coffee rushing to the train); parallelism in series and paired connectives; agreement (each has, neither… nor + nearer subject, the box of chocolates is); the quick tests (he/him, drop the other person, can you count it?); the mandative subjunctive (that he be present). Then slide 17: rule, convention or myth? Groups sort the 12 cards in 3 minutes and check.
Drill: hear the marks4T–S → SSee Drill script. Read-aloud pairs with and without commas (pitch drop and pause on the non-restrictive clause), the colon's suspense, the dash's sharp turn, and the "short sentence after a long one."
Listening: hear the punctuation6T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 6.3-A: six sentences read with clear pauses and intonation, no punctuation shown. Learners write the punctuation they hear (comma pairs, semicolon, colon, dash). Then Script 6.3-B: learners say "one" or "several" for each restrictive / non-restrictive pair.
Controlled practice9S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (restrictive or not?), Ex 3 (choose the mark) and Ex 4 (danglers and parallelism) alone; check in pairs. Ex 5 (US or UK?) is quick and oral, and feeds the style-sheet negotiation.
Style-sheet negotiation5S–SSlide 17. Pairs with Style-Sheet cards: A is a copy editor at a US publisher, B is a British author whose manuscript is being published in the US (information gap: each card lists different preferences and one non-negotiable). In four minutes they agree a five-line house style for the book (quotation marks, dashes, Oxford comma, spelling, collective nouns) and write it down. Early finishers compare with another pair. This is also practice for 7.2's negotiation.
Freer: the Editing Race22S–S → T–SSlide 18. Teams of 3 rotate through four stations (4 min each + 1 min to move), each with a short passage in a different genre (a café review, a research paragraph, a feature on night buses, an office memo). Each passage has a stated number of faults. Teams mark every fault, write the correction and name the point on the race sheet. Scoring: +1 correct fix · +1 correct name · −1 "false alarm" (changing a myth or a correct sentence, e.g. un-splitting an infinitive, "fixing" a sentence that ends with a preposition) · +2 bonus per station for one deliberate style improvement (a semicolon, colon or dash used for effect) that the team can justify. Final 2 minutes: you read the answer strips, teams self-score, and the class votes on the best style improvement. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Precision: "a colon after an incomplete clause" (The ingredients are: flour… → acceptable in some US styles, but better The ingredients are flour… or You need three things: …). Appropriacy: whom in a friendly text. Naturalness: an infinitive contorted to avoid splitting it. Effect: five dashes in one paragraph. Learners fix and say "rule, convention or myth?"
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.3 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists for the module (see Closing the Module).
Total907 + 4 + 12 + 11 + 4 + 6 + 9 + 5 + 22 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: restrictive vs non-restrictive. A restrictive clause identifies which one; a non-restrictive clause adds information about something already identified. The commas, not just the word, carry the difference.

"The report that I sent yesterday is final." How many reports are there? (More than one.) Which one is final? (The one I sent yesterday.) · "The report, which I sent yesterday, is final." How many reports? (One.) Is "I sent it yesterday" needed to know which report? (No: it's extra.) · "The employees, who worked overtime, got a bonus." Did everyone get a bonus? (Yes, all of them; and all of them worked overtime.) Without commas? (Only the ones who worked overtime.)

Meaning: the marks.

"The rain stopped; the match resumed." Two sentences or one? (Two independent clauses, held together.) Could we use a comma? (No: that's a comma splice.) A period? (Yes, but the link is weaker.) · "She had one goal: to win." Does the colon promise something? (Yes: the goal.) "Her goals are: to win." (No: the clause before the colon isn't complete.) · "The proposal (after much debate) was approved." / "The proposal — after much, much debate — was approved." Which aside is louder? (The dashes.) Which almost whispers? (The parentheses.)
"Walking to the office, the rain soaked me." Who was walking? (Me, but the sentence says the rain!) Fix? (Walking to the office, I was soaked by the rain.) · "We almost drove 500 miles." Did we drive? (As written, no: we almost did.) What did the writer mean? (We drove almost 500 miles.) · "To boldly go." Rule, convention or myth? (Myth: splitting an infinitive is fine.)

Form.

Semicolonindependent clause ; independent clause · list items that contain commasThe café was full; the bakery next door was empty. · Lisbon, Portugal; Accra, Ghana; and Hanoi, Vietnam
Coloncomplete clause : what it promised (list, explanation, example, punchline)The menu has one weakness: dessert.
Em dashaside or sudden turn; closed in most US book style (word—word), often spaced in newspapers and online (word — word); UK usually a spaced en dash (word – word)They had planned for everything—and then the power failed.
Restrictivethat / who, no commas (identifies)The app that crashed was mine.
Non-restrictive, which / , who , (adds)The app, which crashed twice, is free.
Parallelismsame form in a series and after paired connectives (not only… but also · either… or · both… and)diligent, creative, and able to lead · not only skilled but also disciplined
Modifiersan introductory participle attaches to the subject; only / almost / even go right before what they limitHaving finished the report, I found that the printer had jammed. · We sell only organic eggs.
Agreementeach / every / either / neither → singular · neither A nor B → nearer subject · head noun, not the intervening phraseEach of the plans has merit. · Neither the manager nor the staff were told. · The box of chocolates is here.
Subjunctivecontrary-to-fact were · mandative base form after insist, recommend, demand thatIf I were you… · The board recommends that she step down.

Pronunciation: punctuation is prosody. A non-restrictive clause is read on a lower pitch with a pause either side ("My brother | who lives in Berlin | is a doctor"); a restrictive clause runs straight through with no pause. A colon gets a slight rise and a held pause (suspense); a dash a sharper, shorter break; a semicolon a pause longer than a comma but with the voice not fully falling. Reading aloud is the best splice detector: at a comma splice the voice falls to a full stop and the ear hears the error. Model, don't lecture: learners copy you.

Drill script
  1. One or several? (T reads, class answers): "My sister | who lives in Madrid | is a lawyer." → "One sister." "My sister who lives in Madrid is a lawyer." → "Several sisters." Then learners read each version aloud, choral, then individual.
  2. Colon suspense: T: "She had exactly one demand ↗ | … that the meeting be held in public ↘." Choral, then individuals with their own endings ("The menu has one weakness: …").
  3. Dash turn: T: "They had planned for everything — and then the power failed." Stress the pause before and then. Choral.
  4. Fix it fast (individual): T: "between you and I" → S: "between you and me." T: "less mistakes" → "fewer mistakes." T: "Each of the proposals have merit." → "Each of the proposals has merit." T: "I insist that he is present." → "I insist that he be present." T: "To who should I write?" → "To whom should I write?"
Editing Race — how to run it
  • Set up before class: four stations around the room (tables or wall spots), each with one passage (set G): Station 1 a café review (8 faults), Station 2 a research paragraph (8 faults), Station 3 a feature on night buses (8 faults), Station 4 an office memo (8 faults). Each passage also contains two traps: a correct "myth" sentence (a split infinitive, a final preposition, a sentence starting with And) that must not be changed.
  • Teams of 3, one race sheet per team, one pen. Roles rotate at each station: editor (marks the passage), namer (writes the name of each point: "comma splice," "dangling modifier," "non-restrictive commas"), judge (decides whether a change is a real fault or a myth). Give the judge role to a dominant speaker: they must listen to both teammates before ruling.
  • Timing: 4 minutes per station, 1 minute to move (use a visible timer). Teams start at different stations. After four rounds, 2 minutes for scoring: read the answer strips aloud; teams self-score on the race sheet and you board the totals.
  • Scoring: +1 each fault correctly fixed · +1 each correct name · −1 each false alarm (a myth "corrected," or a correct sentence changed) · +2 per station for one style improvement the team can justify (e.g. joining two related short sentences with a semicolon; a colon before a payoff). Ties are broken by the fewest false alarms.
  • Why the "name it" rule: at C2 the goal is not to fix by instinct but to be able to explain a change to a colleague, a client or a student. A fix without a name scores half.
  • If time is short: run three stations (3 × 5 min) and do Station 4 as homework. Early finishers: rewrite the station passage's weakest paragraph for rhythm (a long sentence, then a short one that lands).
ICQs before the Editing Race
How many minutes at each station? (Four.) How many faults in each passage? (Eight.) · Do you just correct the fault? (No: correct it and name it.) What do you lose a point for? (Changing something that isn't wrong: a myth.) · Is "to really understand" a fault? (No: a split infinitive is fine.) · How do you get the bonus? (One style improvement we can justify.) Who decides if it's a fault or a myth? (The judge, after listening to both teammates.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give these learners the quick-test card (he/him · drop the other person · can you count it? · identify or add? · whose action? · read it aloud) and let them work by ear: in the race, the namer role suits a strong reader, while the learner who hears well reads the passage aloud and says "stop" at every snag. Pre-mark the number of faults per paragraph on their copy. Reduce the controlled practice to Ex 2 items 1–3 and Ex 4 items 1–3; target: hear and fix the comma splice and the dangler.

Stretch

Punctuation as music. Give strong writers a flat paragraph of eight even sentences (the "reads flat" paragraph from the online lesson's editor dialogue) and ask them to rewrite it for rhythm using exactly one semicolon, one colon, one dash and one short sentence, then justify each mark in a margin note. For editors and translators: compare the house styles of two real publications (a US magazine and a UK newspaper) and write a six-line style sheet. For the hardest usage cases, discuss none is / none are, singular they (accepted in US and UK style guides), and data is / data are by register.

L1 watch-outs
  • Comma splices by transfer: Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian, Russian, Vietnamese and Chinese all allow (or tolerate) joining independent clauses with a comma. This is the most frequent fossilized error at C2. Put it on each learner's personal watch list.
  • German and Russian commas: German requires a comma before dass and every relative clause, Russian before что and который, so learners write "I think, that…" and "The report, that I sent yesterday, is final" (a restrictive clause with non-restrictive commas). Contrast with English, where commas around a relative clause change the meaning.
  • French spacing: French puts a space before : ; ? ! ("Attention : …"). Learners who write French professionally carry it over. Also French and Russian guillemets « » and German „…“ quotation marks.
  • Dashes for dialogue: Spanish, Russian and French fiction use a dash to open dialogue. In English quoted speech uses quotation marks.
  • Hypercorrection is native too: "between you and I" and "whom shall I say is calling?" are errors of educated native speakers. Tell learners so; it is a relief for them to know that some "native" usage they have copied is itself the error.
  • Arabic punctuation: Arabic uses the comma (،) and wa ("and") far more freely between clauses, so long comma-linked chains are a natural transfer. Also watch the inverted comma form and question mark (؟) in typed work.
Homework

Online lesson 6.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_18.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and read them aloud, letting the punctuation shape the pauses; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the project (correct the flawed passage, justify three changes by name, and flag one convention-vs-rule judgment). Workbook 6.3: finish any Editing Race station you missed, "My new words" and the journal (proofread a page of your own writing for your personal watch list; name the fine point you most often miss).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B The writer has one sister; that she lives in Madrid is extra information · Q2 A "Never end a sentence with a preposition" · Q3 (new) C "Having checked the figures twice, the analyst approved the report; the board, which met on Friday, agreed." Listening (Ex 1, Script 6.3-A): 1 The proposal, after much debate, was approved. · 2 The café was full; the bakery next door was empty. · 3 She had one goal: to win. · 4 They had planned for everything — and then the power failed. · 5 My colleague, who speaks four languages, translated the menu. · 6 We visited Lisbon, Portugal; Accra, Ghana; and Hanoi, Vietnam.

Closing the Module

End of C19: Module 6 wrap-up

Recap on slide 19, "earn, carry, polish": a hook that makes the second sentence unavoidable, a line in one breath, a grounded verdict (earn the reader) → a through-line, claims in a necessary order, signposts at every seam, sources synthesized with stance verbs (carry the reader) → the comma that carries meaning, the dangler repaired, the myth left alone, one house style held (never let the reader snag). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook and write their personal watch list (two or three fine points they will proofread for in every piece from now on) on the inside cover. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you; revisit it in the C20 warmer and in C25.

Watch for in Module 7: 7.1 (cross-cultural pragmatics) returns to audience from a new angle: the same op-ed hook or joke lands differently across cultures, so the "picture the reader" habit from C17 transfers directly. The C19 style-sheet negotiation is a low-stakes rehearsal for 7.2's negotiation; point it out. 7.3 (translation awareness) will reward the C19 learners who noticed their L1 punctuation habits.

Assessment: this module feeds the C26 final assessment. Before C26, check the final test and its key for the exact items; the skills to revise are an audience-aware piece for a named outlet (hook, line or verdict, grounding, close), a synthesized paragraph with stance verbs, and the fine points from 6.3 (comma splices, restrictive commas, danglers, parallelism, agreement, rule vs myth). The 8.3 masterwork is the natural home for the long piece learners planned in C18: encourage them to write it.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 7 — Communicating Across Cultures

Three classes about what happens when fluent English meets different unwritten rules. First, cross-cultural pragmatics: politeness strategies, directness and indirectness, small talk, compliments and silence, taboo as a linguistic phenomenon (euphemism, minced oaths, the euphemism treadmill) and humor that does or doesn't travel, with a repair toolkit for when the wires cross. Then diplomacy and negotiation: acknowledge-then-counter, positions versus interests, the BATNA, the conditional trade (If you were able to…, then we could…), face-saving and the cushioned difficult message, practiced in a full negotiation simulation between invented organizations. Last, working between languages: why word-for-word fails, false friends, the untranslatable, register loss, translation versus interpreting and the ethics of interpreting, with a lab in which learners may (voluntarily) bring their own languages into the room.

3 lessons Lessons 7.1–7.3 Classes C20–C22 Weeks 10–11 90 min per class CEFR C2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → a demanding freer task (a workshop, not a quick chat) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-7-across-cultures.html. Print materials/module-7/classroom-materials.html before C20. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Critical-Incident Clinic case cards and analysis frame, the euphemism ladders, the two Negotiation Simulation scenarios (confidential A/B briefs, prep sheet, observer checklist), the Translation & Interpreting Lab cards (ethics dilemmas, register rescue, plain-English sight texts, the untranslatables gallery), the Bridge It! board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has Listen buttons, so learners can hear the dialogues and model sentences again at home, and shadow the tone, which matters more than usual in this module.
  • These are workshop classes. Each class spends roughly a quarter to a third of its time on one extended performance: diagnosing and bridging invented cross-cultural incidents (C20), a full multi-issue negotiation (C21), a mediation lab with sight translation and interpreting-ethics dilemmas (C22). Protect that time. If you run late, shorten the controlled practice (set the rest for homework), never the workshop.
  • Refinement, not a first meeting. These learners met implicature and irony in C2 Module 2 (C4–C6), register agility in 1.2 (C2), and diplomatic softening and hedging at C1 (C1 3.3). Don't re-teach "I was wondering if…". The C2 step is calibration and metapragmatic control: choosing the degree of directness for this person and moment, decoding what others' softeners are doing, repairing a misfire lightly, and being able to explain a pragmatic choice to someone else, which is exactly what a mediator does.
  • Feedback on four things. At C2 accuracy is rarely the story. Take notes in four columns: accuracy (fossilized slips worth fixing: I don't hope so, agree to disagree used as a threat, a false friend like actually for "currently"), range (well-chosen moves to praise and spread: I don't suppose there's any chance…, Where might there be room to move?, The nearest we get in English is…), appropriacy (grammatical but miscalibrated: a hedge so heavy the refusal vanished, with respect used as a sneer, a joke at a colleague's expense) and effect (what the move did to the other person: Did they relax? Dig in? Save face?). In the feedback stage, board 2–3 items from each column, without names.
  • Peer review, every class. Each workshop ends with a structured peer check, each with its own test: the good-faith test (C20: could both people in the incident read your analysis and feel fairly described?), the "Can they sell it upstairs?" test (C21: each negotiator states in one sentence how they will present the deal to their own side; if either can't, face wasn't saved) and the "Explain it back" test (C22: the listener explains the concept or text back in their own words; if the meaning and the feeling both arrive, the mediation worked). Reviewers always give one strength and one suggestion, in that order, and quote the words rather than summarizing.
  • Dominant speakers. C2 classes often include confident professionals: managers, lawyers, teachers, interpreters. Give them the roles that need listening: in C20 the good-faith auditor (who may only ask questions until the group has written two readings), in C21 the observer or the "board chair" who hears the deal only at the end and must be convinced by it, in C22 the listener who explains it back. Say it kindly: "Your ear is excellent. I want you on the checklist this round." In whole-class feedback, use "a new voice first" before opening the floor.
  • Useful feedback for near-native learners. Many of these learners are fluent beyond their teacher in some domains and fossilized in others. Don't praise fluency in general; praise a specific move and its effect ("Your 'Correct me if I'm wrong, but…' made him laugh and concede in the same breath"). Target fossilized items with a private written note, not a public correction. And ask for metapragmatic reasoning: "Why that softener, for that person, at that moment?" At C2, the explanation is part of the skill.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and some British idioms (humour, recognise, cosy, cancelled, per cent, brilliant, have I got the wrong end of the stick?). Teach humor, recognize, cozy, canceled, percent, and give the American equivalent of British-flavored idioms alongside the original (Did I get the wrong idea? / Did I misunderstand you?). Accept British forms whenever a learner uses them. This module is a good place to say it out loud: varieties of English have pragmatic differences too (understatement, for example), and none is the neutral default.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Read the rules → negotiate across them → carry meaning between them. In C20 learners notice that the same words can follow different rules, and practice decoding a hedge, handling a delicate topic and repairing a misfire, without ever turning a person into a stereotype. In C21 they take that calibration into the pressure-cooker: a negotiation where tone decides whether the other side digs in or works with you, and where the best deal comes from interests, not positions. In C22 they cross the widest gap: between languages, where the unit of meaning is the idea, not the word, and where the mediator's ethics matter as much as their vocabulary. Every class produces something another learner hears and judges: a bridge script, a signed deal, a mediated text.

Culture-safe classroom: situations, not nationalities

This module is where a well-meant class can slide into stereotyping. Set the rules on day one (slide 2) and keep them all module. (1) Talk about situations and settings, never nationalities. Don't ask "In your country, how do people refuse?" Ask "In a setting you know well (a workplace, a family, a school, a community), what would be usual? And is everyone there the same?" (2) Nobody speaks for a culture. No learner is ever the "representative" or "example" of a country, a religion or a language community. Any learner can use an invented setting or a new identity card, with no reason given. (3) Individuals, not types. When a generalization slips out ("They're always so direct"), reframe it without shaming: "Some people, in some settings. What about the specific person in the story?" (4) Humor has limits. No jokes at the expense of nationalities, religions, accents or disabilities, and nobody performs an accent, their own or anyone else's. (5) Taboo is studied as language. We look at euphemism and mild substitutes (passed away, let go, the facilities, darn, gosh). Nobody is asked to say a swear word, name a taboo from their own language or describe what is taboo "where they come from." (6) Own languages are voluntary. In C22, learners may draw on their other languages; every task has an English-only route of equal value.

Trauma-informed: invented stakes, and the child interpreter in the room

Keep every negotiation and difficult message invented and low-stakes: room bookings, a coffee cart, a price rise, a launch date, a volunteer rota. Never role-play a firing or layoff, a salary cut, a disciplinary hearing, a medical diagnosis, a landlord eviction, a police, court or immigration interview, or a family conflict. Positions are assigned on cards; learners play the role and anyone can say "Pass." C22 needs particular care: many adult immigrants and refugees interpreted for their parents as children, or have depended on an interpreter (or a child) at a hospital, a benefits office or an immigration appointment. Those settings appear in this lesson only as ethics discussed in the abstract ("Why do professional interpreters exist?"), never as a role-play. If a learner shares such an experience, thank them, don't probe, and offer to talk after class. Journals always offer "a real one, an invented one, or one from a new identity card."

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the four picture "moments" (coffee, cake, umbrella, email) for C20; politeness-strategy, repair and euphemism cards (C20); negotiation-move cards (C21); false-friend and mediating-frame cards and the four picture "everyday mediation" cards (menu, phone, book, passport) for C22. Cut them before C20.
  • For C20: one set of Critical-Incident Clinic case cards (8 invented incidents: one per group of 3, two spare) and one analysis frame (Describe · Interpret · Evaluate · Bridge) per group; one set of euphemism ladder strips per pair.
  • For C21: the Negotiation Simulation. Two scenarios (The Hall and The Coffee Cart), each with a confidential A brief and B brief, a prep sheet per learner and an observer checklist per group. Decide groupings in advance: two negotiators per side works best (A-pair vs B-pair, plus one observer), so groups of 5; with smaller classes, 1 v 1 + observer. Keep the A and B briefs in separate envelopes: the simulation only works if the other side's points stay secret.
  • For C22: the Translation & Interpreting Lab: 8 ethics dilemma cards and 2 register rescue cards per group; the untranslatables gallery (a set of word cards on a side table); 3 plain-English sight texts (the English-only route). A week before, ask learners (optionally) to bring a short, simple, public text in any language they read: a recipe, a museum sign, a product label, a children's news story, a tourist leaflet. Say clearly: nothing personal, no letters, no medical or legal documents, and bringing nothing is completely fine.
  • Print the new identity cards (six workplace profiles, each with an invented cross-cultural moment, a negotiation and a word to convey) and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • The Bridge It! board game (1 board per group, 1 die, counters) is for early finishers in C22 and the C23 warmer.

7.1C20 · wk 10Cross-cultural pragmatics: directness vs indirectness in requests, refusals, criticism and disagreement; politeness strategies (bald on record, positive, negative, off record); small talk, compliment responses, silence; taboo as a linguistic phenomenon (euphemism, dysphemism, minced oaths, the euphemism treadmill) and signal-soften-defer for sensitive topics; humor (irony, self-deprecation, banter, understatement) and why it fails; the repair toolkit (notice · clarify · realign)diagnose an invented cross-cultural critical incident from both sides in good faith, decode the softeners in it, and script and perform a bridge that repairs it (a softener, a clarifying check and a realigning line), without generalizing about any group

7.3C22 · wk 11Working between languages: why word-for-word fails (idiom, collocation, grammar); false friends; the untranslatable and culture-bound, conveyed by the paraphrase pattern (signal → literal image → plain meaning → feeling); register loss; translation vs interpreting (consecutive, simultaneous, sight translation); the ethics of interpreting (accuracy and completeness, impartiality, confidentiality, transparency, role boundaries, the first-person convention)sight-translate a short, simple text of their choice into natural English (or render a dense English notice into plain spoken English), convey an untranslatable word so that a listener can explain it back with its feeling, and reason through an interpreting-ethics dilemma using the core principles

Lesson Plans

7.1 · C20

Cross-Cultural Pragmatics — Politeness, Taboo & Humor

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • place a request, refusal, criticism or disagreement on a directness dial and move it up or down on purpose, using the grammar of indirectness (I don't suppose you could…? · Would there be any chance of…? · I was wondering whether…)
  • name the four politeness strategies (bald on record, positive, negative, off record) and explain which one a speaker chose and why
  • decode a softener as content (That would be quite challenging · I'll see what I can do) and check its meaning without accusing: When you said…, did you mean…?
  • discuss taboo as a linguistic phenomenon: place words on a dysphemism–neutral–euphemism ladder, explain minced oaths and the euphemism treadmill, and raise a sensitive topic with signal → soften → defer
  • recognize why irony, self-deprecation, banter and understatement misfire across a gap, and repair a misfire lightly: notice, clarify, realign
Materials
  • Workbook 7.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Critical-Incident Clinic frame, self-check)
  • Slides 3–8 · Picture "moments" cards (coffee, cake, umbrella, email) · politeness-strategy, repair and euphemism flashcards · Critical-Incident Clinic case cards (8) + analysis frames · euphemism ladder strips · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 7.1-A (Maya and Jun: a misunderstanding, repaired) and 7.1-B (what do they really mean?)

Left: the directness dial. Send me the file today. → Could you send it today? → I was wondering whether you could… → I don't suppose there's any chance of…? · Refusal: No, that won't work. ↔ That would be quite challenging. An indirect refusal is still a refusal. Strategies: bald on record · positive (we, compliments, in-group) · negative (hedge, apologize, give options) · off record (hint).

Center: delicate ground. Ladder: kicked the bucket · died · passed away (dysphemism → neutral → euphemism). Minced oaths: darn · gosh · heck. Treadmill: toilet → bathroom → restroom. Signal (May I ask something a little personal?) → soften (Some people find…) → defer (Say as much or as little as you like.)

Right: humor and repair. irony · self-deprecation · banter · understatement. Repair: notice (Did I get the wrong idea? / Have I got the wrong end of the stick?) → clarify (When you said…, did you mean…?) → realign (That's on me. Let's start again from there.) Default: assume good faith.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: fine points relay7S–SReview C19 (6.3, fine points of usage and punctuation). Board five "almost right" sentences ("Less people came than we hoped." · "Between you and I, the plan is weak." · "Each of the proposals have merit." · "I wish it was otherwise." · "Walking into the room, the lights went out."). Pairs fix and justify each in 3 minutes (fewer · you and me · has · were · As I walked in…). Then twist: "Which of these could you say to a senior colleague without sounding pedantic if you corrected them?" This opens today's theme: correct is not the same as well received.
Lead-in: same words, different rules5T–SSlide 3 (four pictures: coffee, cake, umbrella, email). For each, one line: "So, how much do you pay in rent?" (coffee) · "You made this? It's amazing!" / "Oh, it's nothing." (cake) · "Lovely weather!" in a downpour (umbrella) · "Send me the figures by 3." (email). Ask: "Is any of these ungrammatical? (No.) Could any of them go wrong? How?" Elicit that the risk is in the rules for use, not the words. Set the culture-safe rules from slide 2: settings, not nationalities.
Meaning: directness and politeness strategies10T–S → S–SSlide 4: the directness dial for four speech acts (request, refusal, criticism, disagreement). Key point: neither end is "more polite" in the abstract; each is polite by its own rules. Slide 5: the four politeness strategies with one example each. Pairs get 3 minutes to sort six strategy flashcard examples. CCQs (below). Plant the C2 insight: "I'll see what I can do" can be a complete, courteous no.
Small talk, compliments, silence · taboo as language9T–S → S–SSlide 6. Quickly: safe and risky small-talk topics, compliment acceptance vs deflection, silence as vacuum vs resource, and the default: match, then observe. Then taboo as a linguistic phenomenon: pairs order three euphemism ladder strips (kicked the bucket · died · passed away; got the boot · was fired · was let go; the john · the bathroom · the facilities) and say where each belongs. Introduce minced oaths (darn, gosh, heck, shoot) and the euphemism treadmill. Finish with signal → soften → defer for raising a sensitive topic.
Humor and repair7T–SSlide 7. Four kinds of humor and why each can fail (a literal listener hears irony as a false statement; self-deprecation read as real low status; banter without a shared frame lands as a real jab; understatement underestimated). "What travels best?" (Warm, situational, gentle, self-directed.) Then the repair toolkit in three phases: notice, clarify, realign. CCQs.
Drill6T–S → SThree rounds (see Drill script): dial it down, decode and check, repair it. Choral for the formulaic frames (with the tentative intonation), then individual.
Listening: a misunderstanding, repaired7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 7.1-A (Maya and Jun) twice, two voices if possible. First listening: "What deadline do they agree in the end?" (Monday.) Second: learners answer the "what did Jun mean" questions and complete the five repair phrases. Pairs compare. Ask: "Who adapts? (Both. The repair is mutual.)"
Controlled practice7S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (label the strategy) and Ex 3 (decode the hedge and write a check) alone, then compare in pairs. Board-check only disagreements: most will be about Ex 2 items 3 and 8, which is good discussion. Ex 4 (euphemism ladders) and Ex 5 (humor repairs) are homework unless a pair finishes early.
Freer: Critical-Incident Clinic22S–S → T–SSlide 8. Groups of 3 get one invented critical-incident card (a "yes" that wasn't, a silent video call, a deflected compliment, a welcome-lunch question, banter in week one, a blunt email, irony about the rain, "I'll think about it"). Round 1, Diagnose (8 min): on the analysis frame, Describe only what was said and done (no adjectives about people), Interpret with at least two good-faith readings for each person, Evaluate which pragmatic dimension was in play (directness, silence, compliment, taboo/small talk, humor). Round 2, Bridge (7 min): script the repair or the pre-emptive move: at least one softener, one clarifying check and one realigning line; rehearse it as a two-person scene. Round 3, Perform and review (7 min): groups pair up, perform their bridge for each other, and run the good-faith test on each other's analysis (see below), then one strength and one suggestion, quoting the words. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction in four columns. Accuracy, for example: "I don't suppose you can send…" (needs could) · "Please feel free to not answer" (fine, but not to answer is the fixed frame) · "Have I got the wrong side of the stick?" Range: "Would there be any chance of…," "I'll leave it to you to say as much or as little as you like." Appropriacy: a clarifying check that sounded like an accusation ("So you lied when you said yes?"). Effect: which bridge made the other person visibly relax?
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.1 on screen; point at a Listen button beside the dialogue. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 10 + 9 + 7 + 6 + 7 + 7 + 22 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: pragmatics and the directness dial. Pragmatics is the meaning that isn't in the words but in how, when and to whom they are said. The same act (a request, a refusal) can be done at many points on a dial from bald to oblique; different settings expect different points, and the listener decodes accordingly.

"I don't suppose there's any chance of the file today?" Is it a question about my beliefs? (No: it's a request.) Strong or gentle? (Very gentle.) Why the negative? (It assumes the answer might be no, so the listener isn't cornered.) · "That would be quite challenging." Is it a yes with a warning, or a no? (In many settings, a courteous no.) What happens if you hear it as "doable"? (You commit to something that won't happen, like Maya.) · Is the direct end rude? (Not necessarily: in some settings it's honest and efficient.)

Meaning: politeness strategies. A useful research framework (Brown and Levinson) treats requests, refusals and criticism as face-threatening acts, with four ways to perform them: bald on record (direct, no softening: urgent or close relationships), positive politeness (closeness: we, let's, compliments, shared ground), negative politeness (distance and deference: hedges, apologies, options, I don't suppose…) and off record (a hint the listener must infer: It's chilly in here). The frame isn't in the online lesson; it gives C2 learners the metalanguage to explain their choices.

"Watch out, the step!" Softened? (No.) Rude? (No: it's urgent.) Strategy? (Bald on record.) · "You're a genius with spreadsheets. Can we look at this together?" What's the compliment doing? (Building closeness before the ask: positive politeness.) · "Gosh, it's chilly in here." Is it about the temperature? (Maybe: it could be a hint to close the window.) Can the speaker deny it was a request? (Yes: that's why it's off record.)

Meaning: taboo as language. Every language fences off some topics (death, money, the body, illness, bodily functions). Languages respond with euphemisms (softer words: passed away), dysphemisms (deliberately harsher or jokier words: kicked the bucket) and minced oaths (disguised swear words: darn, gosh, heck, shoot). Euphemisms wear out: as a polite word takes on the associations of the thing it names, a new one replaces it (the euphemism treadmill: toilet → bathroom → restroom). We study this as language, with mild examples only.

"She passed away last spring." Softer or harder than "died"? (Softer.) Where would you use it? (A condolence card, a conversation with the family.) "He kicked the bucket." When might someone say that? (Joking, about someone not close, or to lighten a heavy moment; risky in front of family.) · "Oh, shoot!" Did the speaker swear? (No: it's a mild substitute, a minced oath.) · Why do English speakers say "restroom" in a restaurant, when nobody rests there? (It's a euphemism; the older word felt too direct.)

Meaning: humor and repair. Humor depends on a shared frame; across a gap it's the first thing to fail, and a failed joke can look like an insult. The skill is less the joke than the light repair.

A new colleague arrives late on day one. "Working part-time already?" Is it a joke? (For the speaker, yes: teasing.) What might she hear? (A real criticism.) Why? (No shared history yet: banter needs a relationship.) · "Sorry, that was meant as a joke, and not a very good one." Long or short? (Short.) Do you explain why it was funny? (No: drop it and move on.) · "Have I got the wrong end of the stick?" British or American flavor? (British. American: "Did I get the wrong idea?" / "Did I misunderstand?")

Form.

Negative + modal questionI don't suppose + could / wouldI don't suppose you could take another look? · I don't suppose there's any chance of…?
Distancingpast / progressive / wouldI was wondering whether… · Would there be any chance of…? · Might it be possible to…?
Conditional cushionIf + it's not too much trouble / you have a momentIf it's not too much trouble, could you…?
Hedged disagreementconcede + counter + open doorI see where you're coming from, but… · I see it slightly differently, though I may be missing something.
Clarifying checkWhen you said X, did you mean Y? · Just so I don't assume, are you saying…?When you said "we'll see," did you mean it's unlikely?
Offering an exitfeel free not to + verb · leave it to youPlease feel free not to answer. · Tell me if I'm straying into something you'd rather not discuss.
Repairnotice · clarify · realignI think I may have misread that. · Let me play that back to you… · That's on me. No harm done.

Pronunciation. Tentative frames only work with a tentative tune. Model a slow start and a fall-rise at the end of softened requests and hedges: "I don't supPOSE you could…?" ↘↗, "That would be quite CHALlenging…" ↘↗ (the fall-rise signals "there's more I'm not saying"). A flat or fast delivery turns the same words into sarcasm. In repair phrases, keep the pitch low and the pace unhurried: "That's on ME." A light laugh-voice on "and not a very good one" signals the joke is dropped. Linking: I don't suppose /aɪ ˈdəʊn(t) səˈpəʊz/ (American /doʊn(t) səˈpoʊz/), with the t nearly silent.

Drill script
  1. Dial it down (choral first, fall-rise tune): T: "Send me the file." → S: "I don't suppose you could send me the file?" T: "Move your bag." → "If it's not too much trouble, could you move your bag?" T: "Check the figures again." → "I was wondering whether you might check the figures again." T: "I disagree." → "I see it slightly differently, though I may be missing something."
  2. Decode and check: T says a hedge; S gives the likely meaning, then a check. T: "I'll see what I can do." → S: "Probably no. When you say you'll see, do you mean it's unlikely?" T: "It's an interesting idea." → "Maybe not keen. Can I check what you mean by interesting?" T: "We'll have to see." → "Uncertain or no. Just so I don't assume, are you saying it probably won't happen?"
  3. Repair it: T describes a misfire; S repairs. T: "Your joke fell flat." → S: "Sorry, that was meant as a joke, and not a very good one." T: "You talked over your colleague." → "That's on me for talking over you; let's back up." T: "Your question was too personal." → "Please tell me if I'm straying into something you'd rather not discuss."
  4. Up the ladder (euphemism): T: "died" → S: "passed away." T: "fired" → "let go." T: "old people" → "older adults / seniors." T: "the toilet" → "the restroom / the facilities." Then reverse one for humor: T: "passed away" → S (jokey, informal only): "kicked the bucket." Ask: "Where would that be risky?"
Critical-Incident Clinic — how to run it
  • What a critical incident is. A short, invented, true-to-life moment where two people behaved reasonably by their own rules and still misread each other. The cards give names and settings only, never nationalities. If a group asks "Where is he from?", answer: "It doesn't matter, and it wouldn't tell you what he meant. Work from what he said."
  • Groups of 3, with three roles: scribe (writes on the frame), voice of person 1 and voice of person 2 (each argues for the good-faith reading of their person). Give one dominant speaker the extra job of good-faith auditor: they may only ask questions until two readings are written.
  • Round 1, Diagnose (8 min): Describe (only observable words and actions: "Ravi said 'Yes, yes, I understand'", not "Ravi was evasive") · Interpret (at least two good-faith readings for each person) · Evaluate (which dimension: directness, silence, compliment, small talk/taboo, humor; what each person was probably trying to do).
  • Round 2, Bridge (7 min): write the exact words that would repair the moment afterward, or pre-empt it. Checklist: ☐ a softener ☐ a clarifying check (When you said…, did you mean…?) ☐ a realigning line (That's on me / We'll get the hang of each other) ☐ responsibility taken, not assigned. Rehearse it as a two-person scene; mind the tune.
  • Round 3, Perform and review (7 min): two groups pair up. Each performs its bridge (60–90 seconds) and reads its Interpret box. The other group runs the good-faith test: "Could both people in the incident read this analysis and feel fairly described?" Flag any sentence that judges a person or a group ("typical," "they always"). Then one strength and one suggestion, quoting the words.
  • Share: one bridge is performed for the whole class. Ask: "What did the repair cost the person who made it? (Very little.) What did it save?"
ICQs before the Clinic
Where are the people in the story from? (It doesn't say, and it doesn't matter.) In "Describe," can you write "he was rude"? (No: only what was said and done.) How many readings for each person? (At least two, both in good faith.) What three things must your bridge include? (A softener, a clarifying check, a realigning line.) In the review, what's the question? (Could both people feel fairly described?) Can you say "Pass" or use a new identity card? (Yes.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some very fluent speakers write far less easily than they speak (often learners with interrupted schooling or literacy in a non-Latin script). Give them the case card read aloud by a partner and a bridge strip of six ready frames to choose from (I think I may have misread that · When you said…, did you mean…? · Just so I don't assume… · That's on me · No harm done · Let's start again from there). They take a voice role in Round 1 (arguing a reading aloud) rather than the scribe role, and perform the bridge. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–5, Ex 3 items 1–3. Target: one good-faith reading said aloud, and one complete notice → clarify → realign repair performed with the right tune.

Stretch (toward professional mastery)

Metapragmatic commentary. Ask strong learners to take a real, public, non-personal text (a company's apology email, a customer-service reply, an out-of-office message) and annotate every politeness strategy in it, then rewrite it twice: one notch more direct, one notch more indirect, and say which reader each version would suit. In the Clinic, their bridge must also include an off-record move that the other person could safely ignore. Ask them to explain in two sentences why a strategy that works with one colleague could backfire with another, without naming any group.

L1 watch-outs (language transfer, not national character)
  • Answering negative questions: speakers of Japanese, Korean, Chinese and some other languages often answer the proposition: "You don't mind? — Yes" (meaning "Yes, that's right, I don't mind"). In English this reads as the opposite. A pure transfer of grammar that looks like a pragmatic clash. Teach: No, not at all / No, I don't mind.
  • Imperative + please as neutral: in Russian, Polish, Czech, Arabic and others, a polite imperative is ordinary courtesy, so "Please send me the report today" feels soft to the speaker and firm to many English listeners. Offer the question and distancing forms.
  • Present-tense requests: Spanish, Portuguese and Italian ¿Me pasas…? / Me passa…? → "You pass me the salt?" and "You send me the file?" Tie it to Could you…? / Would you mind…?
  • "I want" and "must": Spanish quiero, Arabic ureed, German müssen, French il faut can surface as "I want that you…" or "You must…", far stronger in English than intended. At C2 these are fossilized; flag them privately.
  • "Please feel free to not answer": split infinitives are fine in English, but the fixed courtesy frame is feel free not to. Also watch the invented "Please feel free for not answering."
  • False-friend politeness words: Spanish constipado (= has a cold) used for "constipated" can land a speaker in exactly the embarrassing territory euphemism avoids; French sensible (= sensitive); German eventuell (= possibly) → "I'll eventually come" meaning "I might come." Keep a board corner for them; they return in C22.
Homework

Online lesson 7.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_19.html. Listen to the Maya and Jun dialogue with the Listen buttons and shadow it, exaggerating the softening tones; say the five model sentences back; do Exercises 1–2, the Quick Quiz and the "Bridge a Real Difference" project (a real difference, an invented one, or the one on a new identity card: all three are equally good, and nobody has to describe their own culture). Workbook 7.1: Exercises 4 and 5, "My new words" and the journal (one moment when a pragmatic difference surfaced, described as a situation, not a stereotype, and the one line you'd use now).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B It may well be a polite "no": treat the hedge as the answer · Q2 C Lightly acknowledge it ("Sorry, that was meant as a joke") and move on · Q3 (new) B "Oh, enough to keep me in coffee! How are you finding the new office so far?" Listening (Ex 1): 1 Thursday isn't realistic (an indirect refusal) · 2 "I'll certainly do my best" · 3 Jun went quiet (the silence) · 4 end · talked · on · back · hang · 5 Monday.

7.2 · C21

Diplomacy, Negotiation & High-Stakes Communication

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • disagree, refuse and hold a line without hostility, using acknowledge-then-counter, downgraded verdicts and regretful hedges (I take your point; my only concern is… · I have one or two reservations · I'm afraid I can't quite see how…)
  • separate positions (what someone says they want) from interests (why they want it), know their own BATNA (best alternative to a negotiated agreement) and walk-away point, and explore the other side's interests with open questions
  • make conditional trades with remote conditionals and conditional connectors (If you were able to…, then we could… · provided that · as long as · on condition that · What if we were to…?) and build a package rather than haggle one issue at a time
  • protect the other side's face: blame the situation, credit their reasoning, offer a golden bridge, move from "you vs me" to "us vs the problem"
  • deliver a difficult message that is clear and early, then cushioned (signpost → state → explain → look forward), and stay calm and assertive under pressure with a holding phrase
Materials
  • Workbook 7.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Negotiation prep sheet, self-check)
  • Slides 9–13 · Negotiation-move flashcards · picture cards (bill, notebook) · Negotiation Simulation: scenario 1 The Hall and scenario 2 The Coffee Cart (confidential A and B briefs in separate envelopes), prep sheets, observer checklists · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 7.2-A (Rosa and Daniel: a delicate negotiation) and 7.2-B (dictation)

Left: firm on substance, warm on person. You're wrong. → I appreciate where you're coming from, but I see it differently. · That's a bad idea. → I have one or two reservations. · No. → I'm not sure we'll be able to do that, I'm afraid. · We'll never agree. → We may have to agree to differ. · You didn't deliver. → Things didn't quite go to plan.

Center: the machinery. POSITION = what they say (Thursday mornings or nothing) · INTEREST = why (clients need a big screen) · BATNA = best alternative if no deal (hotel room at $150) · walk-away point. Moves: open → explore (What matters most to you here?) → propose conditionally: If you were able to X, then we could Y · provided that · as long as · What if we were to…? → package → close (Shall we say…?)

Right: face and hard news. Blame the situation (The timing has been against us) · Credit the reasoning (Your logic was sound given what we knew) · Golden bridge (With the new figures in, it'd be completely reasonable to revisit this) · Us vs the problem. Cushion: signpost → STATE → explain → forward. Holding phrase: Let me make sure I understand your concern.

Hold up five hedge flashcards one at a time (That would be quite challenging · I'll see what I can do · It's an interesting idea · We'll have to see · Mm, maybe next time). Pairs say the likely meaning and a clarifying check. Then two euphemism ladders from memory. Quick round: one learner reads their homework bridge line (invented incidents welcome); the class names the three phases. Collect journals, or glance at them.
Lead-in: the price is what it is5T–SSlide 9. Board: "Our costs have gone up. We need a higher price." Ask: "Can you change the content? (No.) What can you change? (The tone, the framing, the offer around it.)" Read the brutal and the diplomatic versions of one line aloud. "Today: firm on substance, warm on person, and a negotiation where the best deal isn't a split down the middle."
Meaning/Form: diplomatic language and face10T–S → S–SSlide 9 table (blunt → diplomatic → what the softening does). Highlight three shapes: acknowledge-then-counter, the downgraded verdict, the regretful hedge. Warn about weaponized courtesy (with respect as a sneer). Then face-saving (slide 11, lower half): blame the situation, credit the reasoning, the golden bridge, "us vs the problem". Pairs turn three blunt workplace lines into diplomatic ones in 2 minutes. CCQs.
Negotiation: positions, interests, BATNA, the conditional trade10T–SSlide 10: position vs interest with the meeting-room example (two teams, one room); elicit two interests behind each position; then BATNA (the best thing you can do if there's no deal) and why it sets your walk-away point: "Never accept a deal worse than your BATNA; never reveal it as a threat." Slide 11: the moves (open, explore, propose conditionally, trade, package, close) and the grammar of the conditional trade: remote If you were able to… vs open If you can…; provided that, as long as, on condition that, supposing, What if we were to…? (3% for twelve months, rate frozen, in writing today.) Second: Ex 1 questions (the trade, the numbers, Daniel's real interest). Pairs compare. Ask: "Who won? (Both: the deal was built on interests.)"
