Freer: Negotiation Simulation28S–S → T–SSlide 13. Two invented scenarios, run side by side (neighboring groups get different ones): The Hall (Harbor Lane Community Center vs Blue Heron Youth Robotics Club: shared use of a hall) and The Coffee Cart (Maplewood Public Library vs Fernside Coffee Co-op: a cart at the library entrance). Each side has a confidential brief with its interests, a points table and its BATNA. Prep (8 min): same-side pairs fill in the prep sheet: interests ranked, BATNA, walk-away point, three conditional offers, one face-saving line, one holding phrase. Negotiate (12 min): A-side vs B-side; the observer uses the checklist; a written deal on the deal sheet, or an honest "agree to differ." Debrief (8 min): each side runs the "Can they sell it upstairs?" test (one sentence each: how will you present this to your board?), then sides reveal their points and compare with their BATNA; observers give one strength and one suggestion, quoting a line. Whole class: which groups found the package where both sides scored high? ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Accuracy: "If you would be able to…" · "provided you will sign" (provided you sign) · "agree to disagree" used as a door slam. Range: "Where might there be room to move?", "What if we were to…?", "Your logic was sound given what we knew." Appropriacy: a BATNA revealed as a threat; "with respect" said through the teeth. Effect: the moment the other side's shoulders dropped, and what caused it.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.2 and a Listen button beside the Rosa and Daniel dialogue. Set the homework (below). Learners tick "I can…".
Total907 + 5 + 10 + 10 + 6 + 7 + 7 + 28 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: diplomatic language. The content may be fixed (the answer is no, the price is higher); the tone is yours. Diplomatic language lowers the temperature so the other person can move without feeling cornered. It is honest: it changes the framing, never the truth.

"I have one or two reservations about that approach." Does she like the approach? (Not really.) Is it a verdict or a concern? (A concern: downgraded.) Why downgrade? (It leaves the other person room to improve it.) · "With respect, we may have to agree to differ." Has anyone lost? (No.) Is the discussion over? (On this point, yes.) · "With RESpect…" said slowly through the teeth: diplomatic? (No: it's contempt in a velvet glove. The words only work if the respect is real.)

Meaning: positions, interests and BATNA. A position is the demand on the table; an interest is the need underneath it. Two positions can be incompatible while the interests fit together. The BATNA (Fisher and Ury, Getting to Yes) is what you'll do if no agreement is reached; it tells you when to walk away. The online lesson gestures at this ("naming your alternative without threat"); the kit makes it explicit.

"We need the big room every Thursday morning." Position or interest? (Position.) Ask "why?": "Our clients have to see the demo on a large screen." (Interest.) Could another room with a big screen work? (Maybe: interests open options.) · "If this doesn't work out, we'll book the hotel room at $150 a morning." What is that? (Their BATNA.) Should they accept a deal that costs them $200 a morning? (No: it's worse than the BATNA.) Should they say "Give us the room or we go to the hotel"? (No: it's a threat, and it closes the door.)

Meaning: the conditional trade and face. Every concession is tied to a return, so nothing is given away for nothing and the talk moves toward a package. Face = a person's dignity and public self-image; a person who can move without losing face will move.

"If you were able to commit to twelve months, then we could hold the rate." Is Rosa promising to hold the rate? (Only if Daniel commits.) Why "were able to" and not "can"? (More tentative: it offers without pushing.) · "With the new figures in, it'd be completely reasonable to revisit this." Was the other person wrong before? (No: the situation changed.) What does it give them? (A dignified way to change position: a golden bridge.) · "You keep missing the deadline" vs "How do we get the timeline back on track?" Which invites a defense? (The first.) Which invites a solution? (The second.)

Form.

Acknowledge → counterI appreciate / take / see + your point / where you're coming from, but…I take your point; my only concern is the timeline.
Remote conditional tradeIf you were able to / were to + verb, (then) we could / would + verbIf you were to extend the deadline, we could absorb the extra scope.
Conditional connectorsprovided (that) / as long as / on condition that + present simple (not will)We could move on price, provided the timeline shifts.
Hypothetical proposalsWhat if we were to…? · Supposing we…, would you…? · How would you feel about…?Supposing we added a Saturday, would you consider a longer term?
Face-saving framingsituation as subject · agentless · shared weThe timing has been against us. · Things didn't quite go to plan. · I don't think either of us saw this coming.
ClosingShall we say…? · So, to recap… · I'll put it in writing.Shall we say three percent, twelve months, rate frozen?

Pronunciation. In negotiation, pace and pitch carry authority. Model a slower pace, lower pitch and falling tones on the substance ("the budget is locked ↘"), with rising or fall-rise tones only where you invite response ("What matters most to you here? ↗"). In conditional trades, put contrastive stress on the two sides of the exchange: "If YOU could move on the TIMEline, then WE could look again at the PRICE." In agree to DIFfer the stress is on differ; in reSERvations on the third syllable. For holding phrases, a deliberate pause before speaking ("Let me take a moment… before I respond to that") signals calm, not weakness.

Drill script
  1. Diplomatic swap (calm, even pace): T: "You're wrong." → S: "I appreciate where you're coming from, but I see it differently." T: "No." → "I'm afraid we won't be able to do that." T: "That's a bad idea." → "I have one or two reservations about that approach." T: "You failed." → "Things didn't quite go to plan on this occasion." T: "We'll never agree." → "With respect, we may have to agree to differ on this one."
  2. Make it a trade: T gives two halves; S joins them with the cue. T: "you pay a deposit / we deliver early (provided)" → S: "We could deliver early, provided you pay a deposit." T: "you commit to twelve months / we hold the price (If you were able to)" → "If you were able to commit to twelve months, then we could hold the price." T: "we add Saturdays / you sign for a year (What if)" → "What if we were to add Saturdays, and you signed for a year?"
  3. Explore, don't argue: T states a position; S asks for the interest. T: "We need it by Friday." → S: "Help me understand what's driving Friday." T: "We won't pay more than $50." → "What matters most to you in the budget?" T: "Thursday or nothing." → "What would Thursday give you that another day wouldn't?"
  4. Hold the line (T pushes; S stays calm): T (sharply): "This is unacceptable!" → S (slowly): "Let me make sure I understand your concern." T: "Just give me a yes or no!" → "I'd like to take a moment before I respond, so I give that a fair answer."
Negotiation Simulation — how to run it
  • Set-up. Groups of 5 (A-pair, B-pair, observer) or 3 (A, B, observer). Neighboring groups get different scenarios. Hand each side its confidential brief in an envelope. Say it twice: "Your points table is secret. Don't show it. Don't compare your points with the other side's: the tables measure different things."
  • The design. Both scenarios have five issues. Some matter a lot to one side and little to the other (in The Hall: storage matters hugely to the Robotics Club and costs the Center little; cleaning matters to the Center and costs the Club little). Groups that haggle issue by issue and split every difference land around the middle; groups that explore interests and trade across issues find a package in which both sides score well above their BATNA. Don't tell them this in advance; it's the debrief.
  • Prep (8 min): same-side pairs fill in the prep sheet (Workbook 7.2): position, interests ranked, BATNA, walk-away point, the other side's likely interests, three conditional offers, one face-saving line, one holding phrase. Circulate and ask each pair: "What's your BATNA worth? So what's the worst deal you'll accept?"
  • Negotiate (12 min): open with a position, not an ultimatum. The observer ticks the checklist and writes down one quote for each move. At minute 10, call "two minutes": they must close on the deal sheet (all five issues filled in) or agree to differ honestly.
  • Debrief (8 min): (1) The "Can they sell it upstairs?" test: each side says in one sentence how it will present the deal to its board or members ("We've secured a year of steady income and the club will handle cleaning"). If a side can't say it with a straight face, face wasn't saved. (2) Sides reveal their points and compare them with their BATNA. (3) Observer: one strength, one suggestion, quoting. (4) Whole class, 2 minutes: board the scores; ask the highest-scoring groups what they traded.
  • If a group deadlocks: that's a legitimate outcome if both sides' BATNAs really are better. Ask them to close well: "We may have to agree to differ; let's keep the door open for the spring."
ICQs before the simulation
Are you playing yourself? (No, the brief.) Can you show your points table? (No.) Should you compare points with the other side? (No: they measure different things.) What's your BATNA? (What we do if there's no deal.) Do you accept a deal worse than your BATNA? (No.) Do you announce your BATNA as a threat? (No.) What grammar do you use for offers? (If you were able to…, then we could… / provided that.) What must be on the deal sheet at the end? (All five issues, or "agree to differ.") What's the test? (Can each side sell it upstairs?) Can you say "Pass"? (Yes.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Pair the learner with a stronger reader on the same side, so the brief is read aloud and discussed. Give a six-line move card: "Ideally, we'd be looking at… / What matters most to you here? / If you were able to…, then we could… / We could move on…, provided… / I take your point; my only concern is… / Shall we say…?" and let them be the voice of the pair while the partner keeps the notes. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 4 items 1–3. Target: one complete conditional trade and one acknowledge-then-counter, said aloud in the simulation.

Stretch (toward professional mastery)

Packages and anchors. Teach multiple equivalent simultaneous offers: put two or three packages on the table at once ("We could do $55 with the storeroom, or $45 with the cabinet: which works better for you?"), then read the other side's choice as information about their interests. Discuss anchoring (the first number shapes the range) and how to re-anchor gracefully ("That's a long way from where we'd need to be; let me explain our costs"). After the simulation, they write a 120-word deal memo to their board: clear decision first, then the reasons, framed so the board's face is saved too.

L1 watch-outs
  • Conditional connectors + will: "provided you will sign," "as long as you will pay," "on condition that you will…" (common transfer from future-marked languages, including French, Spanish, Portuguese and Italian, where the subjunctive or future appears after similar connectors). English uses the present simple: provided you sign.
  • "If you would be able to…": learners over-use would in both clauses. Teach the remote pattern: If you were able to…, we could… (though If you would as a polite request, "If you'd sign here," is fine).
  • "Agree to disagree" as a door slam: learners from exam-trained systems deploy it to end a discussion. The idiom is courteous only when it keeps the door open; at C2, prefer We may have to agree to differ on this one, for now.
  • Modal strength: German müssen, Russian нужно, French devoir → "You must lower the price," which sounds like an order in English negotiation. Offer We'd need…, It would help us if…
  • False friends in the deal: Spanish/Portuguese discutir and Italian discutere (often "argue" as well as "discuss"): "We discussed" may be heard as calmer than the speaker meant, or vice versa; French compromis and Spanish compromiso (= commitment as well as compromise); German Konsequenz (= consistency, also consequence). A compromise in English can sound like both sides losing; a package or solution doesn't.
  • Numbers under pressure: thirteen/thirty, fifteen/fifty stress (thirTEEN / THIRty); percentages ("three percent," not "three per cents"). Always confirm figures in writing: Just to confirm, that's fifty, five-zero?
Homework

Online lesson 7.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_20.html. Shadow the Rosa and Daniel dialogue with the Listen buttons, matching its calm, even pace; say the five model sentences back; do Exercises 1–2, the Quick Quiz and the "High-Stakes Conversation" project (script both sides of an invented negotiation or difficult message: a price, a schedule, a shared resource; the new identity cards each have one). Workbook 7.2: Exercise 5 (the cushioned message), "My new words" and the journal (a high-stakes conversation, real, invented or from a card, rewritten from "you vs me" to "us vs the problem").

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A "If you were able to move the deadline, then we could look again at scope." · Q2 C The listener may not realize they were turned down and feel misled later · Q3 (new) B your best alternative if no agreement is reached. Listening (Ex 1): 1 material costs have risen across the whole sector · 2 the budget is more or less locked · 3 commit · could · 4 10% · 3% · 5 predictability / something he can defend to his own bosses (not having the same conversation in six months) · 6 put it in writing (today).

7.3 · C22

Working Between Languages — Translation & Interpreting Awareness

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain why word-for-word translation fails (idiom, collocation, grammar) and treat the idea, not the word, as the unit of translation
  • spot and warn against false friends, and keep a personal false-friend list
  • convey an untranslatable or culture-bound word with the paraphrase pattern (signal → literal image → plain meaning → feeling) and the mediating frames (The closest equivalent would be… · It literally means…, but we'd actually say… · The nearest we get in English is…, though that loses…)
  • distinguish translation, consecutive and simultaneous interpreting, and sight translation, and carry register and tone across the switch
  • reason through an interpreting-ethics dilemma with the core principles: accuracy and completeness, impartiality, confidentiality, transparency, role boundaries and the first-person convention, and know when a professional interpreter is needed
Materials
  • Workbook 7.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Lab record, self-check)
  • Slides 14–18 · Picture "everyday mediation" cards (menu, phone, book, passport) · false-friend and mediating-frame flashcards · Translation & Interpreting Lab: 8 ethics dilemma cards, 2 register rescue cards, 3 plain-English sight texts, the untranslatables gallery · learners' own short texts (optional) · New identity cards · Bridge It! boards (early finishers)
  • Listening scripts 7.3-A (Ana and Tom: sobremesa) and 7.3-B (the helpful volunteer: spot the ethics breaches)

Left: why word-for-word fails. IDIOM: Je te tiens au courant ≠ "I hold you in the current" → I'll keep you posted. COLLOCATION: make a decision / take a photo. GRAMMAR: I have 30 years → I'm 30. False friends: embarazada = pregnant · actuellement = currently · Gift = poison · libreria = bookstore · eventuell = possibly. Unit = the idea.

Center: the paraphrase pattern. signal (There isn't a single English word for it, but…) → literal image (It literally means "over the table"…) → plain meaning (the talk after a meal) → feeling (not being in a hurry). Frames: The closest equivalent would be… · Think of it as a kind of… · The nearest we get is…, though that loses…

Right: between speakers. Translation (written, time) · Interpreting: consecutive (chunks) / simultaneous (booth) / sight translation (text → speech). Register survives the crossing. Ethics: accurate & complete · impartial · confidential · transparent ("Interpreter speaking: may I check a word?") · first person ("I have two children") · know your limits.

Stage plan

Warmer: trade it!7S–SReview C21. Board four blunt demands ("Pay by Friday." · "Lower the fee." · "Give us Saturdays." · "Sign for a year."). Pairs turn each into a conditional trade with a different connector (If you were able to… · provided · as long as · What if we were to…?), then add one face-saving line to the hardest. One pair reads its best trade; the class checks the tense after provided. Ask one or two groups: "Could you sell your deal upstairs?"
Lead-in: I hold you in the current4T–SSlide 14 (menu, phone, book, passport): "When did you last mediate between languages: glossing a menu, checking a phone translation, explaining a word to a friend?" (Answers voluntary; "never" is fine.) Board: "I hold you in the current." "Grammatical? (Yes.) Meaningful? (No.) It's French Je te tiens au courant. What would an English speaker say?" (I'll keep you posted.) "Today: meaning, not words."
Meaning: why word-for-word fails · false friends10T–S → S–SSlide 15. The three mismatches (idiom, collocation, grammar) with one example each; "machine translation makes exactly this mistake, often." Then false friends: the six from the online lesson on the slide; pairs have 2 minutes to add any they know between any two languages (voluntary; English-only examples like eventually / possibly count). Board them in a "danger map" corner. CCQs.
Meaning: the untranslatable and the paraphrase pattern10T–SSlide 16. hygge, saudade, Schadenfreude, sobremesa: the words aren't untranslatable, they're un-one-word-able. Model the pattern with sobremesa, then elicit it for tsundoku from a gallery card. Point out the reverse direction too: English words other languages borrow or struggle with (cozy, awkward, binge-watch, brunch). Mediating frames on the board. CCQs.
Translation vs interpreting · ethics8T–SSlide 17. Translation (written, time to craft) vs interpreting (live, real time): consecutive, simultaneous, and sight translation (reading a written text aloud in another language on the spot). Register and tone must survive. Then the core ethics principles found in professional interpreter codes: accuracy and completeness, impartiality, confidentiality, transparency, role boundaries, the first-person convention. Say plainly: in many public services in the U.S. (hospitals, courts, schools) people have a right to a professional interpreter, and children should not be asked to interpret in high-stakes settings. Discuss in the abstract only.
Drill5T–S → STwo rounds (see Drill script): mediating frames, and first person. Choral for the frames with an easy, explanatory rhythm, then individual.
Listening: sobremesa5T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 7.3-A (Ana and Tom) once; learners map Ana's explanation onto the four steps of the pattern and note what Tom's "closest equivalent" loses. Pairs compare.
Controlled practice7S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (false friends) and Ex 3 (why word-for-word fails: label and fix) alone, then compare. Board-check disagreements. Ex 4 (paraphrase idioms) and Ex 5 (register and ethics) are homework unless a pair finishes early.
Freer: Translation & Interpreting Lab24S–S → T–SSlide 18. Part 1, Ethics and register (8 min): groups of 3 take two ethics dilemma cards (a volunteer interpreting at a parent–teacher conference, a rude remark at a community meeting, a joke that won't cross, a number you didn't catch…) and one register rescue card; for each dilemma they name the principle at stake and script what the interpreter should say. Then listen to Script 7.3-B as a class (2 min of the 8) and count the volunteer's breaches. Part 2, Mediate (13 min): same triads, roles mediator · listener · checker, three rounds of about 4 minutes. The mediator chooses: (a) sight-translate a short, simple text they brought, in any language they read, into natural spoken English; or (b) render one of the dense English sight texts into plain spoken English; or (c) convey an untranslatable word, their own or from the gallery. The listener then runs the "Explain it back" test; the checker notes one register-and-tone point and one frame used. Part 3, Share (3 min): two mediators repeat their best rendering for the class. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SFour columns. Accuracy: false friends that slipped out ("actually" for "currently," "eventually" for "possibly," "sensible" for "sensitive"), "It literally means…, but we'd actually say…" with the wrong tense. Range: "The nearest we get in English is…, though that loses…" Appropriacy: a rendering that flattened a friendly text into officialese (register loss), or an interpreter who "helpfully" added advice. Effect: which rendering made the listener smile or say "Oh, I get it"? Close the module with slide 19 (see Closing).
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.3 and a Listen button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick all three "I can…" lists.
Total907 + 4 + 10 + 10 + 8 + 5 + 5 + 7 + 24 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the unit of translation is the idea. A mediator first asks "What does this mean, and what is it doing here?" and only then looks for the natural target-language way to do the same job, which may share no words with the original.

"I hold you in the current." Is the grammar wrong? (No.) Is the meaning there? (No: it's a literal rendering of an idiom.) What job is the French doing? (Promising updates.) So in English? ("I'll keep you posted.") Same words? (None.) Same job? (Yes.) · "She did a mistake." Is it an idiom problem? (No: collocation. English makes mistakes.)

Meaning: false friends. Words that look or sound alike across languages but mean different things. They're dangerous because the sentence stays grammatical and the word feels right.

"Actuellement, je travaille à Lyon" → "Actually, I work in Lyon." What's wrong? (Actuellement means "currently.") Does the English sentence look wrong? (No, and that's the danger.) · Which false friends are most dangerous for you? (Only the ones between your languages: that's why the list must be personal.) · Are all similar words false friends? (No: most cognates are true friends. Suspect the too-convenient match; don't distrust them all.)

Meaning: the untranslatable. Culture-bound concepts can't be carried by one word, but they can be conveyed: give the category, then paint the situation and the feeling.

"Saudade is a kind of longing." Enough? (No: it's just the category.) What's missing? (The situation and the feeling: longing for someone loved and absent, nostalgia with an ache in it.) · "The closest we'd get is 'chatting over coffee after dinner,' but that loses the sense that it's a whole custom." What is Tom doing? (Offering an equivalent and flagging what it loses.) Is that a failure? (No: it's honest mediation.)

Meaning: translation, interpreting, ethics. Translation = written, with time to research and revise. Interpreting = spoken, in real time: consecutive (the speaker pauses; the interpreter renders each chunk), simultaneous (almost at the same time, usually from a booth) and sight translation (a written text read aloud in another language on the spot). Professional interpreters speak in the first person ("I have two children," not "She says she has two children") and step out of it transparently only to manage the conversation ("Interpreter speaking: may I ask the speaker to repeat the number?").

A speaker makes a rude remark. Should the interpreter soften it? (No: accuracy and completeness. The listener has a right to hear what was said, and in the same register.) · The parent asks the volunteer, "What would you do?" Should the volunteer answer? (No: impartiality and role boundaries. "I'm only here to interpret. You could ask the teacher.") · After the meeting, a neighbor asks what was said. (Confidential: say nothing.) · You didn't catch a number. Guess? (Never: ask, and tell both sides you're asking.) · A child interpreting at a hospital appointment: good idea? (No: that's what professional interpreters are for.)

Form.

Signal the gapThere isn't a single English word for it, but… · We don't really have a word for that in English.There isn't a single English word for hygge, but…
Literal vs realIt literally means…, but what we'd actually say is… · Word for word it's…, but…It literally means "over the table," but really it's the talk after a meal.
ApproximateThe closest equivalent would be… · The nearest we get in English is…, though that loses… · Roughly, it's the feeling of…The nearest we get is "cozy," though that loses the social side.
Describe useIt's the word we use when… · Think of it as a kind of… · You'd say it when…It's the word we use when you wear something for the first time.
WarnCareful with that word: it looks like…, but it really means…Careful: eventuell looks like "eventually," but it means "possibly."
Interpreter's voicefirst person for the speaker · third person only to manage: The interpreter would like to…"I have two children." · "Interpreter speaking: may I check one word?"

Pronunciation. Borrowed words: model them once and accept approximations; nobody is marked on Danish vowels. hygge /ˈhjuːɡə/ (often anglicized "HOO-guh"), Schadenfreude /ˈʃɑːdənfrɔɪdə/, saudade /saʊˈdɑːdə/ (Portuguese varieties differ), sobremesa /soβɾeˈmesa/. More important is the explanatory rhythm: a slight pause after the signal ("There isn't a single English word for it… | but"), stress on the contrast in it LITerally means…, but what we'd ACtually say…, and chunked, unhurried delivery in sight translation. Give learners time-buyers for live mediation: Let me put that another way… · What she's saying, roughly, is… (the second only in informal mediation, never in professional first-person interpreting).

Drill script
  1. Frame it (easy, explanatory rhythm): T: "hygge, no single word" → S: "There isn't a single English word for it, but the closest equivalent would be a kind of cozy contentment." T: "I hold you in the current" → "It literally means 'I hold you in the current,' but what we'd actually say is 'I'll keep you posted.'" T: "actuellement, trap" → "Careful with that word: it looks like 'actually,' but it really means 'currently.'" T: "saudade, feeling" → "Roughly, it's the feeling of longing for someone you love who isn't there anymore." T: "sobremesa, use" → "It's the word we use for the lingering talk at the table after a meal."
  2. First person, please: T reads a reported line; S renders it as an interpreter would. T: "She says she has two children." → S: "I have two children." T: "He's asking if the fee can be waived." → "Can the fee be waived?" T: "She says she's sorry she's late and the bus didn't come." → "I'm sorry I'm late. The bus didn't come." T: "He wants to know what time practice ends." → "What time does practice end?" Then T: "You missed a number." → S: "Interpreter speaking: could you repeat the number, please?"
Translation & Interpreting Lab — how to run it
  • Own languages are voluntary, always. Every part has an English-only route of equal value, and nobody is asked to translate for the class, teach a word or "represent" a language. Learners who share a language may choose to work together; nobody should be paired because of a shared language without asking.
  • Part 1, Ethics and register (8 min): each group takes two ethics dilemma cards and one register rescue card. For each dilemma: ☐ name the principle(s) at stake (accuracy/completeness · impartiality · confidentiality · transparency · role boundaries · competence) ☐ script the interpreter's exact words ☐ decide whether a professional interpreter should be there instead. For the register card: restore the original tone to a flattened rendering. Then, all together, Script 7.3-B: the teacher reads what was said and what the volunteer "interpreted"; groups count the breaches (there are six).
  • Part 2, Mediate (13 min): triads, three rounds, rotate roles. Mediator (about 2 minutes): option (a) sight translation of a short, simple, public text they brought (recipe, sign, label, children's news story), read aloud directly in natural English, not word by word; option (b) render a plain-English sight text (a dense English notice) into friendly spoken English for a neighbor; option (c) convey an untranslatable word (their own or from the gallery) with the paraphrase pattern. Listener: runs the "Explain it back" test: "So what you're telling me is…" (meaning) "…and it feels…" (feeling/tone). Checker: notes on the lab record ☐ idea not words ☐ register kept (friendly stays friendly, formal stays formal) ☐ one mediating frame used (quote it) ☐ any false-friend trap.
  • Sight-translation tips to give first: read the whole text silently first (30 seconds); chunk it into ideas; start with the main point; if a word won't cross, paraphrase and keep going; keep the tone of the original.
  • Part 3, Share (3 min): two mediators repeat their best rendering for the class; the class says what the original text was for and what tone it had.
  • Early finishers: the Bridge It! board game.
ICQs before the Lab
Do you have to use your own language? (No: there's an English route.) Can you bring a letter from the bank or the doctor? (No: public, simple, non-personal texts only.) In sight translation, word by word or idea by idea? (Idea by idea.) If a word won't cross? (Paraphrase and keep going.) What does the listener do? (Explain it back: the meaning and the feeling.) In the ethics cards, can an interpreter leave out a rude remark? (No.) Give advice? (No.) Guess a number? (No: ask, transparently.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Learners with limited literacy in their first language (or in any script) can do the lab entirely orally: option (c), conveying a word or idiom they know from speech, is just as demanding and needs no reading. Give a frame strip with the four steps of the paraphrase pattern and four frames. For the ethics cards, a partner reads the card aloud; the learner decides the principle from a picture-free list of six single words. Fewer items: Ex 2 items 1–6, Ex 3 items 1–4. Target: one complete signal → image → meaning → feeling explanation that a partner can explain back.

Stretch (toward professional mastery)

Back-translation and the translator's note. Learners who work in two languages sight-translate a short text into English, then a partner who shares the language (if any) back-translates it; they compare what drifted. Others write a 60-word translator's note justifying one choice (why they kept an image, dropped a pun or changed the register). Try 60 seconds of whispered simultaneous interpreting (chuchotage) of the teacher reading a plain-English sight text into simpler English, and discuss why professionals rotate every 20–30 minutes. For ethics: when is cultural brokering (explaining a cultural point) part of the job, and how do you do it transparently so both sides know?

L1 watch-outs (false friends by first language)
  • Spanish: actualmente (= currently), asistir (= attend), carpeta (= folder), éxito (= success), librería (= bookstore), constipado (= has a cold), embarazada (= pregnant), sensible (= sensitive). Portuguese: pretender (= intend), puxar (= pull), assistir (= watch/attend).
  • French: actuellement (= currently), éventuellement (= possibly), assister à (= attend), librairie (= bookstore), sensible (= sensitive). Italian: libreria (= bookstore), camera (= room), morbido (= soft), fattoria (= farm).
  • German: Gift (= poison), eventuell (= possibly), bekommen (= get/receive), Chef (= boss), sensibel (= sensitive). Polish: ewentualnie (= possibly), aktualny (= current). Russian: магазин (= shop), аккуратный (= neat, careful), фамилия (= surname).
  • Japanese and Korean loanwords that look English but aren't used that way: Japanese manshon (= apartment building), kurēmu "claim" (= complaint), sumāto "smart" (= slim), konsento "consent" (= power outlet); Korean haendeu-pon "hand phone" (= cell phone), aisyoping "eye shopping" (= window shopping), paiting "fighting!" (= good luck, go for it!).
  • Varieties, not errors: some forms that look like transfer are standard in a variety of English, such as Philippine English comfort room (= restroom) or Indian English prepone (= bring forward). Treat these as audience questions ("Will this reader know it?"), which connects to C2 5.1 (Global Englishes).
  • Collocation and grammar transfer: "do a mistake," "make a photo," "open/close the light," "I have 30 years," "arrive to," "strong in math." These are the "grammar" and "collocation" rows of the mismatch table: fix them as chunks.
Homework

Online lesson 7.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_21.html. Listen to the Ana and Tom dialogue and the five model sentences with the Listen buttons and say them back in an easy, explanatory rhythm; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Convey the Uncrossable Word" project (a word from any language you know, or one from the gallery or a new identity card: using your first language is welcome but never required). Workbook 7.3: Exercises 4 and 5, "My new words" and the journal (start a personal false-friend list with three entries, from your languages or from this lesson).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B An idiom's meaning isn't the sum of its words, so the literal version is opaque · Q2 A Translation is written with time to craft; interpreting is live speech in real time · Q3 (new) B Tell both people you need to check the word, then ask the teacher to explain it. Listening (Ex 1): 1 sobremesa · 2 "over-the-table" · 3 the time you spend still sitting and talking after a meal · 4 not being in a hurry (the meal isn't over just because the food is) · 5 "chatting over coffee after dinner" · 6 the sense that it's a whole custom.

Closing the Module

End of C22: Module 7 wrap-up

Recap on slide 19, "read the rules, negotiate across them, carry meaning between them": That would be quite challenging = probably no · When you said…, did you mean…? · That's on me (read and repair) → If you were able to…, then we could… · What matters most to you here? · With the new figures in, it'd be completely reasonable to revisit this (negotiate and save face) → It literally means…, but we'd actually say… · The nearest we get is…, though that loses… · Interpreter speaking: may I check a word? (mediate). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you; review it in the C23 warmer (the Bridge It! board game is ready for that).

Watch for in Module 8: the CPE speaking paper (8.1) rewards exactly the collaborative moves practiced here: inviting a partner in, disagreeing diplomatically, negotiating toward a joint decision; make that link explicit when you run the paired speaking task. Public speaking in 8.2 needs the calm, low-pitched delivery of C21 and the audience awareness of C20 (humor that travels; no jokes at anyone's expense). For the 8.3 masterwork, a cross-cultural or mediation topic is a strong, safe choice: offer it, with an assigned angle.

Final assessment (C26) covers all eight modules. From this module it can test: decoding an indirect refusal and writing a clarifying check; labeling a politeness strategy; placing words on a euphemism ladder; repairing a misfired joke; turning a blunt line diplomatic; positions vs interests and BATNA; conditional trades (If you were able to…, then we could… · provided + present simple); a cushioned difficult message; false friends; conveying an untranslatable word with the paraphrase pattern; translation vs interpreting and one interpreting-ethics principle.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 8 — The Summit: Exams, Performance & Lifelong Command

The last three classes of the whole A1–C2 ladder are about performance, in three different arenas. First, the exam room: a C2 exam-skills clinic in which learners treat proficiency-level tasks (key word transformations, gapped text, the essay summary, the speaking long turn and collaborative task) as solvable puzzles with their own logic. Then the stage: a public-speaking lab on structure for the ear, rhetoric performed rather than written, the working voice, nerves and Q&A, ending in three-minute speeches with peer feedback. Finally, the review project, Your Masterwork & Lifelong Command: each learner presents a capstone piece of their own choosing (a speech, an op-ed, a creative piece or a research summary), fields a searching discussion, and shares a personal plan for keeping their English growing when there is no next level.

3 lessons Lessons 8.1–8.3 Classes C23–C25 Weeks 12–13 90 min per class CEFR C2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. The shape is the usual one: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. At C2, 8.1 and 8.2 are workshops: the "language" is a set of task logics, delivery techniques and discussion moves, and most of the time goes to doing them under realistic pressure. C25 is a project class: short input, then the Masterwork Salon.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-8-masterwork.html. Print materials/module-8/classroom-materials.html before C23. It holds the phrase cards, the collaborative-task picture cards, new identity cards, the transformation relay cards, the cut-up gapped text, the essay summary task sheet, long-turn prompt cards and the examiner's descriptor sheet, the speech cards and speech feedback slips, the Summit Climb board game, the masterwork kit (format cards, rotation sheet, chair's script, response slips), the four format rubrics and the discussion rubric, the lifelong-command exchange card, the teacher observation checklist and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home and choose a US or UK voice. For this module the online lessons are also where learners practice alone under time (8.1), record a talk (8.2) and record their masterwork (8.3).
  • The project starts in C23, not C25. A polished capstone cannot be produced in class. Learners choose a format and a subject at the end of C23, fill in the masterwork planning frame (Workbook 8.3) before C24, draft and polish after C24, and send written pieces to their salon group before C25. The timeline is in the box below; board it in C23 and leave it up.
  • Refinement toward mastery, not re-teaching. Every skill this module asks for was built earlier: precision and collocation (1.1, 1.3), register (1.2), implicature (2.1), figurative language (2.3), stance and cohesion (3.1–3.2), summary and synthesis (3.3), rhetoric (4.1), style and voice (4.2), writing for readers and at length (6.1–6.2), diplomacy (7.2). Point back whenever a learner reaches for a tool: "That's your 4.1 antithesis, now performed." The new element is performance under constraint: a mark scheme, a clock, an audience, a challenger.
  • Feedback that is genuinely useful at C2. Your learners are near-native in some areas and fossilized in others, and they know it. Generic praise ("very fluent!") is useless to them, and so is a list of ten corrections. Take notes in three columns: Fix (errors, especially the persistent ones: an article with an abstract noun, discuss about, a present perfect with a past time phrase), Refine (correct but imprecise, unidiomatic, off-register, or lacking effect: "correct, but a native editor would say cast doubt on, not put doubt on") and Wow (a choice worth spreading). Board 2–3 of each without names. Keep a private fossilization log: two or three persistent items per learner, which you hand back on a slip at C26. At this level, one precise, named observation is worth more than any score.
  • Dominant speakers. C2 classes often contain one or two brilliant, fast talkers who fill every silence. In C23 give them the examiner role (they must listen, rate against the descriptors and report in 20 seconds). In the collaborative task, the task itself rewards inviting a partner in: tell them so ("Monologue loses marks here"). In C24 they are timekeepers with a strict three-minute limit, and in C25 the chair, whose job is to bring others in and keep the challenge fair. Say it early and kindly: "Great point, Daniel. Let's hear Mei's take first."
  • Quiet or anxious learners. Speaking in front of peers is the most exposing task in the course, even for a learner who writes beautifully. Allow a seated speech in C24, notes on the table, and a shorter slot (two minutes) if needed. In C25 the four formats exist partly for them: a writer may choose the op-ed or research summary, present a short spoken frame and read a passage. Nobody presents to the whole class: all groups run in parallel.
  • Stretching the strongest. Each lesson has a stretch box. The direction for this module is invisibility of craft: the exam answer that looks effortless, the rhetorical device that sounds like thought rather than technique, the response to a challenge that reframes the question rather than defending against it. Ask strong learners to explain why a choice works, and give them the hardest cards.
  • Peer review, the C2 way. All three classes use the same protocol, which learners know from earlier modules: (1) quote before you judge (name the exact phrase or moment); (2) describe the effect it had on you; (3) ask a genuine question before you suggest a change; (4) offer one change, framed as "If this were mine, I might…"; (5) the writer or speaker decides. Post it on the wall. Comments are about the work, never the person, and never about accent.
  • Exam material: original only. Everything in this module is written for teaching and imitates the style of proficiency-level task types. Nothing reproduces a real or past exam paper. Cambridge English C2 Proficiency (often called CPE) and IELTS are the trademarks of their owners; the kit is independent and unofficial. Formats, timings and word limits change: tell learners to check the current official specification for any exam they plan to take, and describe tasks in class as "typical" rather than "exact."
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (recognise, organised, practise as a verb, analyse, judgement, defence, humour, programme, full stop, public transport, towards). Teach recognize, organized, practice, analyze, judgment, defense, humor, program, period, public transportation, toward. Accept British forms when learners use them, but ask for one system per piece. Both exams accept either spelling system used consistently.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Perform for a mark scheme → perform for a room → perform as yourself. In C23 the audience is an examiner with descriptors, and the skill is converting existing mastery into points: read the task, bank the sure marks, prove every answer. In C24 the audience is a room of people, and the skill is making meaning easy to receive: a hook, a spine, a close; the pause; the stressed word; poise under a hard question. In C25 there is no mark scheme and no set topic: learners choose what to make and whom to make it for, and then say how they will keep growing. The module ends the whole ladder on the learner's own terms: command is not something the course gives them; it is something they now keep.

Trauma-informed: topics, exams, audiences and "pass"

Exams carry real weight for many adults at this level: a visa, a university place, professional registration, a job that depends on a band score. Some learners have failed a high-stakes test before, sometimes more than once. Never ask anyone to share a past score or a reason for taking an exam. Frame 8.1 as "technique for anyone who performs English under pressure," not "exam prep for exam candidates." All topics stay in workplace, education, science, arts/culture, technology, language and community territory: the kit's cards contain no party politics, religion, immigration or border policy, policing, war or national identity; don't add any, and steer a masterwork away from them privately if a learner proposes one ("Let's find an angle on this that the group can discuss as ideas"). Long-turn prompts that invite a personal story can always be answered as a new identity or invented, and the lifelong-learning plan may be written for a persona. Any learner may say "Pass, next card." In C25 the discussion challenges the work; the chair stops any question that becomes personal, and nobody is asked where they are from, what they believe, or to speak "for" a culture.

The project timeline (board it in C23)
End of C23Choose a format (speech · op-ed · creative piece · research summary: see the format cards) and a subject you genuinely have a view on. Read Workbook 8.3: the four formats and the planning frame.
Before C24Fill in the masterwork planning frame: controlling idea in one sentence, audience, shape, the "range plan" (which tools the piece needs), the hardest question you expect. Notes only. Also prepare the three-minute speech skeleton for C24 (speech-format learners may use the opening of their masterwork).
C24 (last stage)Salon groups announced. Each learner tells their group, in one sentence, what their piece will argue, show or explain.
After C24Draft, then polish until the seams disappear. Written pieces: 500–700 words. Speeches: 4–5 minutes. Send written pieces to your salon group by the evening before C25 (or bring 4 printed copies); speakers send a five-line abstract. Complete the lifelong-command plan (Workbook 8.3).
C25Read your group's pieces closely, prepare a response, then the Masterwork Salon: present (4–5 min), take the discussion (about 4 min), receive written response slips. Then the lifelong-command exchange in pairs.
After C25Hand in the piece (or its script/notes), the plan and the rubric self-check. You return them with written feedback, and the fossilization slip, at C26.

Why these four formats, when the online lesson names a talk, an essay-into-speech or an article you then present? They are the same idea, widened so that every kind of C2 user can show their best: the speech is the online "talk"; the op-ed is the "article you then present" (the C2 6.1 genre); the research summary is the "essay-into-speech" for learners whose English lives in academic or professional reports (3.3 and 6.2); the creative piece (a short story, a personal essay, a sequence of prose poems, a monologue) serves learners whose voice is literary (2.3, 4.2, 5.2). All four are presented aloud and discussed, so all four test the same integration. If a learner's week is too full, 400 well-polished words or a three-minute speech is fine: tell them privately.

Before the Module

  • Print the phrase cards (exam self-talk, collaborative moves, delivery, Q&A, response protocol) and the six collaborative-task picture cards. Card stock helps.
  • For C23: the transformation relay cards (1 set per group of 3), the gapped-text strips (1 set per pair), the essay summary task sheet (1 per learner), the long-turn prompt cards (1 set per group of 3), the examiner's descriptor sheet (1 per group) and the picture cards for the collaborative task (1 set per group). Phone timers.
  • For the end of C23: the masterwork format cards and subject starters, and the speech cards (for learners who want a topic for the C24 speech).
  • For C24: speech feedback slips (3 per learner), the Summit Climb board (1 per group of 3–4, a die or coin, counters), a timer on the screen.
  • For C25: the rotation sheet and chair's script (1 per group), response slips (3 per learner), the four format rubrics and the discussion rubric (each learner needs the one for their format, plus copies for their respondents), the lifelong-command exchange cards (1 per learner) and your observation checklist on a clipboard.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Set up a way for salon groups to share written pieces before C25 (a group email, a shared folder, or printed copies). Decide the groups at the end of C24.
  • Read the kit's C2 final assessment before C25. Your C25 observation notes feed straight into how you run C26.
