Lesson Plans

8.1 · C23

C2 Proficiency Exam Skills — The Strategist's Clinic

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain why marks are lost at C2 to technique rather than language, and name the four classic leaks: the under-answered prompt, the "true but not there" answer, the miscounted transformation, the monologued collaborative task
  • complete key word transformations in the proficiency style by applying the four checks: key word unchanged · word limit respected · meaning preserved exactly · the gap grammatical with what surrounds it
  • decide the part of speech and the prefix in word formation, and choose the word that collocates rather than the one that merely means the right thing
  • place sentences in a gapped text by tracking reference words and connectors, and reject any reading answer they cannot underline evidence for
  • plan an essay summary that identifies, paraphrases and evaluates the key points of two short texts and adds their own view, covering every part of the task
  • sustain a structured two-minute long turn and take part in a collaborative task that rewards inviting, building and negotiating toward a decision
Materials
  • Workbook 8.1 (New words, two Grammar boxes, Exercises 1–5, the collaborative-task dialogue, the Speaking circuit + descriptor self-rating)
  • Slides 3–8 · Phrase cards (exam self-talk, collaborative moves) · Transformation relay cards · Gapped-text strips · Essay summary task sheet · Long-turn prompt cards · Collaborative-task picture cards (park, market, bus, garden, restaurant, school) · Examiner's descriptor sheet · phone timers · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 8.1-A (Two pairs, one task), 8.1-B (Listen past the distractor) and, optionally, 8.1-C (a model long turn)
  • Masterwork format cards, subject starters and speech cards (for the project launch at the end)

Left: where C2 marks leak. (1) the under-answered prompt · (2) "true but not there" · (3) the miscounted transformation / changed key word · (4) the monologued collaborative task. The strategist: read the task before the text · spend time in proportion to marks · bank the sure points · prove every answer.

Center: key word transformation, the four checks. ① key word unchanged ② word limit (a contraction = 2 words) ③ same meaning, nothing added or lost ④ fits the grammar on both sides of the gap. "I couldn't understand the lecture at all." (NO) → I could make no sense of the lecture. · "As soon as she arrived, the argument started." (SOONER) → No sooner had she arrived than… Word formation: part of speech first → prefix? → plural / comparative?

Right: speaking. Long turn: frame → 2–3 signposted points → deliberate close. Collaborative: invite (What's your take on…?) · build (I'd add that…) · differ softly (I see it slightly differently, in that…) · decide (So, are we agreed that…?). Corner: the project timeline.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: the untranslatable6S–SReview C22 (7.3). Pairs: each learner explains one word that "doesn't translate" (from any language they know, or an English word that is hard to translate out), using the 7.3 frames: There isn't a single English word for it, but… · The closest equivalent would be… · It literally means…, but we'd actually say…. Partner offers the best English paraphrase they can. Two pairs share their best paraphrase. Note one Fix, one Refine, one Wow.
Lead-in: where the marks leak5T–SSlide 3. "You already have the English a top exam measures. So why do strong candidates lose marks?" Elicit ideas, then reveal the four leaks. Give the online lesson's four situations orally (one gap has stumped you for two minutes · an option "feels right" but you can't find the words · a great idea for part one of a two-part prompt · your partner has paused in the collaborative task); pairs decide the strategist's move in 20 seconds each. Stress the frame: technique is fixable in an afternoon.
Key word transformations12T–S → S–SSlides 4–5. Show the two worked examples (NO, SOONER). Elicit the four checks from the examples before you name them. CCQs (below). Then the families the task keeps testing: negative inversion (No sooner… than · On no account · Not until… did · Never before have…), fixed phrases with dependent prepositions (make sense of · on the brink of · in the wake of · make light of), passives and causatives, reporting structures. Pairs do Workbook Ex 1 items 1–2 together.
Drill: the transformation race6T–S → SOral, fast (see Drill script): you say a sentence and a key word; learners say the transformed sentence aloud (choral for the first two, then individuals, then teams with the transformation relay cards). Every answer is checked aloud against the four checks: "Key word? Count? Meaning? Fit?"
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1 (items 3–8) and Ex 2 (word formation + collocation) alone, under a light time limit: 6 minutes, "bank the sure ones, mark the hard ones, come back." Pairs compare, count words aloud, and board-check only disagreements.
Reading & listening clinic12S–S → T–SSlide 6. Gapped text (Workbook Ex 3; the cut-up strips for pairs who like to move paper): place three sentences, one is extra. Rule: underline the breadcrumb (the reference word or connector) that proves each placement. Then the two "true but not there" items. (7 min.) Then Script 8.1-B: four short extracts, read once each; learners write what each speaker settles on, not what they mention first, and one word for the attitude (Workbook Ex 5B). (5 min.)
Writing clinic: the essay summary10S → S–SSlide 7. The task type: summarize and evaluate the key points of two short texts and give your own view, in a set number of words (typically 240–280 in this style of task; tell learners to check the current specification). Workbook Ex 4 with the task sheet: (1) underline two key points per text; (2) paraphrase each in your own words (3.3 skills: no lifted phrases); (3) plan four paragraphs. Pairs swap plans and check one thing: does the plan cover every part of the task? The opening paragraph is written at home.
Freer: the Speaking Circuit20T–S → groups of 3Slide 8. Read Script 8.1-A (Pair 1 monologues, Pair 2 interacts; 2 min): "Which pair would score higher? Why?" Then groups of 3: candidate, interlocutor (gives the card, times, asks one follow-up), examiner (rates on the descriptor sheet and reports in 20 seconds). Round 1, the long turn (about 11 min): 1 minute to prepare (notes allowed, three words), 2 minutes of speech, one follow-up (30 s), report. Rotate three times. Round 2, the collaborative task (about 5 min): two learners discuss the picture cards ("A town wants to make its center more welcoming…") for 3 minutes and must reach a decision; the third examines interaction only. ICQs first. See "How to run it."
Feedback6T–SDelayed correction. Board 2 Fixes (e.g. a transformation that changed the key word: no sooner she had arrived · a collocation near-miss: make a big mistake is fine, do a mistake is not), 2 Refines (e.g. a long turn with no close: add "So, to pull that together…"; a collaborative turn that answers but doesn't invite) and 1–2 Wows. Ask: "Which leak do you personally need to plug first?"
Homework & project launch5T–SShow online lesson 8.1 and a Listen button; set the homework (below). Launch the project: board the timeline, show the four formats (slide 15, briefly), pass round the format cards and subject starters. Everyone leaves with a format and a first idea for a subject, and a speech card or their own topic for the C24 three-minute speech. Learners tick the 8.1 "I can…" list.
Total906 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 8 + 12 + 10 + 20 + 6 + 5
If you run long

Protect the Speaking Circuit: speaking under exam conditions is the thing learners cannot practice alone. Cut in this order: (1) Script 8.1-B shrinks to extracts 1 and 2; (2) the collaborative round runs as a single demonstration pair while the others examine; (3) the essay-summary plan stops after step 2 (key points and paraphrase), with the plan at home. Never cut the project launch: learners who leave C23 without a format lose two days of planning.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the strategist's mindset. A proficiency exam is not a fair sample of a learner's English; it is a stylized, time-pressured performance. At C2, lost marks are almost always leaks in technique, not gaps in knowledge.

"My English is excellent but my score was lower than I expected." Is the problem probably vocabulary? (No, usually technique.) · A reading option is true in real life, but you can't find it in the passage. Right or wrong? (Wrong: "true but not there.") · You wrote a brilliant answer to half of a two-part writing task. Can it reach the top band? (No: the prompt is under-answered.) · In the collaborative task, is a fluent two-minute monologue a good answer? (No: the task rewards interaction.)

Meaning and form: key word transformations.

"I couldn't understand the lecture at all." → "I could make no sense of the lecture." Did the meaning change? (No.) Is NO exactly as given? (Yes.) How many words in the gap? (Three: no sense of; "the lecture" is already printed.) What fixed phrase is being tested? (make sense of.) · "No sooner had she arrived than the argument started." Which word order after No sooner? (Auxiliary before subject: inversion.) Could you write "No sooner she had arrived"? (No: that loses the mark.) · If the limit is 3–6 words and "I'll" is in your answer, how many words is it? (Two.) · You write a perfect sentence but change SOONER to SOON. Mark? (Zero.)

Meaning: word formation and collocation.

"The evidence was, regrettably, ___ (CONCLUDE)." Which part of speech? (Adjective.) Does "regrettably" suggest the evidence settled the question? (No.) So? (inconclusive: the prefix carries the meaning.) · "a strong / heavy / thick / hard possibility." Do all four mean something like "big"? (Roughly.) Which one keeps company with possibility? (strong.) So what are you testing: meaning or partnership? (Partnership: collocation.)

Meaning: gapped text and the essay summary.

The sentence after the gap begins "This compression…". What must the missing sentence mention? (Something being compressed.) · A distractor sentence is about the right topic but has no link to either neighbor. Keep or reject? (Reject: it must fit what comes before and after.) · The essay summary asks you to "summarize and evaluate." If you only summarize, what's missing? (The evaluation and your own view: part of the task.) · Can you copy a key sentence from the text? (No: paraphrase. Lifted phrases don't show your range.)

Form: the patterns the transformation task loves.

Negative inversionNo sooner had + subject + past participle… than · Hardly / Scarcely had… when · On no account + aux + subject · Not until… + aux + subject · Never (before) + aux + subject · Only when / Only after… + aux + subjectC2 1.2 register, C2 4.1 rhetoric; C1 2.2
Fixed phrase + prepositionmake sense of · make light of · on the brink of · in the wake of · take into account · come in for (criticism) · cast doubt on · have no intention ofC2 1.3 collocation
Passive / causativeIt is thought that… → X is thought to have… · have / get something done · There is said to be…C1 review
Word formationpart of speech → negative prefix (in-, un-, dis-, mis-, ir-, il-) → suffix (-ive, -ous, -ance, -ity, -able) → plural / comparativepersuade → persuasive · conclude → inconclusive · foresee → unforeseen

Pronunciation: the speaking criteria. Proficiency speaking assesses pronunciation as intelligibility and prosody, never as closeness to a native accent. Say this explicitly, because many C2 learners believe their accent costs them marks. What earns credit is chunking (pausing at the boundaries of ideas, not mid-phrase), sentence stress on the new or contrasted information, and intonation that signals structure: a fall at the end of a completed point, a rise or level tone on a list that continues. In the long turn, a signpost followed by a short pause ("My second point | is cost.") sounds controlled. In the collaborative task, questions that invite should rise at the end ("What's your take on it? ↗"); a proposal to close should fall ("So, are we agreed that these two are strongest? ↘").

Drill script
  1. The four checks (choral, as a chant, twice): "Key word · word count · same meaning · fits the gap."
  2. Transformation race (T gives the sentence and key word; S says the full new sentence): T: "I couldn't understand the report at all. NO." → S: "I could make no sense of the report." T: "As soon as he sat down, the phone rang. SOONER." → S: "No sooner had he sat down than the phone rang." T: "You should never underestimate her. ACCOUNT." → S: "On no account should you underestimate her." T: "The company very nearly collapsed. BRINK." → S: "The company was on the brink of collapse."
  3. Spot the lost mark (T says a wrong answer; S names which check failed): T: "No sooner he had sat down than the phone rang." → S: "Fits the gap: no inversion." T: "On no account you should underestimate her." → S: "Same problem: inversion." T: "The company was on the edge of collapse (BRINK)." → S: "Key word changed." T: "I could make hardly any sense of the report (NO)." → S: "Key word missing, meaning changed."
  4. Word-formation flash: T: "PERSUADE, adjective." → S: "persuasive." T: "RELUCTANT, noun." → "reluctance." T: "CONCLUDE, adjective, negative." → "inconclusive." T: "FORESEE, adjective, negative." → "unforeseen."
  5. Collaborative moves (with intonation): "Shall we start with…? ↘" · "What's your take on…? ↗" · "I see it slightly differently, in that… ↘" · "So, are we agreed that…? ↘". Then open pairs: S1 proposes an option from the picture cards; S2 builds on it and invites S3.
The Speaking Circuit — how to run it
  • Groups of 3 (candidate · interlocutor · examiner). Each group has the long-turn prompt cards face down, the six picture cards, one examiner's descriptor sheet and a phone timer.
  • Round 1: the long turn (three rotations of about 3½ minutes). The interlocutor offers two cards; the candidate chooses one, has 1 minute to prepare (three key words only), then speaks for 2 minutes. The interlocutor calls "30 seconds" and "Thank you," then asks one follow-up question from the card (30 seconds). The examiner reports in 20 seconds, using the sheet's four criteria: "Strong frame and close; two signposted points; one great collocation, a double-edged sword; pace rushed in the middle."
  • Round 2: the collaborative task (about 5 minutes). Two candidates discuss the six picture cards for 3 minutes: first how effective each idea might be, then which two to recommend. The third learner examines interaction only (tallies invites, builds, soft disagreements and the move to a decision). If time is short, one pair demonstrates and everyone else examines.
  • Descriptor sheet (materials set F): four criteria (fluency & coherence · lexical range · grammatical range & accuracy · pronunciation) plus interaction for the collaborative task, each at three levels written in plain language. The descriptors are original and simplified; they are a practice tool, not an official band scale. Say so.
  • Personal prompts ("Describe a skill you learned later than most people…") can be answered as a new identity or invented. Any candidate may say "Pass, next card."
  • You circulate with Fix / Refine / Wow columns. Sit through one full long turn per group. Listen especially for the ending: most strong candidates run out of time rather than closing.
ICQs before the Speaking Circuit
Long turn: how long do you prepare? (One minute.) How long do you speak? (Two minutes.) Can you write sentences? (No, three key words.) · Who asks the follow-up? (The interlocutor.) · What does the examiner report? (The four criteria, briefly, with one example.) · Collaborative task: is the goal to give the best speech? (No, to discuss and reach a decision together.) How many ideas do you recommend? (Two.) · What does the third person listen for? (Interaction: inviting, building, disagreeing politely, deciding.) · Can you say "Pass"? (Yes.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Some C2 learners speak brilliantly but read slowly under time, or learned to read English late. Give them the four checks as a card to hold, and let them do Ex 1 items 1–4 and Ex 2 part A only. For the gapped text, give the cut-up strips and ask them to find the breadcrumb for one gap first, then the others. In the long turn, allow a 90-second turn and let them choose from the more concrete cards. In the essay summary, a partner may read the two texts aloud once. Target: one transformation checked aloud against all four checks, and one long turn with a clear frame and close.

Stretch

Write two original transformation items for the class (sentence, key word, limit, answer) that test different structures, and justify why each has only one correct answer. In the gapped text, explain why the distractor fails in both positions it seems to fit. In the long turn, take the hardest card and add a concession ("Admittedly…") before the close. As examiner, give feedback that names a specific phrase for each criterion. Homework: the full essay summary under 40 minutes, self-marked against the descriptors.

L1 watch-outs
  • Articles inside fixed phrases. Learners whose languages have no articles (Russian, Polish, Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese, Thai, and others) may write on brink of or in wake of. In a transformation, a missing article loses the whole mark. Train the eye: the brink, the wake.
  • Negative prefixes by analogy. Romance-language speakers transfer in- where English takes un- (inforeseen, Spanish imprevisto) or the reverse (unconclusive). Keep a class list of the tricky ones: inconclusive, unforeseen, irrelevant, illegible, dissatisfied, misleading.
  • Light-verb collocations transfer from L1: Spanish/Portuguese/Italian make a question (hacer una pregunta), German/Dutch make a photo, Arabic and Persian do a mistake, French take a decision (British, but check the context). These are exactly what the collocation task targets.
  • Memorized essay formulas. Learners from exam systems that reward set phrases (common in Chinese, Arabic, Persian, Turkish and South Asian test-preparation cultures) may open with Nowadays, with the development of society… or Every coin has two sides. Examiners at this level read these as formulaic: they lower the range score. Offer an alternative that engages the actual texts.
  • "True but not there." Learners from academic traditions that value bringing in outside knowledge (a strength in a seminar) are especially prone to choosing the option that is true in the world. Frame the exam as a different game with different rules.
  • Monologue as politeness. Some learners avoid interrupting or inviting a partner because it feels rude or presumptuous (often Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai and Vietnamese speakers); others hold the floor because turn-taking in their L1 conversation is more competitive (sometimes Spanish, Italian, Arabic, Hebrew or Greek speakers). The task rewards the middle; teach the invitation phrases as the polite move.
Homework

Online lesson 8.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_22.html. Listen to the five model sentences and say them back until they feel automatic; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the project Two Tasks, Self-Assessed (one should be the essay summary from Workbook Ex 4: write it in full, about 240–280 words, in 40 minutes). Workbook 8.1: Ex 5A (if not done in class), "My new words" and the journal. Project: fill in the masterwork planning frame (Workbook 8.3, notes only) and prepare the skeleton of your three-minute speech for C24: write your first and last sentences in full.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B genuine interaction: inviting, building, negotiating toward a decision. · Q2 C reject it: if you can't underline the evidence, it isn't supported. · Q3 (new) A "was on the brink of" (5 words, within the limit, key word unchanged, article present). Ex 1: 1 no sense of · 2 sooner had he sat down · 3 no account should you underestimate · 4 the first time I have (I've) · 5 was on the brink of · 6 in the wake of · 7 made light of · 8 until the report came. Ex 3 gapped text: 1 B · 2 C · 3 A (D is extra). Full answers in the answer key.

8.2 · C24

Public Speaking & Delivering with Impact — The Speech Lab

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • shape a talk for the ear: an open that earns attention in fifteen seconds, a signposted spine of two or three points, and a resonant close that ends on a full stop, not a fade
  • perform the rhetorical figures of 4.1 rather than merely write them: the tricolon whose third beat falls, the rhetorical question followed by a pause, antithesis and anaphora with parallel stress
  • use the voice deliberately: the pause before and after a key line, contrastive stress on the one word that carries the meaning, breath groups, and variety of pace, pitch and volume
  • build rapport (eye contact, inclusive we, reading the room) and channel nerves (automatic bookends, the "excited" reframe, slow exhales)
  • handle Q&A with poise: listen to the whole question, acknowledge, answer what was asked, concede the valid part and narrow to the real claim, bridge back
  • deliver a three-minute speech and give a peer precise, useful feedback with the speech rubric
Materials
  • Workbook 8.2 (New words, two Grammar boxes, Exercises 1–5, the speech-and-Q&A dialogue, the Speech Lab plan and feedback record)
  • Slides 9–14 · Phrase cards (delivery, Q&A) · Speech cards (spares for anyone without a topic) · Speech feedback slips (3 per learner) · Summit Climb board game (warmer) · a big timer on the screen · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 8.2-A (Same words, two deliveries), 8.2-B (the speech on talent and its Q&A) and 8.2-C (I never said she stole the money)
  • Learners' speech skeletons (homework from C23)

Left: build for the ear. A listener can't rewind. OPEN (15 s): a question · a striking fact · a short story · a bold claim; never an apology. SPINE: "There are three things I want to leave you with. First…" (number them, say the numbers). CLOSE: echo the open · a call to action · one line to carry home; end on a full stop. Performed rhetoric (4.1): tricolon (let the third beat fall) · rhetorical question + beat · antithesis (stress both halves) · anaphora (same stress, rising energy).

Center: the voice. | = short pause · || = long pause. Pause before a key line to prime it, after to let it land. Stress one word per idea: "I never said SHE stole the money." One idea, one breath. Slow down for what matters; speed up for asides.

Right: Q&A with poise. listen to the end → acknowledge (I'm glad you raised that.) → answer what was asked → concede + narrow (You're right about that; where I'd differ is…) → bridge back (Which brings me back to…). Don't know? I don't have that to hand, but here's how I'd find out. Nerves: bookends by heart · "I'm excited" · slow exhale · friendly faces first.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Summit Climb6S–SReview C23. Groups of 3–4 play Summit Climb (materials set H) for 5 minutes: each square is a quick exam item (a transformation, a word-formation stem, a collocation choice, a "strategist's move") or a 30-second speaking challenge. Then ask: "Which of the four leaks have you plugged since Thursday?" Note one Fix and one Wow.
Lead-in: same words, two deliveries5T–SSlide 9. Read Script 8.2-A twice: first flat and rushed, then performed (pauses, stress, a slower close). Ask: "Were the words different?" (No.) "Which version do you remember? Why?" Elicit the lesson's premise: an audience remembers how you made them feel; delivery is part of the meaning. Frame delivery as generosity to listeners, not vanity.
Structure & rhetoric for the ear10T–S → S–SSlide 10. Open · spine · close, with the online model open and close ("Every one of you made a decision this morning…"). CCQs (below). Then the rhetoric recap: learners name the 4.1 figures from four example lines, and you show how each is performed (tricolon: rising-rising-falling; antithesis: stress both poles; rhetorical question: a real beat before the answer). Workbook Ex 3 (open, close, Q&A choices) in pairs, 2 minutes.
The voice: pause, stress, pace10T–S → S–SSlides 11–12. Script 8.2-C: read "I never said she stole the money" five times in random order, stressing a different word each time; learners match each reading to its meaning (Workbook Ex 1B). Then "one idea, one breath": you mark the first lines of the talent speech on the slide with | and ||, learners mark the rest (Workbook Ex 4) and read it to a partner. CCQs.
Drill5T–S → SSee Drill script: contrastive stress, the pause before the key line, the falling third beat, the Q&A acknowledgements. Choral first, then individuals; insist on real silence in the pauses (count "one-and" in your head).
Nerves & Q&A with poise8T–S → S–SSlide 13. Read Script 8.2-B (the talk on talent and the audience question; two voices, or a confident learner reads the questioner). Ask: "How does the speaker handle the challenge?" (Concedes the valid part, narrows the claim, checks the answer landed.) Elicit the five-step method. Then nerves: ask for what learners already do; add the four online techniques. Pairs: Workbook Ex 2 and Ex 5 (turn a defensive answer into a poised one).
Speech prep: mark up and rehearse10S → S–SLearners take their homework skeleton (or a speech card) and complete the Speech plan in Workbook 8.2: write the first and last sentences in full, mark three pauses and three stressed words, place one rhetorical device. Then pairs rehearse only the open and the close, twice each, with the partner giving one note: "Slower on the last line." You circulate and help anyone without a topic.
Freer: the Speech Lab25groups of 4Slide 14. Groups of 4: speaker, timekeeper (3 minutes; shows "1 min" and "30 s"; stops at 3:15), questioner (asks one genuine or gently challenging question), feedback lead (fills in a speech feedback slip, and all three listeners add one line). One turn is about 6 minutes: speech (3) → one question and answer (1) → feedback (1½). Rotate roles. ICQs first. See "How to run it."
Feedback6T–SBoard 2 Fixes (e.g. an apologetic open: "Sorry, I'm not a good speaker, but…" · a fizzled close: "So… yeah, that's it"), 2 Refines (stress on the wrong word in a key line; a rhetorical question with no pause after it) and 1–2 Wows (a close that echoed the open; a concession in the Q&A). Ask each learner to read aloud the one line from someone else's speech that they remember: that is the real score.
Homework & salon groups5T–SShow online lesson 8.2 and a Listen button; set the homework (below). Announce the salon groups for C25 (3–4 learners, mixed formats) and how pieces will be shared. Each learner says one sentence to their group: "My masterwork will argue / show / explain that…". Learners tick the 8.2 "I can…" list.
Total906 + 5 + 10 + 10 + 5 + 8 + 10 + 25 + 6 + 5
If you run long

Protect the Speech Lab: a learner who prepared a speech and doesn't get to give it will be hurt. Cut in this order: (1) the warmer becomes 3 minutes of transformation relay in pairs; (2) Script 8.2-B becomes homework (it is the online excerpt), and you give the five-step Q&A method from the slide only; (3) speech prep shrinks to 6 minutes (bookends only). If a group runs out of time, the last speaker gives their speech first in C25's warmer slot, not never.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: content vs delivery; structure for the ear.

Two speakers read the same script; one bores the room, one holds it. Is the difference the argument? (No, the delivery.) · A reader can re-read. Can a listener rewind? (No.) So must a talk be more or less signposted than an essay? (More.) · "Sorry, I'm a bit nervous and not very prepared." When is the audience's attention highest? (In the first fifteen seconds.) What did this open do with them? (Wasted them, and lowered expectations.) · "So… yeah, that's about it." Is the end a good place to relax? (No: attention peaks again. Write the last line in advance.)

Meaning: rhetoric performed.

"It was quick, it was clean, and it was completely wrong." How many parts? (Three.) Which part carries the surprise? (The third.) So where does the voice fall? (On wrong.) · "And what did all that planning achieve?" [pause] "Nothing at all." Why the pause? (The audience answers in their heads; the speaker's answer lands on their thought.) · "We have never known more and understood less." What's the figure? (Antithesis.) Which two words carry the stress? (more and less.) · On the page a device is a pattern. In a speech? (An event: it depends on timing.)

Meaning: the pause and stress.

A pause before a key sentence says what? ("Listen: this matters.") After it? ("Let that sink in.") · Does a two-second silence feel long to the speaker? (Very.) To the audience? (No: it feels weighty.) · "I never said SHE stole the money." Who stole it, then? (Someone else.) "I never SAID she stole the money." (I implied it.) So is stress decoration? (No: it's part of what you say.)

Meaning: Q&A with poise.

"Isn't it a bit easy to say? Some people start with more advantages." Is the questioner partly right? (Yes.) The speaker says: "You're absolutely right: the starting lines are not equal." Has she lost the argument? (No.) What does she do next? (Narrows her claim: persistence is the part you control.) · Is "That's just wrong, actually" persuasive? (No: defensive.) "I'd rather not get into that"? (Evasive.) · You don't know the answer. Do you invent one? (No: "I don't have that to hand, but here's how I'd find out.")

Form.

OpenEvery one of you… · Here's a number that surprised me: … · Picture this: … · I want to begin with a question. · I'm going to argue something that sounds wrong.a curiosity gap
SignpostI want to leave you with three thoughts, and here's the first. · Which brings me to my second point. · Now, and this is the part that matters, … · Let me put that another way.say the numbers
Emphasis grammarwh-cleft: What I want you to remember is… · it-cleft: It's not talent that separates us; it's time. · fronting: This, I think, is the heart of it. · the thing is: The thing is, we already know how.C2 3.1 stance; C1 2.3
CloseIf you remember one thing today, let it be this. · So where does that leave us? · And that brings me back to where we began. · Don't wait to be good before you begin. Begin.end on a full stop
Q&AThat's a good question; let me take it in two parts. · I'm glad you raised that. · If I've understood your question correctly… · You're right about that; where I'd differ is… · That's outside what I've studied, but my honest guess is… · Can I check I've answered what you were really asking?acknowledge · answer · concede · narrow · bridge

Pronunciation: the working voice. English is stress-timed: stressed syllables fall at roughly regular intervals and unstressed ones are squeezed between them. That is why one strongly stressed word in a thought group carries so much meaning, and why stressing every word ("I-NE-VER-SAID-SHE…") sounds robotic. Teach the notation: | short pause, || long pause, CAPITALS for the stressed word, ↘ for a final fall. A tricolon rises on items one and two and falls on three ("quick ↗ | clean ↗ | completely WRONG ↘"). Antithesis stresses both poles ("known MORE | and understood LESS"). A rhetorical question falls (it is not a real question) and is followed by a real silence. For intimacy, drop the volume, not the clarity; for a call to action, lift pitch and slow down. Accent is never the target. A learner's accent is part of their voice; the goal is intelligible chunking, deliberate stress and controlled pace.

Drill script
  1. Shift the stress (Script 8.2-C, then learners): T says a meaning, S says the sentence with the right stress. T: "Someone else stole it." → S: "I never said SHE stole the money." T: "She stole something else." → "…stole the MOney." T: "I only implied it." → "I never SAID…" T: "It wasn't me who said it." → "I never said…"
  2. The primed line: T: "Pause before the key phrase." → S: "The gap between you and the person you admire || is not talent. | It is TIME." Choral, then three individuals; the class claps once if the silence was real.
  3. The falling third beat: T: "quick, clean, wrong." → S: "It was quick ↗ | it was clean ↗ | and it was completely WRONG ↘." Then learners invent their own triads: "It was cheap, it was fast, and it was…"
  4. Acknowledge, then answer (T asks a challenging question; S gives only the first move): T: "Isn't that a bit idealistic?" → S: "That's a fair challenge, and you're right that…" T: "What's your evidence?" → S: "I'm glad you asked; let me take it in two parts." T: "Do you know the figures for other countries?" → S: "I don't have that to hand, but here's how I'd find out."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says their first sentence; S2 repeats it back with one change of pause or stress and asks "Better or worse?" Swap.
The Speech Lab — how to run it
  • Groups of 4, chairs in a small arc so the speaker can stand (or sit) facing three listeners. All groups run at once: nobody speaks to the whole class. Put a big timer on the screen and call "Change" every 6 minutes so groups stay together.
  • One turn ≈ 6 minutes: speech (3 minutes; the timekeeper shows "1 min" and "30 s" cards and says "Thank you" at 3:15) → the questioner's one question and the speaker's answer (1 minute) → feedback (1½ minutes): the feedback lead reads the slip, and each other listener adds one line they remember and one change.
  • Topics: learners' own (prepared at home), a speech card (materials set G), or, for speech-format masterwork learners, the first three minutes of their masterwork (a real rehearsal). Speeches on a personal topic may use a new identity.
  • The feedback slip has five criteria (open · spine · close · voice · presence & Q&A) at three levels (Mastery · Secure · Developing), plus two lines: "The line I'll remember:" and "If this were mine, I might…". The speaker keeps all three slips.
  • Rules on the slide: no apology to open; no "that's about it" to close; a question is answered, not argued with; feedback quotes before it judges, and never mentions accent.
  • You circulate with Fix / Refine / Wow. Hear at least one full speech per group. The most common C2 problem is not language but time: rich speakers overrun. The timekeeper's "Thank you" at 3:15 is final.
ICQs before the Speech Lab
How long is each speech? (Three minutes.) What happens at 3:15? (The timekeeper says "Thank you" and the speech ends.) · How many questions? (One.) · Can you open with "Sorry, I'm nervous"? (No: a hook.) · What does the feedback lead write? (The slip: five criteria, one line to remember, one change.) · Does feedback mention accent? (Never.) · Can you sit down to speak? (Yes.) · When do you change roles? (When the teacher says "Change.")
Differentiation
Literacy support

Allow a two-minute speech from a skeleton of five key words, with the first and last sentences written in full on a card. Give the learner three phrase cards to hold: I want to leave you with two thoughts · Which brings me to my second point · If you remember one thing today, let it be this. Let them be timekeeper first so they hear two speeches before giving theirs. In the workbook: Ex 1, Ex 3 and Ex 4 (first two lines). Target: a real hook, one deliberate pause, and a close that ends on a full stop.

Stretch

Build the speech on a curiosity gap that the close shuts, and use two rhetorical devices so that neither is audible as a device. In the Q&A, don't just concede and narrow: reframe ("I'd put the question slightly differently…"). Give the speech twice in the week (once in class, once recorded) with one deliberate change, and compare. As feedback lead, name the exact moment a pause or stress changed the meaning. Early finishers write a 60-second version of the same speech: what survives?

L1 watch-outs
  • Syllable-timed rhythm. Speakers of syllable-timed languages (Spanish, French, Italian, Portuguese in part, Turkish, Tagalog, and many others) and mora-timed Japanese give syllables more equal weight, so contrastive stress and the squeezed unstressed syllable feel unnatural. The stress drill helps more than explanation. Praise audible contrast, not accent change.
  • Tone languages and pitch. For Mandarin, Cantonese, Vietnamese and Thai speakers, pitch carries word meaning in L1, so using pitch for emphasis or attitude can feel risky, and some speakers flatten it. Show that English uses length and loudness as well as pitch for emphasis.
  • The opening apology. In several speech cultures (Japanese is the best-known example, and it occurs in Korean, Chinese and many others) a modest opening ("I am not a skilled speaker, but…") is a courtesy. In an English speech it reads as a lack of confidence. Present it as a genre convention, not a moral one, and offer a warm alternative: "Thank you for having me. I want to begin with a question."
  • Eye contact norms. Sustained eye contact with individuals can feel intrusive or disrespectful to some learners (in parts of East Asia, among some West African and Indigenous communities, and for many neurodivergent people regardless of background). Offer "scan the room and rest on friendly faces" or "look at the space between two people" as full alternatives; never mark eye contact down on its own.
  • Bold claims and modesty. Learners from traditions that prize understatement (Japanese, Korean, Scandinavian, Dutch and others, for different reasons) may resist a "bold claim" open. Offer the question or story open instead. Learners from more oratorical traditions (Arabic, Persian, Greek, Italian, many West African) may over-ornament: remind them that at C2 the device should be invisible.
  • "Discuss about," "explain me," "return back." These fossilized items come back under pressure, especially in the Q&A. Note them for the private fossilization log, not the board.
Homework

Online lesson 8.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_23.html. Listen to the speech and its Q&A with the Listen buttons and shadow the speaker's lines, matching the pauses and the stress; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Do the project Deliver a 2–3 Minute Talk as a recording of today's speech with one change suggested on your feedback slips; watch it once with the sound off and once with the picture off. Workbook 8.2: "My new words" and the journal. Project: draft and polish your masterwork; send written pieces (or a five-line abstract for a speech) to your salon group by the evening before C25; complete the lifelong-command plan in Workbook 8.3.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A it primes the audience's attention and lets the line land. · Q2 A concede the valid part, then narrow to your actual point. · Q3 (new) B memorize the open and close; speak the middle from a skeleton of key points. Ex 1A: 1 pause · 2 rhetorical question · 3 stress · 4 rule of three · 5 eye contact · 6 vocal variety. Ex 2: thing · question · differ · began · parts · hand. Ex 3: 1 b · 2 c · 3 b.

8.3 · C25

Review Project — Your Masterwork & Lifelong Command

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • present a substantial capstone piece of their own choosing (a speech, an op-ed, a creative piece or a research summary) that integrates the full C2 range: the exact word, controlled register, cohesion across the whole, rhetoric and figurative language with purpose, and a recognizable personal voice
  • make the craft invisible: deploy the range deliberately in the draft, then polish until nothing sounds placed rather than arisen
  • field a searching discussion on their piece: understand the question behind the question, concede the valid part, narrow to the claim that survives, stay composed
  • respond to a peer's work with the peer-response protocol: quote, describe the effect, probe, challenge fairly, offer one change
  • judge their own performance against the C2 standard (spontaneous, very fluent, precise, differentiating finer shades of meaning) and name one triumph and one growth edge
  • commit to a specific, self-diagnosed lifelong-command plan built on four habits: read widely, use the language where it matters, teach it, live in it
Materials
  • Workbook 8.3 (the four formats, the integration map, discussion language, Exercises 1–5, the masterwork planning frame, the pre-presentation checklist, the lifelong-command plan, the rubric self-check, the final journal)
  • Slides 15–18 · Phrase cards (response protocol, concede → narrow) · The masterwork kit (materials set I): rotation sheet, chair's script, response slips (3 per learner) · The four format rubrics + discussion rubric (set J) · Lifelong-command exchange cards (set K) · Teacher observation checklist (set L) on a clipboard · a timer on the screen
  • Listening scripts 8.3-A (a masterwork and its discussion) and 8.3-B (a model response round)
  • Learners' pieces (shared the evening before, or 4 printed copies each) · spare copies of the model excerpt for anyone who arrives without a piece

Left: THE SALON (per learner, about 10 min). Chair introduces (15 s) · Presentation (4–5 min) · Reflect: Respondent 1 quotes what landed (1 min) · Probe: Respondent 1 asks a genuine question (1 min) · Challenge: Respondent 2 raises one challenge; presenter concedes → narrows (1½ min) · Offer: "If this were mine, I might…" (30 s) · Response slips (1 min) · rotate

Center: CONCEDE → NARROW. That's a genuinely important challenge, and you're right to press it. · Let me draw the distinction I actually mean. · I'm not saying…; I'm saying… · Granted, …; what I'm claiming is narrower: … · I'd resist the word "…"; I'd say "…" instead. · If I've understood you, the worry is that…

Right: RESPONDING. What landed for me was the phrase "…": it made me… · I wonder whether… · What made you choose… rather than…? · Someone might push back on… · If this were mine, I might… Lifelong command: read · use · teach · live in it.

Corner: Challenge the work, never the person. · The chair stops personal questions. · Not a grade: C25 is practice. · C26 = the final assessment + celebration.

Stage plan

Warmer: one line to carry home4S–SReview C23–C24. Partner repeats the line back and names its device, if any. Then everyone sits with their salon group and checks that all pieces arrived.

Point out the move under challenge: she concedes the risk of elitism, draws the distinction she actually means, and narrows the claim to one that survives.

Plans may be for a new identity.

Then board 2 Fixes, 2 Refines and 1–2 Wows. Learners complete the rubric self-check in Workbook 8.3.

If you run long

Protect the Salon: a learner who made a masterwork and doesn't get to present it will be hurt. Cut in this order: (1) Script 8.3-B shrinks to the challenge and offer only; (2) reading time drops to 5 minutes if pieces were shared the night before (they should be); (3) the discussion in each salon drops from 4 to 3 minutes (the offer round is written on the slip only); (4) the lifelong-command exchange shrinks to 4 minutes, with the accountability message sent after class. Never cut Looking Ahead: learners must leave knowing exactly what C26 looks like.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review)

No new grammar. The work today is integration (every C2 thread in one piece), composure (answering the challenge actually made) and generosity (responding to a peer precisely). Keep CCQs fast.

"You are asked to deploy the full range deliberately, then make that deliberateness invisible." Is a piece where you can hear "there's the metaphor, there's the inversion" a success? (No: the seams show.) · Five metaphors, three inversions and a chiasmus in one paragraph? (A showreel, not a piece: deploy only what the thought needs.) · Is the masterwork about learning a new technique? (No: orchestrating the ones you have.)
A discussant asks: "Isn't there something a little elitist in that?" Response A: "No. That's not what I said." (Stonewall.) Response B: "You're right. Forget it." (Collapse.) Response C: "That's a genuinely important challenge, and you're right to press it. Let me draw the distinction I actually mean: I'm not saying mastery makes a person richer; I'm saying it opens particular rooms." Which is poised? (C.) What are its moves? (Acknowledge → concede the risk → narrow the claim.) · Is narrowing the same as giving up? (No: the claim that survives is often stronger.)
A respondent says "It was really good, well done." Useful to a C2 writer? (Not very: nothing specific.) Better? ("What landed for me was 'information's impersonation': it made the familiar phrase suddenly suspicious.") · A respondent gives ten corrections. Useful? (No: one or two, framed as offers.) · Who decides whether to take a suggestion? (The writer.)

Check the questionIf I've understood you, the worry is that… · Do you mean… or…? · Is the question really about…?find the question behind the question
Concede → narrowThat's a genuinely important challenge, and you're right to press it. · Granted, … · Let me draw the distinction I actually mean. · I'm not saying…; I'm saying… · What I'm claiming is narrower: … · I'd resist the word "…"; I'd say "…" instead.C2 7.2 diplomacy

Pronunciation for presenting and discussing. Bring 8.2 into the salon. The opening line is said slowly with a pause after it. A read-aloud passage (op-ed, creative piece) is performed, not read: look up at the end of each sentence, pause at paragraph breaks, and slow the last line. In the discussion, the concession falls and is sincere ("You're right to press it ↘"); the narrowing restarts with a slightly higher pitch and stresses the contrast ("I'm not saying RICHer; I'm saying it opens ROOMS"). A respondent's quotation is said in a slightly different voice, so the group hears it as quoted.

Drill script (quick, choral → individual)
  1. Concede → narrow chain: T gives a challenge: "Isn't this just nostalgia?" S1: "That's a fair challenge, and you're right to press it." S2: "Let me draw the distinction I actually mean." S3: "I'm not saying the past was better; I'm saying we lost one thing worth keeping." New challenge, new chain.
  2. Vague → precise: T: "It was really interesting." → S: "What landed for me was the image of…, because…" T: "Maybe change the ending?" → S: "If this were mine, I might end on the image of the door, and cut the last sentence."
  3. Resist the word: T: "So you think experts are useless?" → S: "I'd resist the word 'useless'; I'd say 'overtrusted.'" T: "So technology is bad for learning?" → S: "I'd resist 'bad'; I'd say 'badly used.'"
  4. Check the question: T asks a long, slightly unclear question. S: "If I've understood you, the worry is that… Is that right?" Then S answers in one sentence.
The four formats in the salon
FormatLength (prepared)In the salon (4–5 min)Rubric focus
Speech4–5 minutes spokenDelivered in full, from notes; bookends by heart.structure for the ear · rhetoric performed · voice · audience
Op-ed500–700 words, for a named publication or readershipA 1-minute spoken frame (the argument and why now), then the presenter performs the two strongest paragraphs. The group has read the whole piece.thesis & angle · argument & concession · voice for a general reader · concision
Creative piece500–700 words: a short story, a personal essay, a sequence of prose poems, a dramatic monologueRead aloud (up to 4 minutes; an excerpt if longer), then a 30-second craft note: one choice the writer made and why.controlling image · style & voice · figurative language · diction · reading aloud
Research summary500–700 words synthesizing at least three sources on one questionA 4-minute spoken briefing from notes for an intelligent non-specialist: the question, what the evidence says, how strong it is, what remains open.question & scope · synthesis · attribution & calibrated hedging · clarity

Subjects are the learner's choice, within the module's territory (workplace, education, science, arts and culture, technology, language, community). The subject starters on the format cards help anyone who is stuck. Research summaries must use real, checkable sources: ask for a short source list at the end, and remind learners not to invent statistics. Creative pieces may be fiction; a personal essay may draw on invented or composite experience, and nobody is asked whether it is "true."

Setting up the salon (end of C24) — groups, roles, rotation

Groups: 4 is ideal (3 works). Mix formats inside a group so respondents meet something new, and mix confident and anxious presenters. Avoid putting close friends together: they respond gently. Announce groups at the end of C24 so pieces can be shared. Each group gets a rotation sheet (materials set I):

SalonPresenterRespondent 1 (reflect & probe)Respondent 2 (challenge)Chair & timekeeper
1ABCD
2BCDA
3CDAB
4DABC

Every learner presents once, reflects-and-probes once, challenges once and chairs once. They read closely only two pieces. Groups of 3: A–B–C, with Respondent 2 also acting as chair. Absences: you become Respondent 2 in one group, or run a group of 5 with two Respondents 2. A learner who arrives without a piece still responds and chairs, and presents the planning frame as a 2-minute pitch ("Here is what my masterwork will be"), then takes the discussion: the discussion is the C2 skill you most want to see.

Who chairs well: the chair keeps time, reads the introduction, runs the offer round and stops any question that becomes personal ("Let's keep to the piece. Next question, please."). A dominant speaker makes a good chair, because the job is to bring others in; so does an anxious learner who likes structure, because the chair's script tells them exactly what to say.

ICQs: How long do you present? (Four to five minutes.) · Respondent 1: what two things do you do? (Quote what landed, then ask a genuine question.) · Respondent 2? (One fair challenge.) · Is a challenge about the person OK? (No: the chair stops it.) · When a challenge is fair, what does the presenter do? (Concede it, then narrow.) · What does everyone write at the end? (A response slip, and circle the rubric.) · How many changes do you offer? (One.) · Is this a grade? (No, it's practice for C26.)
Running a salon — the chair's order (about 10 minutes)
  1. Chair introduces (15 s): "Welcome. Our presenter is Lina. Her masterwork is an op-ed for a city magazine, arguing that every library should keep one room free of screens. Lina, you have five minutes."
  2. Presentation (4–5 min): the chair shows "1 MINUTE" and "TIME."
  3. Reflect (Respondent 1, 1 min): "What landed for me was…" (a quoted phrase or moment, and its effect).
  4. Probe (Respondent 1, 1 min): one genuine question about meaning or a choice; one short follow-up allowed.
  5. Challenge (Respondent 2, 1½ min): one fair challenge to the thesis, the evidence or a craft choice. The presenter concedes what is valid and narrows. One follow-up allowed.
  6. Offer (both respondents, 30 s): "If this were mine, I might…". The presenter says only "Thank you": no defending during offers.
  7. Response slips (1 min): everyone except the presenter writes a slip and circles the format rubric; the presenter notes the question they found hardest. Rotate.
The rubrics and the observation checklist → C26

The format rubrics (set J; the self-check version is in Workbook 8.3) each have five criteria, three shared by all formats (integration of the C2 range · precision & register · coherence across the whole) and two specific to the format (for example rhetoric performed and voice & delivery for a speech; synthesis and calibrated attribution for a research summary). The discussion rubric has three: understanding the question · concede & narrow · composure. Three levels: Mastery (the C2 standard: precise, nuanced, effortless) · Secure (strong, with occasional visible effort or near-misses) · Developing (the range is there but not yet controlled). Respondents circle the rubric for the presenter; learners self-assess; you use both for written feedback returned at C26. Don't grade C25: it's practice. Say so clearly.

The observation checklist (set L) has one row per learner and 10 can-do items. Three marks: ✓ did it clearly · ~ did it with visible effort, or with slips that affect precision · ○ not yet / not observed. You can't sit through every salon: see each learner's whole presentation or at least their discussion, and one of their responses. After class, use it to:

  • Plan each learner's C26 assessment. The C2 final assessment is an individual speaking interview (about 12 minutes) plus listening, reading and writing tasks — see the kit's C2 final assessment. Before C26, read it and match your checklist notes to its speaking criteria: which subjects suit each learner, and where to begin the interview so that each learner starts with something they did well.
  • Plan the C26 warm-up: the two items with the most ~ / ○ across the class become a short review before the assessment begins.
  • Write one sentence of feedback for each learner on the returned piece, naming a real strength from the salon specifically ("Your concession to Sam's point about cost narrowed your claim and made it stronger"), and fill in their fossilization slip (two or three persistent items, with the correct form).
  • Spot who needs reassurance: anyone who froze under challenge gets a quiet word at the end of C25: "Next class is one-to-one and friendly. No audience. You'll be fine."
Differentiation
Literacy support

Before C25 (in C24 or by message), agree a lighter piece: a 3-minute speech, or 350–450 words in any format, built directly from the planning frame's sentence starters. In the salon, allow a 3-minute presentation and ask the group's Respondent 2 to use a probe rather than a hard challenge. Give the learner four phrase cards to hold: That's a fair point · Let me draw the distinction I mean · What landed for me was… · If this were mine, I might…. As a respondent, they may write their question in advance and read it. Target: one precise quotation as a respondent, and one concede-and-narrow as a presenter.

Stretch

Answer the question behind the challenge: name its assumption ("That question assumes that access is the only barrier; I'd question that"). Reframe rather than defend. In the piece, aim for one device that carries the whole (like the door and the room) rather than several. As Respondent 2, challenge a craft choice, not only the argument ("Does the second metaphor compete with the first?"). At home: write a 150-word afterword revising the piece in light of the hardest question, and a second version of the lifelong plan for five years, not one.

L1 watch-outs
  • Where the main point goes. Some rhetorical traditions build toward the point (Japanese ki-shō-ten-ketsu, Korean gi-seung-jeon-gyeol, and some Chinese, Arabic and Persian essay conventions value context before claim). An English op-ed or briefing puts it early; a creative piece may delay it on purpose. Frame it as a choice of genre, not a better way of thinking.
  • Fossilized items at C2. The ones most likely to surface in a polished piece: articles with abstract or generic nouns (the society, the nature meaning "nature in general") · uncountables made plural (researches, advices, informations, evidences) · present perfect with a finished time (I have finished it last year) · since / for confusion · dependent prepositions (depend of, consist in for consist of, interested for) · discuss about, emphasize on, explain me. Put them on the fossilization slip, one learner at a time, never on the board with names.
  • Challenging a peer in public. Learners from cultures where questioning a peer publicly risks loss of face (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai, Vietnamese, Filipino, and many others) may only praise; learners from more direct traditions may challenge bluntly. The role card helps both: "The card asks for a challenge; it's your job, not your opinion." Teach the softeners: Someone might push back on… · I wonder whether….
  • Reading instead of presenting. Learners from exam systems that reward memorized recitation may read an op-ed or research summary word for word. The format rules help: op-eds perform two paragraphs, briefings are from notes. Allow a full script at home for confidence.
  • Spelling system. Written pieces often mix American and British spelling (analyze and organisation in one paragraph). Ask for one system; it's the kind of consistency a C2 reader notices.
Homework

Online lesson 8.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-c2/lesson_24.html. Listen to the masterwork and its discussion with the Listen buttons and shadow the speaker's concession; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Do the Capstone Project as a recording of your masterwork, with the one change from today's slips you chose to make, and answer the hardest question your group asked, better than you did in class. Workbook 8.3: the rubric self-check (if not finished) and the final journal: the letter to your A1 self and your own curriculum for the years ahead. Send your accountability partner the message you agreed. Bring the workbook to C26.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B integrating the whole range into one seamless, effortless piece. · Q2 C keep growing through lifelong reading, real-world use, teaching, and living in it. · Q3 (new) B the specific response that quotes, describes an effect, wonders and offers one change. Ex 1: mot juste · register · cohesion · rhetoric · personal voice. Ex 2: reading · teach · garden · door. Ex 3: 1 below the bar · 2 meets it · 3 below the bar · 4 (new) meets it. Full answers in the answer key.

Closing the Module and the Course

End of C25: Module 8, C2 and the whole ladder

Recap on slide 18: "Key word · count · meaning · fit" → "If you remember one thing today, let it be this." → "You're right to press that; let me draw the distinction I actually mean." → read · use · teach · live in it. Perform for a mark scheme, perform for a room, perform as yourself. Learners tick the three "I can…" lists and look back at the "I can…" lists from Module 1. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you, for the C26 warm-up.

The C26 final assessment covers the whole C2 course. From this module the most relevant skills are: sustaining a structured, precise long turn; taking part in a discussion that rewards interaction; answering an unexpected, searching question with composure (concede, narrow, reframe); and writing a controlled, well-organized piece for a specified reader. Check the kit's C2 final assessment for exactly how it tests them.

Looking Ahead — C26: Final Assessment & Celebration

What to tell learners at the end of C25 (slide 18)

C26 has two parts. First, the C2 final assessment: an individual speaking interview (about 12 minutes) plus listening, reading and writing tasks — see the kit's C2 final assessment. Read it before C26 and tell learners how the class will be organized (the order of the tasks and when each learner will be interviewed), so there are no surprises. Then a celebration: certificates, a look back at what everyone can do now, and the next steps, which at C2 are the learner's own: a formal exam if a credential opens a door they want, demanding professional or academic use, teaching or mentoring, translation or interpreting pathways, and the lifelong-command plan.

Say it simply: "Next class is our last class. You'll have a one-to-one conversation with me for about twelve minutes, and some listening, reading and writing. It's everything we've practiced. Today you presented a piece you chose and answered questions you didn't expect, from people who had read it closely. That was harder than anything in C26. Then we celebrate."

Reduce anxiety: show C2 lesson 1.1 on screen and ask: "Week 1: could you have chosen between impersonation and copy for a reason, performed it, and defended the choice?" Then remind them of the longer view: for many learners this is the end of a ladder that started at A1. Let them hear how far they've come. Remind them that the online lessons stay open for review, and that their recorded masterwork is the best preparation for the interview.

Celebration planning: if learners want to bring food to share in C26, great. Ask everyone to label their dish with its main ingredients (allergies, halal, vegetarian). Nobody should feel they have to bring anything. Invite 2–3 volunteers to give a one-minute closing speech: "one line I'll carry home from this course", the C24 skill, with the friendliest audience of all. Learners who want to may read their "letter to my A1 self" aloud, or one line of it; nobody is asked to.
